# Game compatibility orchestration

This is the durable playbook and current handoff for concurrent game-compatibility work. It is written for an
orchestrating agent that delegates one bounded investigation to each subagent, integrates real progress, and keeps
the shared repository, GPU, evidence, issues, and pull requests coherent.

Read the repository-root `CLAUDE.md` before this document. `CLAUDE.md` remains authoritative for filesystem,
verification, evidence, review, privacy, and release policy. This document explains how to apply those rules when
several game agents work at once.

The checkpoint below was refreshed **2026-08-10**. The named Lane A–F allocation and Wave 1/2 sections remain
dated historical records; they retain valuable falsifications and apparatus lessons, but their ownership and
"next assignment" text is not live. Update the checkpoint whenever an investigation materially moves, a PR merges,
or ownership changes. Do not let this document replace issue comments: issues are the durable evidence log, while
this document is the map that helps the next orchestrator find and interpret that evidence.

### The standing objective, in the user's words

**2026-09-05, most recent:** *"the goal here is to test games, and get them to title screen with
correct visuals and at least 50% of full PS5 speed. prioritize mid tier games first, where progress is
most likely."* — so **speed is now half the bar**, and it is the half that had no owner before that
night: every title measured so far sits an order of magnitude below it (*Syberia* ~1.9 fps,
*Blue Prince* ~3.2, *Khazan* 1.2 under the renderer). A title that renders correctly and runs at 5% of
hardware speed has met one half of the goal, and the milestone text should say which.

**Earlier, and not superseded — it governs which titles to pick:** **Rung 0 titles first, then title
screens. Breadth beats depth: "progress on many titles is more important than progress in a few."** The user also asked explicitly *not* to spend heavily on Astro
Bot unless it yields real progress — it is a 149 GB AAA showcase that has produced no shippable title
fix across several sessions. Every new visible checkpoint gets an **unmodified frontend screenshot**
committed under `assets/screenshots/` and referenced from **both** the game tracker issue and
`COMPATIBILITY.md`; the user asks for this specifically.

### Tooling hazards that cost real time this session

1. **Build inside the `ps5ys` distrobox.** The host has only the Vulkan *loader*, not the headers. A host
   configure **succeeds while silently omitting** `gpu_replay`, the offscreen graphics harness, and every Vulkan
   **execution** test, logging just `Vulkan not found`. A green host `ctest` is therefore not evidence that
   execution tests ran — confirm the binaries exist and report test names, not an exit code. Wipe the build dir
   when switching; a stale cache keeps Vulkan disabled.
2. **`shader_inspect` was misreporting ~96% of known-good shaders** (109 of 114, across all three stages) as
   `rejected`, because a raw dump carries no descriptors. Fixed in #1575 — it now reports
   `undetermined-no-resource-table` with exit 3. Any shader verdict taken from it before `329153bf` is suspect.
3. **`-DGAME_DUMP` against a non-Messenger title fails 3 dump-parameterized tests** (`module_loads_eboot`,
   `boot_reaches_first_syscall`, `real_shader_render`). This is expected, not a regression — current master is
   **162/162** when configured against `PPSA24651`. Tracked as #1573.

### The instrument-not-the-subject list — read this before believing any surprising measurement

**Every entry below is a phantom defect that came from the measuring apparatus, not the subject.**
Several cost hours; one cost two sessions. This is the single highest-value page in this document.

*Maintenance:* **append, never renumber** — an existing number may already be cited from an issue, a
commit message, another document or a source comment — **118 references on 110 lines in 46 files
do**, naming 63 distinct rows, and **50 of those references are `.cpp`/`.hpp`/`.py` comments**
rather than prose (measured on `origin/main` `8c4c0f09`, 2026-08-17; re-derive rather than
trusting a restated figure). Deliberately
no total is stated here: a restated count goes stale the moment a lane appends, and it already had
(the header read "Fourteen" against a 17-row table). The last row's number is the **high-water
mark**, which is what you want for allocating; it is not the row count, because **gaps are legal**.

*Allocating a number under concurrency:* run
`python3 tools/docs/trap_number.py` before you write the row. It reports the highest number on
`origin/main` **and in every open PR**, names the claimant of each, and prints the next free
number. Reading master alone is what produced the #2574/#2581 collision on 2026-08-17 — both lanes
read 181, both wrote 182 — and it is what #1729 was filed for. The tool is an **advisor, not a
gate**: two lanes running it in the same minute still both see the same free number. It shrinks the
window; only merge order closes it.

*If you collide anyway:* renumber your row to **any** number above the current highest and merge.
Nothing waits on anything. The gapless rule that used to make this a re-derivation against a moving
master was removed on 2026-08-17 (#2089); `check_numbered_table.py --ordered` enforces unique and
ascending, and `--baseline` enforces that no existing row has been deleted or renumbered.

| # | Instrument | How it lied |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `shader_inspect` | Reported **109 of 114 known-good shaders** as `rejected` — a raw dump has no descriptors, so table-dependent MIMG/MUBUF/MTBUF (and SMEM in graphics) correctly refuse. Fixed #1575. |
| 2 | `PROSPER_FS_TAP` | Exports `(dst, dst+1, dst+2, dst+3)`, so taps with different `dst` compare **different registers**. Manufactured a "~14x amplification". |
| 3 | Snapshot guards | `blue-prince-hall` and `terminator-boot` "FAILED" on **fully-rendered healthy frames** whose routes had drifted to another scene/animation phase. A control run on master reproduced both. |
| 4 | `sgpr:106` | Is `VCC_LO` **scratch**, not a uniform — a static constant-buffer read cannot resolve its value at point of use. |
| 5 | `--through-operation N` | Renders the **last executed draw target**, so adjacent prefixes show *different surfaces*. #1486's founding "corruption boundary" was 1920x1080 vs 3840x2160. Fixed by #1580's `target=`. |
| 6 | `git checkout <file>` | Restores from the **index** — it is not an undo for a temporary edit, and silently wipes uncommitted work. |
| 7 | A stale `in-progress` label | Read as occupancy. #1284's claim had been dead six days (no open PR, no activity); CLAUDE.md expires claims at 24 h. |
| 8 | An orchestrator's instruction | "If it is a missing capability, implement it" — all three opcodes were **already implemented**; following it would have duplicated a working `image_gather4_lz`. |
| 9 | `[compute] skip unsupported program` | **Deduped per `code_addr`**: one line means "failed at least once", NOT "always fails". A program running 763 times and failing once looks identical to a permanently blocked one. |
| 10 | `PROSPER_COMPUTELOG_CODE=<addr>` | Correctly protects a realtime route from ~13k submits of logging, and thereby **suppresses `execute` lines for every other program**. Right for one question, silently wrong for the next asked of the same log. |
| 11 | SHA reachability after a **squash** merge | `git merge-base --is-ancestor` reports "unmerged" for work that *is* on master. Verify by **content**, not by SHA. |
| 12 | `PROSPER_REGWATCH` **joined to** `PROSPER_EXECLOG` by stream adjacency | The two timestamp at **different pipeline stages** — REGWATCH at command-processor *decode*, EXECLOG at *realization* — and the phases interleave per submit. Read by log order, #1606 said **1,049 of 1,050** suppressed draws resolved against a *future* register write. Clean, compelling, and entirely false. Fixed by #1633's `order=`. |
| 13 | A **print-capped** diagnostic read as a frequency | `[agc] WaitRegMem … NOT satisfied` prints the first 40 then every 1024th (`ln < 40 \|\| (ln & 1023) == 0`); `[agc] out-of-range indirect reg write dropped` stops after **4**; `[agc] indexed indirect draw skipped` after 24. #1606 called the `WaitRegMem` volume "the loudest signal, dozens per second"; the true rate is **~1.5/s** (see below). A line count from these is a cap, not a rate. Same trap as #9's dedupe, different mechanism. |
| 14 | A **decoded-draw census read without the render phase** | Draw counts per frame are phase-dependent by two orders of magnitude *within one title*. Blue Prince (rung 3) decodes **7-13 draws/frame on its own menus** and **1,500-3,200 in its 3D scene**. So Nikoderiko's 53/frame on a title/EULA screen looks exactly like #1641's "implausibly few" signature and is simply **normal for a 2D UI screen**. Reporting it would have sent a lane chasing nothing. |
| 15 | "is this dword pair a mapped pointer?" | The user-data window is 32 dwords and holds many live pointers, so **several** seed offsets satisfy "every declared descriptor is readable". On #305 a shifted seed made all declared pointers land cleanly on **9 of 9** stages — and the hardware field that bounds the window (`SPI_SHADER_PGM_RSRC2_GS.USER_SGPR`) proved the stage cannot see there at all. A live A/B then raised rejects 118-141 -> 521. A numeric fit over a pointer-shaped predicate is weak evidence; find the register that bounds the search space. |
| 16 | One diagnostic **label** covering two packet kinds | #305's bind trace emitted `[bind] DRAW` from all five per-item snapshot sites in `GpuState::apply` — and **two of those are compute dispatches**. A dispatch never consumes `SPI_SHADER_PGM_LO_ES`, so a bind/draw agreement statistic computed over those lines mixes in events that cannot agree by construction. Graphics-queue dispatches read the *graphics* register file, so they print plausible `es_lo` and are **indistinguishable from draws in the log** — a re-run, not a re-parse. **Re-measured after relabelling** (a fresh boot — the two figures are not the same run: 434,239 bind packets vs 193,397), the contaminated "871,648 of 876,217 (99.5%)" became **300,404 of 300,404 (100%)**, so the residue had been dispatches. Tag every emitter with the kind it actually observed. |
| 17 | **Asymmetric** exhaustion of per-site caps | A cousin of #13. When one side of a paired trace caps (bind lines, one counter at 4,000) and the other runs on **five independent counters**, the first side dies during the pre-title load while the second keeps emitting; a "does each draw follow a bind?" analysis then reported a 41% mismatch that was pure counter exhaustion. Bound the analysis to the region where **every** stream is still logging. Related: `command_order` is **per-submit and resets**, so ordering events globally by it interleaves submits and manufactures impossible pairs. |
| 18 | `VAR=1 $unquoted_var cmd` | The shell expands `$unquoted_var` **after** it has decided which word is the command, so an expansion like `PROSPER_NO_PLUGIN_AUTOLINK=1` becomes the **command name**, not an assignment. The arm never runs, exits 127, and — with stdout redirected to a per-arm log — leaves an empty file that reads exactly like a legitimate "no output" result. #1609's founding 14,666-vs-18,167 "20% regression" came from an A/B whose OFF arm had never executed. Build env into the command directly, or use `env VAR=1 cmd`. |
| 19 | `$HOME` read as private scratch | Every lane on this box shares one `$HOME` (410 files at the time of writing). Generic scratch names — `pr-body.md`, `ab-result.md`, `app.log`, `apr.log` — **already collide across lanes**. Writing one destroys another agent's work in flight, and reading one silently answers your question with someone else's data. Title-scope every scratch file (`tales-pr-body.md`, `tales-rate-*.log`). The tell that saved it here: the Write tool **refused to overwrite a file it had not read**. A tool declining to clobber is a safety net, not an obstacle — an agent that "fixes" it by deleting the file and retrying destroys the thing the guard was protecting. |
| 20 | An **empty CI rollup** read as "queued" | GitHub does **not run checks on a conflicting branch at all**, so a PR whose base has moved reports `pass=0 fail=0 pending=0` with `mergeStateStatus=DIRTY` — permanently, until it is rebased. That is byte-identical to the "checks have not registered yet" state, and #1656 sat in it while master moved twice. Same shape as #18's empty log: **an absent signal mimicking a pending one**. Read `mergeable`/`mergeStateStatus` before concluding anything from a check count, and never wait on zeros. |
| 21 | The **same draw census, now read without the resident *content*** — trap 14 firing a second time, on the lane that recorded it | #1641 spent a session treating "~23 draws/frame is implausibly few for a 4K UE4 title" as the anomaly. Decoding the title's own asset reads against its pak index showed it loads **exactly one of the 481 maps** in the container — `L_GameloftSplash.umap`, **7,870 bytes** — and never opens `L_Main` or `L_StartMap`. 23 draws is a **complete and correct** deferred frame over a splash world. Worse, the premise was invalid in *both* directions: `L_Main.uexp` is **616 bytes**, so this title's front end is UMG/Slate and a *working* title screen would also decode few 3D draws. Nothing was ever missing. |
| 22 | A **runtime `eboot+0x…` offset** | Several diagnostics subtract the literal `0x400000000` while the eboot maps at `BOOT_EBOOT = 0x410000000`, so the printed offset is **`0x10000000` too high** — wrong but entirely plausible, and every downstream `edis.py`/`xref.py`/`PROSPER_BP` lookup then fails in a way that looks like a code problem. `boot_trace.cpp:97` uses the right constant, so two conventions coexist under one label. Filed as **#1659**. Tell: an `eboot+0x1…` offset **past the module image size** is a labelling artifact, not a wild pointer. |
| 23 | A **call-count table read as evidence of *what* is being waited on** | `PROSPER_PROGRESS_UNIMPL`'s per-NID counts showed **1,300,185,430 `select()` calls in 144 s** on PPSA19244 while every other socket call stayed a frozen singleton — a real, liveness-controlled measurement. It was then read as "the guest is blocked polling a socket", and a shared-substrate design decision (implement a loopback socket backing) was nearly escalated on it. Reading the **arguments** took one `PROSPER_STUBDUMP` lookup plus one gdb breakpoint and showed `select(nfds=0, NULL, NULL, NULL, {3,0})` — the portable **`sleep()`** idiom, no descriptor involved. The thread was trying to sleep 3 s; the 0-returning stub made it a no-op. Not a socket poll, not the stall — a CPU tax. A count answers *"is something spinning?"*; it can **never** answer *"on what?"*, and the function's name is not evidence. |
| 24 | A log whose **emission gate** is narrower than the population you reason over | `[udmap]` (#305) prints a stage's user-data map **only under failure replay**, so every row it emits is a stage that ALREADY FAILED. Its rows therefore sample failures, not stages — and a row showing readable pointers is a stage that failed for a *different* reason, **not a passing stage**. Building a pass/fail table from it silently answers a question it was never sampling. Same category error as #9 and #13 (a count that answers "did this ever happen?" but not "how often?"), one level up: here the **rows** are the biased sample, not the counts. Before treating any log's rows as a population, read the condition it is emitted under — grep the `if` above the `fprintf`, do not infer it from the message text. |
| 25 | `gh pr merge --delete-branch` run **from a worktree** | After the server-side merge succeeds, `gh` tries to check out the base branch locally and dies with `fatal: 'master' is already used by worktree at …` — which is unavoidable from a worktree, since master is checked out in the main tree. **The merge lands; the branch survives; the exit status does not cleanly separate the two.** Two lanes hit this in one session, both on their own merge. Read as "the merge failed" it invites a retry against an already-merged PR; read as "it all worked" it leaves a stale remote branch that later reads as an unfinished lane (and doubles as a false claim-lock, per the claim rules). Verify the two halves **separately**: `gh pr view N --json state,mergeCommit` for the merge, `git ls-remote origin 'refs/heads/<branch>'` for the branch, then `git push origin --delete <branch>` explicitly. |
| 26 | A **commit-SHA-pinned asset URL** in a PR body or issue | Screenshots referenced as `raw.githubusercontent.com/<org>/<repo>/<sha>/…` break silently once that SHA is **orphaned** — which a rebase does immediately and a squash merge does at merge time. The images render perfectly while the PR is open and are dead weeks later, when nobody can reconstruct what they showed. Pin progression evidence to a **branch that survives** (`…/master/assets/screenshots/…`) once the file is on it, and re-point any SHA URL after the final rebase. Cousin of #11: both are the same mistake of treating a SHA as a stable name across a history rewrite. |
| 27 | **A confident document header with no observation behind it** | Three parked `reach-gameplay.pad` drafts (Joe & Mac, Asterix, Summer Sports) carried three *different* title-specific rationales — "Options is a fallback for a start-button title prompt", "the title screen is reached with no input at all", "Cross confirms the default selector entries" — over **byte-identical input sequences** (`md5` of the non-comment lines matched across all three). Three different findings cannot come from one identical button mash, so the prose was generated, not observed. The genuine routes were in the tracking issues the whole time. |
| 28 | A **distinct-colour count quoted across two different render scales** | Triage issue #1593 recorded Joe & Mac gameplay at **444,000-468,000** distinct colours; profiling the same route through `tools/snapshot` measured **at most 79,167**, a 5.6x gap that reads exactly like a severe colour regression. Both are correct. Triage captures run the `screenshot` frontend at native 1920x1080; every snapshot guard runs at `scale: 4`, so the frame is **480x270 = 129,600 pixels** and cannot exceed that many colours by construction. A 3840x2160 title (*Summer Sports Games*) yields 960x540 at the same scale, so even `dims` differs between titles. **Never lift a `min_colors` threshold from a triage issue**, and read `dims` off the profile instead of assuming the 1080p value. |
| 29 | **Ranking candidate guard windows by metric stability** | Choosing a content-guard window by "tightest colour spread / highest self-SSIM" is the obvious automation, and on *Summer Sports Games* it confidently selects the settled span from 150 s to the end of the route: 48,201-48,489 colours, 1.0000 coverage, **SSIM 1.000 against itself** — the best-scoring window in the entire run by every available metric. It is the static `JAVELIN THROW` **standings overlay**, not gameplay. A guard adopted there would have passed forever while the athletics scene collapsed. Stability is a property of *held* frames, and menus, results screens and pause overlays are the most held frames a title has, so the metric is actively biased toward them. Rank windows to shortlist; **decide by opening the images**. Same family as the earlier note that a diagnostic clear can out-score real content on distinct colours. |
| 30 | **A wall-clock guard window placed in a route that keeps moving** | A content guard samples by wall-clock seconds, but in a route that drives the player *forward* the scene at second N is a function of ~N seconds of accumulated progress, so ordinary run-to-run variance moves the **scene**, not just the timing. Joe & Mac's movement route passed `verify` twice and a `check` twice, then the third run drifted onto a level transition plus two death fades and failed 19 structural matches against 24 required — no build change, same route. Replacing it with the **stationary** route inverted the problem: the player is killed every ~22 s but respawns to the *same* opening screen, so a 73-frame window spanning three lives is one visual state and tolerates the deaths (69 matches against 55 required, worst-frame SSIM 0.97 instead of 0.81). The more impressive route made the worse guard. Prefer a route whose guarded span is **self-restoring** — an idle screen, a respawn loop, a held camera — over one whose span is a trajectory, and treat two green runs as far too small a sample to call a moving window stable. |
| 31 | The **first `grep` hit for a register**, when it has more than one consumer | Tracing why Astro's draws resolve `cwm=0`, `CB_COLOR_CONTROL.MODE` is read at `render_state.cpp:143` — where it feeds **only** the gated `PROSPER_MSAA_LOG` diagnostic. Reasoning from that site says MODE does not participate in mask resolution, which **inverts the conclusion**. The site that matters is ~650 lines later, where `MODE == DISABLE` explicitly zeroes `color_write_mask`, `color1_write_mask` and every `color_targets[].write_mask`. Same symbol, two consumers, opposite implications — and nothing at the first site announces that it is not the one. **Find the consumer on the path you are reasoning about**: for a render-state question that is where the pipeline state is built, not where a diagnostic prints. |
| 32 | **`std::mismatch` read as a failure count** | The R11G11B10 exactness sweep (#1681) printed its first difference with `std::mismatch`, which returns only the **first** differing element, while the assertion compared the **whole vector**. The printout is byte-identical whether 1 texel differs or 92 — so "the same single texel fails on both machines" was a number the instrument never produced, and the supporting argument built on it ("a genuinely wrong encoding would fail broadly rather than at a single value") had no evidence behind it. The real count was **92**. Cousin of #9/#13/#24: an instrument that answers *"did this happen?"* being read as *"how often?"* — here at the level of a single printed element rather than a rate. **When an assertion compares a collection, the diagnostic must summarise the collection**: count the differences, classify them, and report the classification, because the breadth of a failure is usually the thing that selects between explanations. |
| 33 | **A skipped draw's clear colour read as a rendered pixel** | Six assertions in `test_recompiled_fragment` and one in `test_recompiled_shaders` "failed a rendered-pixel comparison" on lavapipe (#1681). No comparison happened: the shaders require an exact 64-lane fragment subgroup, lavapipe is fixed at 8, and prosper **correctly refused the draw** (`[render] skip draw: fragment shader requires subgroup size 64 (device range 8..8)`, 15 times). The target simply kept its BLUE clear, and a clear colour is a perfectly plausible-looking pixel value — nothing about `center=(0,0,255,255)` announces that no draw ran. The neighbouring assertions in the *same files* already gated on `supports_fragment_wave64_vote`; these did not. **A pixel assertion is only evidence if the draw executed** — assert the capability, or assert the clear, but never read back a target without knowing which of the two you are looking at. Note the failure direction: a fail-visible skip is designed to be loud, and it was — on **stderr**, while the assertions printed to stdout, so the two never appeared together in the ctest summary. |
| 34 | **A check of the ANSWER read as a check of the RULE** — and a shape shared by several entries here | Deriving an NV12 frame size (#1687), the rule "`w*h*3/2` and `wh + (wh+1)/2` agree" took three attempts: "identical for every reachable input", then "agree whenever `w*h` is even", then the correct one. **Two** were wrong, each in a smaller way; the third was right. The truth is **exact iff BOTH dimensions are even** — independently re-derived three times, most widely at 200x200 (40,000 pairs), never over-reporting — an even *product* is not sufficient and is actively a trap, since `1920x1081` has an even product and the derivation is **960 bytes short**. The error survived three rounds of competent review because every size anyone checked (`1920x1088`, `1920x1080`, `640x480`) is **both-even**, so each spot check confirmed the true rule *and* the wrong one equally. **The cases in front of you are not a sample; they are a coincidence until you show they discriminate.** An exhaustive grid settled it in one command and could have been run first. The general form: verifying the *answer* on the examples you already have tells you nothing about the *rule* — you must ask what input would distinguish your rule from its near-miss, and check that one. Named instances rather than a fraction: an A/B whose arms agree when they must differ (the tell under #10), a pre-registered decision rule that cannot separate its two hypotheses, a vacuous assertion that holds whether or not the event occurred, #33's pixel assertion on a draw that never ran, and this one. **Every one of those PASSED**, and that is the mechanism of concealment, not a coincidence: a green result is **self-authenticating**, so a check that *could not have failed* is indistinguishable from a check that genuinely passed, and both get filed as confirmation. A failing check gets investigated; a vacuous one gets believed. **So the question to ask of your own test before you write down what it showed is not "did it pass?" but "what result would have falsified it?"** — if there is no answer, the test established nothing, however green. Note where it bit: this is normally reached for around *instruments*, and here it was plain arithmetic — nobody thinks to apply the discipline to a formula. |
| 35 | A **`.bmp` and a same-named `.prgbundle` assumed to be one F9 grab** | The frontend writes a grab's bundle through a `.tmp` and its screenshot separately, so **killing the app mid-grab leaves a `.bmp` whose same-named `.prgbundle` is from a previous grab entirely**. A Dragon Quest VII investigation was handed `dq7_grab_001.*` and `dq7_grab_002.*` as "two frames of the same scene in two states" — the highest-value comparison available. `stat` showed 001's pair 0.2 s apart, but `dq7_grab_002.prgbundle` **51 minutes older than `dq7_grab_002.bmp`, from a different boot**; the screenshot's real partner was the abandoned `frame_grab_002.prgbundle.tmp` written 0.7 s before it (and it loads fine). The mismatched pair was a 1920x1080/1519-draw **scene** submit against a 3840x2160/97-draw **composite** submit, which would have manufactured a large, entirely artefactual structural difference in exactly the comparison the investigation was told to trust. Names and adjacent ordinals are not provenance: **`stat` the artifacts and confirm sub-second pairing before treating a screenshot as evidence about a bundle** — and prefer a `.tmp` with the right mtime over a completed file with the wrong one. **Fixed at the source (#1693 / #1694), and the replacement rule is exact:** a grab claims BOTH names when it is armed, from one timestamp and at one collision suffix, so **the two artifacts of one grab always carry the identical full stem, suffix included** — the suffix is a property of the capture, not of a file. So pair on the **whole stem**, never on the timestamp: `…-210000-123.bmp` and `…-210000-123-2.prgbundle` are two *different* grabs despite an identical title and millisecond, and reading them as a pair re-creates this exact trap with more confidence than before. And note the superseded `stat` check does not merely go *quiet* on such a pair — it **endorses** it: capture A's screenshot and capture B's bundle are written tens of milliseconds apart, so "confirm sub-second pairing" returns a confident **false positive**. A check that actively affirms the wrong answer has to be superseded in writing rather than left to fall into disuse — which is why this row states where it still applies rather than simply deleting it. The `stat` habit remains correct for artifacts captured before that landed — `captures/frame_grab_001.*` in the main checkout is exactly that shape — and for anything else named by ordinal. |
| 36 | **`snapshot.py verify` passing, on a scene the title regenerates randomly** | `verify` builds its structural references from **both** of its own two runs and then asks whether those runs match that combined set — so a scene that is *different every run* still passes, because each run matches the half of the reference set it contributed. Worms Armageddon's Quickstart generates its terrain per match: two fresh-save runs produced a pirate/treasure map and a fairytale/castle map, each scoring 70/70 against the combined references and looking like a healthy `CONTENT-STABLE` baseline. Measured **across** runs instead — run B's frames against run A's references **only** — the same window scores at most **0.6567**, with **0 of 70** clearing the 0.85 floor. Such a guard would be adopted green and fail on the very first `check`, whose third random map matches neither. The tell is available before any capture: the 16x9 SSIM is highly layout-sensitive on this title (the *same* map mirrored scores 0.4595), so any terrain variation is fatal. **Before adopting a window, score one run against the other run's references alone** — verify's own output cannot answer this, and 'it passed verify twice' is not evidence of stability (cousin of #30, where two green runs were also too small a sample). The fix here was a route change, not a threshold change: Training levels are authored fixed maps and cross-run scoring on them gives 0.9612-1.0000, 47/47. |
| 37 | **A key injected into `prosper-app` that the app never receives — silently** | Validating the F9 frame grab (#1693) on this box, `xdotool key F9` reached a correctly named, correctly focused XWayland window and produced **nothing**. Two readings were available and both were wrong: "F9 is broken" and "my change broke F9". The controls settle it: injected **Esc quits the app** and injected **Pause pauses** it, while **F10 and F11 — which this change does not touch — are lost exactly like F9**, and `Pause` and `F10` are literally the *same command* in the same handler. So XTEST on this host does not deliver **function keys** to the window; nothing about the app is implicated. `ydotool` (uinput, `ydotool key 67:1 67:0` for F9) does deliver them, and the whole F9 path then worked first time. **Two rules:** a key-injection experiment must carry a positive control through the *same* path — end the run with an injected `Esc` and require the app to exit, or the negative result is void rather than negative — and `2>/dev/null` on the injector hides the commonest failure of all (the first attempt ran `xdotool` **inside** the distrobox, where it is not installed, and the redirect swallowed `command not found`). |
| 38 | **A driver-version split, read as a driver bug** | Two 3D storage-volume assertions in `test_game_compute` failed on Mesa 25.2.8 lavapipe and passed on Mesa 26.1.4 lavapipe **and** on RADV (#1690). Both driver *classes* agreed with each other and disagreed with one version, which is about as clean a version signal as this list ever gets — so the working hypothesis was an upstream lavapipe defect, and the proposed resolution was a version-conditioned skip. It was **prosper's own fixture**: `image_copy_3d` prepared a third address register and called itself 3D while leaving MIMG.DIM encoded as 2D, so the recompiler faithfully emitted `OpTypeImage … 2D` with two coordinates while the backend bound the `VK_IMAGE_VIEW_TYPE_3D` view its 3D *resource* descriptor calls for. A 3D view on a descriptor whose module declares Dim 2D is **undefined** in Vulkan, so all three results are conformant and the "version axis" was three drivers each resolving one piece of UB their own way. Encoding DIM=3D passes everywhere. **The rule this teaches is about what a cross-implementation split licenses you to conclude: it localises the disagreement, it does not assign the fault.** Two implementations agreeing does not make them right — UB is agreed on far more often than it is diagnosed — so before attributing a split to the odd one out, ask what *you* are asking all of them to do, and whether it is defined. The cheap discriminator was available the whole time and settles it in one run: a **standalone reproduction of the same operation** with none of prosper's code in the path (own instance, device, images, hand-written kernel). It passed on 25.2.8, which exonerates the driver and points the search back inside — the inverse of the usual instrument trap, since here the *subject* was fine and prosper's own input was malformed. Two corroborations were also sitting in the tree, unread: `fill_3d`, in the same test file, already encoded `0xF0200F10` as `dim:3D` and passed on 25.2.8, and `test_rdna2_decode.cpp:309` pins the same DIM field — bits [5:3] = 2 — against an `llvm-mc gfx1010` capture, though on a different literal (`0xf0000f12`: another opcode, plus an NSA bit), so compare the field rather than the constant when following that pointer. Cousin of #34 — a green result on two of three configurations is self-authenticating, and "it works on the good drivers" is exactly what correct code and undefined behaviour look like alike. Note the cost of the wrong reading: the proposed skip would have removed **484 executed assertions** from CI permanently, annotated as a driver limitation. |
| 39 | **A diagnostic that falls silent in the very cases it was cited to distinguish** | Issue #1692 described `PROSPER_AUDIOLOG=1` as "a three-way instrument whose negative result is informative in each branch" for Dragon Quest VII's missing audio, listing *no lines* -> the guest never submits, *`1s-peak=0.0000`* -> silent PCM, *non-zero peak* -> our sink. Reading the code inverts that: the `[audio] port N` line is only reached after `have_pcm && open_ok`, so it never prints when no MAIN port yields frames, and its companion `PROSPER_AUDIO2_PROBE` deliberately prints only ports whose `\|max\| > 0`. **Both fall silent in branches 1 AND 2**, so the documented "the guest never submits" signature is also exactly what a fully-submitting title produces. A sharper form sat one level down: a port rejected for an unsupported layout (DQ7's 12-channel `fmt=0xc00`, discarded 188x/s) is **never read**, so "no audio there" was an assumption no instrument had tested. **Two rules from that half.** A negative result is only evidence if the instrument would have printed something in the other branches — check where its print sits relative to the early-outs, not what its docstring claims. And a suppression added for log hygiene silently narrows what the instrument can *prove*: `\|max\| > 0` was a reasonable anti-spam filter for object-heavy titles, and it is precisely what made silence unreportable. **Then the replacement instrument caught its own author with the same class of error, one level up — and this is the more useful half.** Its counters reset every second, and the first reading took the LAST line as the run's answer: port1 `nonzero=0/580608` -> "the guest submits only silence", case 2, defect upstream in the title. Wrong, and inverted: that port's run total is **1,782,048 non-zero of 18,456,576** samples at peak **0.38976**, while the 8-channel port prosper actually forwards is the exactly-zero one. The truth is case 3 — **prosper's own mix loop discarding 2.31 MB/s of real audio** because the port declares 12 channels and the loop rejects >8 (#1700). Music has gaps; any single interval can land in one. A second defect compounded it: the per-port table was global while the report iterated it per context, so each context printed **and reset** the other's counters, splitting every port's per-second totals across unrelated lines. **A per-interval counter answers "what happened in the last second", which is not the question "does this port carry audio"** — so the fix is a never-reset lifetime total on the same line, making one line sufficient. Generalising past audio: whenever a diagnostic resets on a timer, one sample of it is a *rate*, never a *verdict*; if you intend to conclude something about the run, aggregate the run or print a running total. Cousin of #34 — ask what result would have falsified the reading. Note where both errors sat: on the **believing** side. The instrument printed enough to catch each one, and the reading did not use it. |
| 40 | A **pipeline's exit status**, read as the exit status of the command you cared about | A shell pipeline reports the status of its **last** stage, so `cmd \| tail`, `cmd \| head` and `cmd \| grep` all discard `cmd`'s failure. Four people hit this in one session, in four disguises: `ctest … \| tail -3` inside an `&&` chain sailed **past a red ctest** and committed (the test source had been restored but not rebuilt, so it was also running a stale binary); `… \| head` truncated a run whose failure was below the cut; `printf "$?"` after a pipeline printed the wrong stage's code; and `grep -c` **exits 1 on zero matches**, so a legitimate "none found" result kills the rest of an `&&` chain. The two failure directions are opposite and both silent: a red that reads green, and a green that aborts the chain. **Never join a command whose status you need to a pipe inside `&&`.** Capture it (`cmd > log; rc=$?`), use `set -o pipefail`, or check `${PIPESTATUS[0]}` — and separate `grep -c` with `;` rather than `&&`. Cousin of #25, where a merge's two halves shared one exit code. **The zero-match exit bites `\|\|` guards too, and there it CORRUPTS A VALUE instead of aborting a chain.** `n=$(pgrep -x -c "<names>" 2>/dev/null \|\| echo 0)` looks like a careful default, and is not: `pgrep -c` prints `0` **and** exits 1 on zero matches, so the fallback fires *in addition to* pgrep's own output and `n` becomes the two-line string `"0\n0"`, which no `[ "$n" = "0" ]` will ever match. The direction is the sting — **a waiter for "is the box free" is broken precisely and only when the box IS free**, because the non-zero exit *is* the zero-match condition, so the bug is invisible in every case it handles correctly and fires only in the case it exists to detect. Any `\|\|` fallback that appends rather than replaces has this shape. Note the contrast with the `&&` form above: **an aborted chain announces itself; a doubled `0` does not.** *Fixes:* put the fallback on the assignment (`n=$(pgrep -x -c "…" 2>/dev/null) \|\| n=0`), or sidestep the exit code entirely with `n=$(pgrep -x "…" 2>/dev/null \| wc -l)`, which always prints one number and always exits 0. *And the mitigation that saved this one is worth its own clause, because it arrived by accident:* the loop carried trap 135's iteration cap, so an incorrect waiter terminated and printed `TIMED OUT waiting for a clear box; NOT launching` instead of hanging silently. **Bounding a waiter does not make it correct, but it turns an incorrect one into a loud failure instead of a quiet absence** — which is the strongest argument for the "bound every waiter regardless" rule in row 135, and it cost eleven minutes of a free box rather than a session. #3348. |
| 41 | A **wholesale `git checkout <branch> -- <file>`**, read as "I brought my edit across" | Rebuilding a branch onto a moved master, taking `tools/AGENTS.md` whole from the pre-rebuild branch **silently deleted the ten lines #1701 had just added** documenting `check_numbered_table.py`. No conflict, no failing job, and the diff read as the clean one-bullet edit it was supposed to be — the file simply reverted to its older state wherever the other lane had touched it. It surfaced only because `--stat` said `12 +-----` for a file believed touched once. **The new `Docs` CI gate cannot catch this**, by design: it validates table structure and numbering, never prose, and `AGENTS.md` has no numbered table — so the numbering half of the two-lane collision problem is now mechanically guarded and the content half is not. After any wholesale checkout of a file another lane may have touched, **diff it against `master` and read the `-` lines, not the `+` ones**. Same family as the clean-merge-semantically-broken hazard: the operation succeeds, the diff looks right, and the loss is invisible without asking what was removed. **A SECOND DELIVERY MECHANISM, and it is worse: STALENESS, with no wrong command at all (#2082).** A branch rebased earlier that then sits while master moves will, on merge, delete other lanes' work from shared documents — no `git checkout`, `mergeable: CLEAN`, every required check green, and the deletions reading as the author's own diff. Two near-misses within an hour: the R-Type Delta lane (#2061) would have deleted the entire Crisis Core rung-2 section from `COMPATIBILITY.md` (landed by #2060) and reverted `CLAUDE.md`'s `[compute] skip` correction (landed by #2074) — after rebasing again, `CLAUDE.md` went to +1/-0; the Crisis Core lane (#2060) had the same shape caught by review, because #2055 had appended to the very `ARCRUNNER_STATUS.md` Ruled-out table it was editing. **What makes this variant worse than the original: trap 41 proper requires someone to run a specific wrong command, so it is avoidable by not running it. This one requires NOTHING — only time between a rebase and a merge, which every PR has.** With several lanes live and master moving every few minutes it is the default state, not the exception. The defence is the same one, made per-file and run against CURRENT master immediately before pushing — a `+N/-0` on a file you only added to is the shape you want, and any `-` line in a file another lane owns is the alarm: `for f in COMPATIBILITY.md CLAUDE.md prosper/docs/GAME_COMPAT_ORCHESTRATION.md; do echo "$f +$(git diff origin/master HEAD -- $f \| grep -c '^+[^+]') -$(git diff origin/master HEAD -- $f \| grep -c '^-[^-]')"; done` |
| 42 | **A validation layer that is enabled but reporting nothing** | Switching `VK_LAYER_KHRONOS_validation` on for the first time (#1704) and seeing a test print no findings is **not** evidence the test is clean, and there are two independent reasons for the silence that look identical. The loader may never have inserted the layer at all — `VK_LOADER_LAYERS_ENABLE` names a layer that need not be installed, and nothing complains. Or it loaded and its `report_flags` default to **`error` only**, so a probe asking for best-practices warnings sees nothing; that first probe here also used the wrong setting key (`validate_best_practices` instead of `enables = VK_VALIDATION_FEATURE_ENABLE_BEST_PRACTICES_EXT`), a silent no-op that reads exactly like success. **Prove the reporting path before trusting a null result**: `VK_LOADER_DEBUG=layer` must print `Insert instance layer "VK_LAYER_KHRONOS_validation"`, and a deliberate violation must come back named. `tools/vkval` now refuses to report clean until it has watched the loader say so. **The second half is about what a green run of this guard does and does not license.** Its finding set is driver- and layer-version-dependent **in both directions**: the same suite yields **9** message ids on RADV with layers 1.4.341 and **7** on lavapipe with layers 1.3.275 (the CI runner) — **8 and 6 after #1726 fixed `pCode-08737`**; `tools/vkval/allowlist.txt` carries the current baseline, and this row is the historical measurement, and the two absent ones are absent for reasons unrelated to correctness — `VUID-vkCmdDispatch-maintenance4-08602` is a newer check than 1.3.275, and `VUID-VkMappedMemoryRange-size-01389` cannot fire on lavapipe, whose `nonCoherentAtomSize` is 1, so every range is trivially aligned. A defect that only a stricter layer or a stricter device reports is invisible to a green CI run, so run the guard on hardware too. Cousin of #34 and #39 — ask what result would have falsified the reading, and where the instrument's early-outs sit. |
| 43 | **A declared format read as a description of the content** | Dragon Quest VII's MAIN AudioOut2 port declares `data_format=0xc00` -> **12 channels**, and #1700 was specified from that number: "12 channels is a plausible real layout (7.1.4 - a 7.1 bed plus four height channels)", with the fold-down's difficulty framed as recovering the height tier's order. Measured with `PROSPER_AUDIO_LAYOUT=1` over **19,962,368 frames**, the bed is a **stereo mix in a 12-channel container**: ch0/ch1 carry everything (peak 0.407, mutual correlation +0.46), ch2 and ch4..ch7 hold a ~1e-9 residue, and **ch3 and ch8..ch11 are exactly zero for the entire run** - five channels never written at all, and ten of the twelve carrying nothing audible. The design problem the issue posed did not exist. Two mechanisms, and the second is the one to carry away. First, a **declared** format describes the container the guest allocated, not what it wrote into it; a title may open the widest bed the platform offers and fill two channels of it, exactly as a 4-channel texture may be written with one. Second, and worse, the corroboration looked independent and was not: the `2,310,144 B/s` in the issue is `frames x channels x 4` with `channels` taken from the same decode, so the byte rate could not have disagreed with the channel count **whatever the buffer really contained** - it restates its own input. Same shape as #34: a check that could not have failed, read as confirmation — and a close neighbour of **#42**, which is the same trap approached from the reporting side: there a positive control passed in the development environment while observing 0 of 187 messages in CI, so the instrument was silent for a reason unrelated to the subject. Both entries reduce to the same question — *what, exactly, produced this number, and could it have produced a different one?* **A statistic derived from a decode cannot cross-check that decode**; you need a measurement whose value comes from somewhere else. Two were available here and only one delivered: the per-channel content settled it, while the guest's grain-pointer stride - the other independent measure, and the more direct one - returned nothing, because this title republishes a single buffer and never exposes a second address to difference. Build both anyway; you do not know in advance which the guest will let you have. The practical rule for any width/stride/format field: before designing for the declared shape, measure the payload, and note that this cuts **both** ways - the same evidence that showed ten channels empty is what confirms the two that are not, and reading `rms=0.000000` alone would have merged "exactly zero" with "1e-9 residue", which are different findings (`nonzero%` separates them). **Postscript, one hour after this entry was written: its author nearly filed a second finding from the same investigation — a `mov edx,0x800` inside the title's UE mixer, read as the port `data_format` it had spent the day looking for. It is a `memset` size; the call target is an import stub, so the three registers are `memset(dst, 0, 0x800)`.** Caught only by disassembling the callee instead of trusting the constant. Knowing the rule does not make you immune to it — a value that matches the shape of your answer is read as your answer, and the defence is mechanical (check what consumes the constant) rather than attentional. |
| 44 | A **documented gate whose external tool was never installed**, and a corpus that never grew | `tools/spv_validate` is what CLAUDE.md, both READMEs, `ROADMAP.md` and `RECOMPILER_REMAINING.md` mean when they say prosper's shaders are strictly `spirv-val`-gated. It shells out to `spirv-val` — and when the validator was absent it printed **`== PASS (recompiled; spirv-val not found) ==` and exited 0**. `spirv-val` is on no GitHub runner image and `ci.yml` never installed `spirv-tools`, so **the gate had never validated a single module in CI**; it degraded the same way on any dev host without the package, and unconditionally on Windows, where the availability probe redirected to `/dev/null` that `cmd.exe` cannot open. What it cost is #1711: `build_compute_compare_uvec4()` walked struct -> runtime array -> `uvec4` and claimed a `uint` pointer for the result, and that module is created at runtime by the live frontend (`live_compute.cpp`'s compare pipeline), not only by a test. **Drivers are not required to validate SPIR-V and neither lavapipe nor RADV does**, so it was accepted everywhere and reported by nothing until the first validation-layer pass (#1704). The second half is coverage: the corpus only ever walked `recompile_*`, so `spirv_builder.cpp`'s hand-assembled modules were **not in it and nothing said so** — an emitter is invisible to a gate that does not know it exists. **Two rules. When a check delegates to an external tool, the tool's absence is a FAILURE, not a pass — and 'the gate exists' is not 'the gate runs', so check that its dependency is installed where it runs, which for a `system()` call means reading the CI dependency list, not the test's source. And a fixed corpus silently stops covering the code as the code grows: derive the list of things that must be checked from the source (here, every emitter declaration under `src/gpu/`) so an omission fails instead of going unmentioned — and derive it under **every spelling the code actually uses**: the first version of this scan keyed on the literal `std::vector<uint32_t>` return type and therefore could not see the live draw path's `SharedShaderWords` entry points at all, reinstating the same blind spot one level up while the docs asserted it was closed.** Closed in both directions plus mutation-tested: **eleven** deliberate re-introductions each make it fail — invalid access chain; an uncovered emitter declared in an existing header, in a NEW header, in a new SUBDIRECTORY, in the frontend root, and under the other return-type spelling the live path uses; missing validator; unwritable output directory; stale gap entry; gap naming no emitter; and an emitter that is called but whose module is never validated. The last two mutations are the ones worth copying: the first drafts of this guard read the tool's own source text for a call-shaped string, so a mention in a comment satisfied it and a call whose result was discarded satisfied it. Coverage is now a **runtime** fact recorded where each module is emitted, which is the property meant all along — **if your check is a proxy for the thing you care about, mutate the gap between them, not the thing** — and mutate it along every axis the proxy could be wrong on, because the first three revisions of this guard were each closed on one axis (text, then filename, then return-type spelling) while remaining open on the next. Note the trap caught its own fix, which is the sharpest part: the first draft recorded `recompile_interpolation_geometry` as a known-gap 'because #1715 says spirv-val rejects it'. **Measured, spirv-val accepts it** under both the universal and vulkan1.1 environments — #1715's `VkPipelineShaderStageCreateInfo-stage-00715` is a *pipeline-creation* VUID that spirv-val does not check — so the emitter is fully covered and the gap list is empty. An issue title is a claim about where a defect lives, not a measurement; running the tool took one command. Cousin of #34: a green `== PASS ==` is self-authenticating whether it validated 49 modules or none. |
| 45 | A **shell string passed to `gh` as a comment body**, read as literal text | Posting a measurement with `gh pr comment --body "…"` ran it through the shell first. A backtick-quoted identifier inside it — `` `pCode-08737` ``, part of a quoted diff excerpt — was taken as **command substitution**, and the comment posted with that token **silently deleted**: `insert at master[1334] -> ' — **8 and 6 after #1726 fixed **; …'`. `gh` exited 0, the URL printed, and the rendered comment looked entirely well formed. **The damage lands in the durable record rather than in your terminal**, which is what separates this from the ordinary quoting mistake — you are not debugging it later, you are not looking at it at all. Note which case this was: the backticks held a NON-command, so bash printed `pCode-08737: command not found` and there was at least a warning to notice. Wrap something that IS a command — `` `date` ``, `` `ls` ``, `` `git status` `` — and substitution succeeds **silently** and splices its output into the permanent artifact; `$(…)` in a pasted log excerpt executes it. So the visible failure is the lucky branch, and reasoning "I would have seen it" from a case where you did is exactly backwards. **The defence is mechanical, not attentional: a quoted heredoc (`<<'EOF'`) or `--body-file`, never an interpolating string** — and this is the same trap as the pipeline in #40, one layer out: the shell silently rewrites what you thought was data. Escaping backticks by hand inside `--body "…"` does work, and is luck rather than discipline the moment a body is pasted rather than typed; two agents' bodies survived this session only that way. Applies equally to `gh issue create/comment`, `gh pr create --body`, and `git commit -m` with a pasted message. Repair with `gh api -X PATCH repos/OWNER/REPO/issues/comments/<id> -F body=@file` and say what was eaten, since a reader cannot tell a mangled measurement from a wrong one. |
| 46 | **Three samples of a monotonic series, read as the value of the series** | Tracing Astro Bot's dominant compute program (#1732), the first three `PROSPER_COMPUTELOG_CODE` lines all read `groups=1x1x1` — one 256-invocation workgroup. That is a clean, repeated, self-consistent reading, and it licensed the conclusion that the launch geometry was fine and the kernel's 128,005-word SPIR-V module must be why one dispatch took 982 ms. Both halves were wrong. The **whole** series grows: `groups_x` is 1 for the first 22 dispatches, then increments by exactly 3,345 every dispatch and never decreases, reaching **752,646 workgroups (192.7 M invocations) by dispatch 247** and still climbing. The geometry was the entire defect, and the sample that hid it was drawn from the only region where the series is flat — its first frames, which is precisely where a `head -3` lands. Cousin of #34: the cases in front of you are a coincidence until you show they discriminate. **The rule is specific and cheap: when a value might drift, never quote an element of it — print the series, or at minimum its first, middle, last and the deltas.** `sort \| uniq -c` on the traced field would also have exposed it in one command, since a constant collapses to one row and a drifting value does not. Note which direction the error ran: the wrong reading was *more* interesting (a recompiler defect in a huge module) and would have sent a lane into the SPIR-V for something a two-column table settles. |
| 47 | **A phase timer that reports a NEGATIVE interval, and relocates the bottleneck while doing it** | `[compute-phase]` initialises every phase mark to `phase_start` and only advances `phase_dispatch`/`phase_writeback` on the success path, so a dispatch that fails early prints the phase spanning the break computed **backwards** — a real Astro Bot record reads `dispatch_ms=-61.52` — while `cleanup_ms` silently absorbs the entire record (#1744). Which field goes negative depends on where the break landed, so a consumer must check every phase rather than grep for one field name. On a title where **82 % of the dispatches that reach `execute_item` fail** (63 % of all guest dispatches — the two figures are true of different populations, and this sentence is about the density of broken records *in the file*, #1743) this is not an edge case, it is most of the data: aggregating the log naively yields `dispatch (GPU) = -20,532 ms` and moves 46 % of the run into `cleanup`, which reads as a perfectly plausible "cleanup is the bottleneck". **The instrument does not fall silent on failure — it confidently names a different bottleneck**, which is strictly worse than #39's silence, because there is nothing missing to notice. Two rules. **A decomposition must be split by success before it is summed**, since failed and succeeded work are different populations with different meanings for the same field names. And **an aggregator should assert its own arithmetic is possible** — elapsed time is non-negative, shares lie in [0, 100], children do not exceed their parent — because those checks cost one line and are the only thing standing between a broken emitter and a confident wrong table. `tools/perf/compute_phase_report.py` now excludes `ok=0` records, reports them separately, and warns on any negative total — on stdout as a banner inside the table as well as on stderr, because `> table.txt` discards stderr and keeps a table that still looks authoritative. |
| 48 | **A mutation that turns the SUITE red, read as proof that the check you wrote for it works** | The escalation of #34 and the natural next error after "mutate the mechanism and watch it fail". Verifying #1747, every fix was mutated away and the suite confirmed red, and the commit message said so. **One check had nevertheless survived its own mutation, masked by three red siblings.** `"0.00" not in token` was standing in for "is zero", and it was wrong in both directions: changing an unrelated fixture constant from 10 to 13 turned it red (it scanned the whole output and caught the phase table's row, passing only because `"10.000"` contains `"0.00"`), while deleting the alias exclusion it guarded left it **green**, because the buggy value is `0.001` and `"0.00" in "0.001"`. Suite granularity cannot see this: a survivor hidden behind other failures is indistinguishable from coverage. **Record the check name per mutation, not the suite's colour** — assert that the mutation is killed by the check written for it, and treat "killed by something else" as a failure. Making that mechanical (`tools/perf/mutate_compute_phase_report.sh`) immediately found two more defects in the very suite that had just been declared fully mutation-checked: a fixture whose succeeded and failed records **cancelled**, so the negative-time assertion proved nothing, and a mutation aimed at the *reporting* filter when the *exclusion* filter was the mechanism — killed by an unrelated check, which at suite granularity reads as success. Note the compounding shape: this is the **fourth** member of the family inside one PR whose subject was documenting rows 46 and 47 of this very table — and row 46 is itself *about* quoting one element of a growing series, which is the same error one level down. Knowing a rule demonstrably confers no immunity to it, which is the argument for mechanising the defence rather than resolving to be careful. And the specific trap in the substring: **a formatted number contains other numbers**, as a prefix (`"0.00"` in `"0.001"`) *and* as an infix (`"0.00"` in `"10.000"`) — both of this row's own examples — so `in`/`not in` against a rendered number is never a numeric comparison. Parse the field and compare it. **Finally, the residual, which this entry of all entries must not overstate: mechanising per-check attribution does not close the ladder.** The committed harness shipped with a *fifth* instance in it — a mutation broader than the defect it stood for (returning on ANY alias where the original returned only on ALL), which let an unrelated check claim the kill and print `killed by:`. So there is a level five: **the mutation must reproduce the defect's shape**, or the per-check assertion is satisfied by a check that could not have caught the original. Expect a level six. |
| 49 | **A falsified hypothesis, still believed after the boot it was measured on stopped existing** | `COMPATIBILITY.md` recorded *"Input gating — falsified. A 200 s route mashing cross/circle/triangle/options left `draws_last=28` across all 99,427 submits"* for Tales of Graces f (`PPSA19991`), and every later pass — including two that re-measured the title — reasoned from it and looked at the opening movie instead. The measurement was sound when taken. Then #1656 and #1666 landed, the guest started reaching a **modal confirmation dialog** it had previously never got to, and an identical mashing route now takes `draws_last` from 8 to 132 and renders the title screen, the EULA, the main menu and the new-game Options screen (#1609). **Input gating was the answer the whole time after those two fixes.** The trap is not the original test; it is that a `## Ruled out` entry is written as a timeless fact ("input gating is not it") when what was measured is a fact about one build ("input gating was not it *at that boot depth*"). A negative result about **guest behaviour** decays whenever a fix changes how far the guest gets, and progression fixes do that by construction — so the entries most likely to go stale are exactly the ones on titles that are actively being advanced. Two practical rules. **Record the boot state a negative result was measured on**, not just the result: the commit, and cheaply, the depth (`draws_last`, the last phase reached, the last visible frame). And **re-run the cheap negatives after any fix that moves a title forward** — an input route costs one run and is the cheapest hypothesis in the catalogue, so it should be re-tested rather than inherited. Cousin of #34 in reverse: there a green check could not have failed; here a red check could not have *stayed* red, and nobody asked it again. |
| 50 | **A diagnostic that reports what came OUT of a transform, read as a description of what went IN** | Earthion's (`PPSA28061`) rejected 320x224 composite samples two textures and its resource table held one, so #1590 concluded the AGC front half had *recovered* only one of the two direct T# slots and re-scoped the blocker to descriptor-decoding in `agc_shader_layout.cpp`. Measured with a new instrument, the front half declares and reaches **both** (`ro[0] offset_dw=1`, `ro[1] offset_dw=9`, `samp[0] offset_dw=17` — exactly the shader's own decode); the second is removed by the degenerate-T# guard because the guest's user-data registers hold **stack residue** at those dwords. **The follow-on reading of that residue — "a `0x7f…` value is host stack, therefore an HLE returned success without writing an out-parameter" — is itself falsified, and by the same output-vs-input mistake one layer down:** prosper runs *guest* threads on host pthread stacks, so the numeric range names no owner, and the classifier added in #1590 puts a guest-module **executable** address (`eboot+0x12255`) inside the same 32 bytes. It is the guest's own uninitialised local — the guest asks a render-target group for index 1 when it created the group with one target, ignores the resulting `0x8A6C0010`, and binds the untouched stack slot — with no HLE anywhere in the chain. Read the residue as an address before attributing it to a writer. Every diagnostic in that function (`[t#]`, the `[mimg-unresolved]` table dump) described its **output**, and `build_shader_resources` has around ten `continue`s between input and output — so "never declared" and "declared, then dropped" produced byte-identical evidence while pointing at different files. This is the general shape, not a graphics detail: **when a stage both selects and transforms, an output-side instrument cannot separate a missing input from a rejected one**, and the reader will supply whichever explanation their current hypothesis prefers. The fix is mechanical and cheap — print the declared input, and make every rejection path name itself (`PROSPER_SHARPLOG=1` now does both, deduped per stage). Distinct from #39, where the instrument fell silent: this one was loud and accurate, and measured the wrong side of the transform.
