A new `[labelhist]` fault dumper formatted into `char b[512]` embedding a `char hist[512]`, then called `raw_write(2, b, (size_t)n)` with `snprintf`'s return — the **would-be** length, not the written one. A full 16-event label ring formats to ~450-500 characters, so the kernel copied up to ~35 bytes past a stack buffer, appending stack residue to evidence lines people paste into issues, or losing the line entirely to `EFAULT`. Caught in review, never shipped. **`snprintf` returns what it WANTED to write; use `strlen(buf)` for the byte count**, and treat any `off += snprintf(...)` accumulation as an out-of-bounds index until proven otherwise — the same file had a second, latent instance in a `buckets[320]` formatter. **Recording the class was not the same as closing it**: the sweep it should have triggered (#2050) found **88** live sites in `exec_image_linux.cpp` alone, so the accompanying claim that every other `raw_write` there was already length-bounded was wrong. They now go through `prosper::raw_write_fmt(fd, buf, sizeof buf, n)` (`src/host/platform/raw_syscall.hpp`), which clamps and announces the loss — **use it rather than re-deriving a clamp**. The predicted `EFAULT` was measured: against a `PROT_NONE` guard page the unclamped write delivers **0 bytes**, so the failure is a lost line, not a dirty one. The `off += snprintf(...)` half is still open as #2161, and it is the worse half — that one overflows a *write*. |
| 110 | **A review whose `file:line` citations come from the shared main checkout instead of the PR head** — the trees differ and nothing announces it | The #2091 reviewer read `command_processor.cpp` from the shared working tree at `0bf229b0` rather than `origin/master` `c3b362f6`. The file differs by **442 lines** between them, so every line number in its first review was wrong — published as verified fact in a review otherwise careful enough to hand-recompute a Fisher exact and open `defer_enabled()` at source. The substance survived re-verification, which is what makes it a trap: nothing failed, and the wrong citations would have been inherited by anyone acting on them. It also *caused* a second wrong finding — `grep -rn 'nonheap' prosper/src/` returned nothing from the stale tree where master has four hits, and that silent disagreement read as "the symbol does not exist" rather than "this is not the tree under review". **A grep that returns nothing where you expect hits is a checkout-identity signal.** CLAUDE.md already requires an own worktree for authors; it binds reviewers just as hard, and a reviewer has no build step that would otherwise expose a stale tree. Same family as traps about a cited claim reading as a verified one, but a distinct mechanism: not misreading a file, but reading the wrong copy of the right one. #2091 |
| 111 | **A wrong-but-plausible palette passes visual review, because the frame is correct in every other respect** | ArcRunner's intro cinematic was one edit away from being published as progression evidence with a green nebula and magenta thrusters. Geometry, composition, frame pacing and decode throughput were all correct (1,901 of 1,908 frames at 4K), and a green nebula in a cyberpunk-styled game reads as art direction. A reference decode of the same movie with prosper out of the path gives **orange** and **cyan**; the transform was then named as `V := U` and confirmed globally by `corr(Cb,Cr)` = 0.994/0.9997 in prosper's frames against **-0.649** in the reference — a global statistic, not a fitted patch. The generalisation is the point: **prosper's acceptance evidence is overwhelmingly visual, and hue is the property a human reviewer is least able to falsify by looking.** A colour claim needs a reference decode, not an opinion. Note this is the converse of the diagnostic-gradient trap — there a metric was fooled and the fix was to open the image; here opening the image is exactly what fails. #2094 |
| 112 | **A rendering pipeline that DISCARDS what it cannot fit, so the page looks authoritative while the evidence column is simply gone** | GitHub-Flavoured Markdown splits a table row into cells on `\|` **before** it parses inline content, so a pipe inside an inline `code span` is a cell boundary and not text. A row that ends up with more cells than the header has the excess **silently discarded** — the spec's word. Six rows of this very table rendered truncated on GitHub for months, losing their trailing text and, where they had one, their issue citation; the raw file is correct, so an editor shows nothing wrong and the diff that introduced each one looked perfect. The sharpest instance is **trap 40 — the rule about `cmd \| tail` — which was itself broken by writing that pipe unescaped**, rendering it as an 8-column row in a 3-column table and dropping most of its text. (Writing this very row unescaped is how the new check earned its keep: it failed on it immediately.) Note what made it durable: `check_numbered_table.py` reported the file `unbroken` throughout, because it validated numbering contiguity and never cell arity, so there was a green gate pointed at the right file that could not see this class at all. **A gate's silence is only evidence about the class it inspects — before citing one as coverage, ask which failures it is constitutionally unable to report.** Cousin of trap 105 (a census is silent about whatever its enumeration excluded), and of trap 53, whose own advice on this — exclude code-span pipes when counting cells — was inverted and would have suppressed the defect; it is corrected above. The gate now checks arity on every tracked `.md`. #2108. |
| 113 | **A breakpoint on a `mov reg,[mem]` reports the register's STALE value — gdb stops before the instruction runs** | Nearly produced a phantom guest bug in the Sonic Origins boot trace. `eboot+0x58751b` is `mov r14,[r12+rbx]`, the success arm of a component search; a breakpoint there reading `$r14` printed `0x0` twice, because r14 still held the `xor r14d,r14d` from the loop preamble. The next instructions store a coroutine continuation through r14, so the reading was "the search failed and the guest is about to write through a null pointer" — a plausible, self-consistent, entirely fabricated defect. The search had in fact succeeded, which the *branch* counters said all along. **Read a register at the instruction AFTER the one that writes it, or read the memory operand the instruction is about to load** — and prefer counting which branch was taken over sampling a register that a nearby instruction owns. Generalises to every `$reg` read in a gdb breakpoint script: the breakpoint address names the instruction that has NOT yet executed. #1905 |
| 114 | **An rbp-chain guest backtrace silently returns the caller's caller when the import is reached through a local wrapper** | The companion to trap 113, from the same PR, and the more expensive one: it put four wrong rows into a *measured* cross-title evidence table that was heading into a durable doc. Breaking on prosper's HLE handler and walking `[fp+8]` recovers a return address, but when the guest calls the import from a thin wrapper the frame belongs to the wrapper's caller, so the reported address sits after a `call` to an ordinary intra-module function. Nothing looks wrong — it is a genuine return address with a plausible instruction after it, which is exactly why 4 of 5 rows were misclassified and one title was recorded as "discards the return" when it has the subject title's identical `test eax,eax; jle` (a second title whose save path was silently failing, #2125). The walk is still the right tool for "who called this" across 23 dumps; it is not sufficient for "what does the call site do". **Confirm the five bytes ending at the reported address are an `e8` whose target is the `ff 25` PLT thunk for that NID**, and read the *thunk's* direct callers instead — resolve the import's GOT slot out of `JMPREL`, find the `ff 25` pointing at it, then byte-scan `PT_LOAD` for `e8` displacements that reach it. #2121, #2125 |
| 115 | **A short census window aimed by a renderer-internal ordinal, with the ordinal estimated from a *different* subsystem's counter** | `PROSPER_DUMP_PERSISTENT` and `PROSPER_PASS_LOG` open a **three-callback** window at a renderer-callback ordinal; `[gpucap]` prints a **submit** number. On one 360 s Sonic Frontiers route those are **6,560** and **26,209** — a 4x gap, and in the direction that makes an estimate overshoot. Overshooting produces **no census at all**, and that silence is byte-identical to "the census ran and every target was empty" — which, on a title being investigated *for black frames*, is the answer you already expect, so nothing about it invites a check. It cost a full 360 s arm before the ordinal was calibrated (`PROSPER_PASS_LOG`'s own out-of-window lines happen to print the counter, which is how it was finally read). Two rules. **An internal counter is neither a clock nor another subsystem's counter — calibrate it in the same configuration before aiming anything at it.** And prefer a window aimed by something externally observable: `ms:<millis>` was added to both switches for exactly this (#2110), with the same kind of origin `PROSPER_GPU_CAPTURE_AFTER_MS` uses. Failing that, make a **missed** window say it was missed, because a diagnostic that is silent when it never ran cannot be told from one that ran and found nothing. Cousin of trap 87. #1968 / #2110 |
| 116 | **A multi-kind census in which one kind is silently unarmed — and whose in-run positive control structurally cannot reveal it** | `PROSPER_PROVENANCE_ADDR=<addr>[:size]` documents itself as reporting *every recorded write overlapping one guest range, naming the writer kind*, and `GuestWriterKind` has four values. Three of them — `ComputeBuffer`, `DmaData`, `WriteData` — gate only on `writer_provenance_enabled()`, which the address watch itself makes true. The fourth, `ColorTarget`, i.e. **every draw**, is recorded only inside `diagnose_resource_provenance()`, which returns early unless the *separate* `PROSPER_PROVENANCE_DIM` is set **and parses as `WxH` with both dimensions non-zero**. An `ADDR`-only run therefore reports zero `color` lines however many draws hit the watched range, while `compute-buffer` lines stream past demonstrating that the watch is armed and printing. **That positive control is real and answers the wrong question**: "this instrument can print" is not "this instrument can print the kind you are asking about". On Sonic Frontiers the zero was **true** for the scanout addresses and **false** for another address in the same run (1,166 pass records show draws targeting it) — a correct conclusion validating a broken instrument, which is the worst case available, because being right is precisely what stops anyone re-checking. Filed as #2111. **The same class reaches you by a second mechanism — a capture's scope rather than a recorder's arming — and it is worth carrying in the same entry.** A single-submit capture **cannot establish a consumer set**: any temporally adapted quantity is written in one submit and read in another, so "X's consumer is Y" is a claim the instrument cannot make, however clean the capture. That one reached a `GRAPHICS.md` row two rows below the warning above — a luminance reduction's 2x1 output was said to be consumed by the tonemap, true of the captured frame and unknowable in general — and was caught only in review (#2118). **So before quoting an absence, or any completeness claim, write down what the instrument structurally cannot observe — arming, scope, or rate — and check that your claim is not in that set.** Then state the scope in the sentence ("in the captured frame") and make the load-bearing half of the argument the half that does not need it. #2110 / #2111 / #2118 |
| 117 | **`nz=0` on a `gpu_replay --inspect-only` resource marked `temporal-RTT-seed`, read as "this target is empty"** | That count is over the resource's **guest-memory** bytes, and a renderer-owned target is legitimately all-zero there on the persistent-GPU-target path — prosper renders into a Vulkan image, not into guest memory. The content is on the `rtt-seed` line for the same address, and on Sonic Frontiers the two disagree outright: the 4K `RGBA16F` post-chain buffer reports `nz=0` while its seed hash is **not** the all-zero hash, and a `PROSPER_DUMP_PERSISTENT` census then showed the target is **opaque black with alpha exactly 1.0** — a different finding from empty, and the one that says the guest programmed it. Read the seed, not the `nz`. **The sub-trap is in the verification you would reach for next, and it is the more dangerous half.** Those hashes come from `gpu_capture_hash`, which uses the FNV prime with a basis of **`0x14650fb0739d0383`** — the FNV-1a 64 offset basis *with a digit dropped*. A stock FNV-1a 64 therefore produces a value that can never match for **any** input (xor and odd-multiplication are both bijections mod 2^64), and the mismatch reads as "this buffer is not zero" — the exact false conclusion the hash was being used to rule out. The constant is deliberately frozen, because changing it would invalidate every recorded capture hash, bundle manifest and seed hash in the project's evidence trail, and it now says so at its definition. To decode one of these offline, reimplement `gpu_capture_hash` including its basis: the hash of *N* zero bytes, or of any repeating texel pattern, is then computable and comparable — which is how "opaque black" and "alpha 1.0" were identified byte-exactly above. Same family as trap 91. #1968 / #2110 / #2118 |
| 118 | **A sampling window measured in the guest's own sleep ticks, compared across two phases of one run** | `hle_calls --ticks N` counts ticks of `prosper::k_usleep`, **not wall time and not frames**, so the size of the window it opens depends entirely on how often the guest happens to sleep. On one Sonic Frontiers route the same `--ticks 300` recorded **1** call during the intro and **164** in the black phase — a >100x difference in call density per tick, because the movie-playback path and the post-chain path sleep at wildly different rates. The intro window was therefore **shorter than a single game frame** and could not have observed a draw of any kind; read as a control against the black-phase arm it would have "shown" that the guest stopped issuing indexed draws, when in fact it had barely been sampled. Note what makes this one hard to see: **the instrument was working exactly as designed and reported its own completeness honestly** (`entries=300/300 window=complete`) — it is the *comparison* that was invalid, and no field in the output is wrong. **So size an `hle_calls` window by something the subject defines, not by ticks: check `agc_dcb_set_flip` (or the title's flip handler) in the result to see how many frames you actually captured, and treat any window with a flip count of 0 as void rather than negative.** Then state the frame count beside every figure, so two windows are only ever compared per-frame. The same caution applies to any `--ticks`-style budget on a clock the subject controls. Cousin of trap 92, and the mirror of it: there the window's hidden variable belonged to the **host** (page-cache residency), here it belongs to the **subject** — which is worse, because a subject that changes behaviour between phases changes your window size in exactly the phases you are trying to compare. #2023 |
| 119 | **A build killed mid-`ar` leaves a TRUNCATED static library, and it resurfaces as a link error in a different target — reading exactly like a code defect** | Killing a contended `cmake --build` (load average 17, six of my own compiler processes, another lane's PR merging under me) is ordinary housekeeping, and it is safe at almost every point except one: partway through writing `libprosper_core.a`. `ar` leaves a valid-looking file that is short, nothing checks it, and the next build happily reuses it. The failure then appears **later, in an unrelated target**, as `ld.bfd: libprosper_core.a: error adding symbols: no more archived files` while linking `test_rdna2_to_spirv` — a *linker* error naming a *test* whose own sources compiled cleanly, so the natural first hypothesis is that the change under test broke something. It did not; the archive was corrupt before the edit. The tell is that the message names the **archive**, not a symbol: a genuine link failure says `undefined reference to …`, while `no more archived files` means the container itself is malformed. **Delete the archive and rebuild — do not debug the target it surfaced in**, and prefer letting a contended build finish over killing it, since the window where a kill corrupts state is precisely the archive step every target downstream depends on. Same family as the traps where the apparatus rather than the subject produced the defect, with the added twist that here the apparatus damage was self-inflicted and silent. **A kill is not the only trigger, and the archive is not the only casualty: TWO `cmake --build` runs on one build dir truncate each other's `.o` files the same way.** The easy way to get there without meaning to is the harness itself — a build backgrounded on timeout is still running, so the "retry" you launch in the foreground is a second concurrent build over the same objects. It presents as `file format not recognized` while linking, names a **different target each rebuild** (the giveaway: a real defect is deterministic), and survives deleting the one object the error names, because the next run corrupts another. Confirm with `pgrep -f 'cmake --build'` before retrying, wait for the background job, then rebuild once — sequentially. Rediscovered at full cost during #2190. #2123 |
| 120 | **A diagnostic whose pattern appears in the diagnostic itself — it reports its own existence as the subject's** | Two instruments, one shape; the recognition cue is identical, so they are one row rather than two pieces of lore competing for the same slot. **`git diff origin/master HEAD` manufactures the very defect trap 41 screens for.** Several lanes share one `.git`, so another lane's fetch advances `origin/master` under you and the diff then reports *their* additions as *your* deletions, in files you never opened — it fired twice in one session and cost a second look each time. *Tell:* `merge-base == HEAD`. *Fix:* rebase first so `merge-base == origin/master`, then `git merge-tree --write-tree origin/master HEAD`, and run the deletion check only after that. **`pgrep -f '<pattern>'` manufactures the process it hunts for**, because the pattern sits in the hunting command's own command line: `pgrep -f 'gh pr checks 2135'` matches the shell running it, and a loop grepping `/proc/*/cmdline` for a marker matches its own `grep` argument — the classic `ps \| grep` self-match, which the process-counting note already records in its over-counting direction. *Tell:* the count never reaches zero no matter what you kill, and every scan returns a **different, always-fresh** pid. *Fix:* the self-excluding bracket form (`chec[k]s`), or `pgrep -x` on the executable name — **and anchor the pattern**, because excluding yourself is only half of it. The bracket form above, run as `pgrep -f 'gh pr chec[k]s 213'`, correctly excluded itself and was **still wrong**: it matched another lane's watcher on PR **2131**, because `213` is a prefix of it. The detector was honest about itself and still wrong about the subject — a PR number is a prefix of ten others. What it nearly cost is why the row is worth reading. A lane told to "kill your remaining waiter loops" found six candidate processes and **none of them were its own** — the orchestrator's heartbeat, the orchestrator's persistent PR monitor, and four other lanes' shells — while the "surviving parent" that kept respawning the match was the lane's **own `claude --resume` session**. `TaskStop` had worked on the first call; there was never anything to kill. The three plausible targets were therefore the orchestrator's heartbeat, the orchestrator's monitor, and the lane itself, and that monitor is specifically the mechanism that catches an **approve/reject split on one SHA** — the exact failure that created the task — so **tidying up would have removed the safeguard against a repeat**. Net terminated: zero, only because ownership was verified before acting rather than the instruction followed as given. Note the instruction was the orchestrator's and it was wrong: a kill order issued without a way to tell one lane's processes from another's, on a machine running eight lanes. **The defence is to make the detector unable to describe itself** — exclude self and ancestry, match on a field the query cannot contain, or ask what the command would report if the subject did not exist. Inverse of trap 110, where a grep returning **nothing** where you expect hits is a checkout-identity signal; here a grep returning **something** is the same signal wearing the opposite sign, and the false positive is the more dangerous of the two because it names a remedy. #2135 |
| 121 | **A recorded trap did not stop its own re-derivation one day later — when the remedy is a discipline the reader must remember, assume it will be skipped and mechanise it instead** | This row is deliberately NOT a second description of trap 116's mechanism; 116 owns that, and #2111 owns the defect. It records what happened *after* both existed. Chasing #2132 I reached for the guest-write history, set `PROSPER_WRITER_PROVENANCE=1` — the switch documented as the explicit "retain everything" request — and got **183 negatives it could not have falsified**, because the `ColorTarget` recorder was behind the unrelated `PROSPER_PROVENANCE_DIM`. That is trap 116 exactly, already written down, already filed, in a document I had read. I re-derived it from scratch and spent arms doing so, then compounded it by inferring "the history was empty" from a zero that a *dim-gated consumer* had produced by never running — it held **42** events, not 0. **The general form is worse than the instance: a diagnostic split across a producer and a consumer can be half-armed, and the half that prints is the half that looks healthy.** A watch that streams `compute-buffer` lines while an entire writer class is unreachable reads as a working instrument, and the in-run positive control confirms the wrong proposition — 116's point. What 121 adds is the conclusion drawn from 116 failing to protect the next reader: **116's remedy is "before quoting an absence, write down what the instrument structurally cannot observe", and that is a discipline, so it decays.** The durable fix is to make the negative carry its own falsifiability — the diagnostic now prints `(history=8589 recorded: color=39 compute-buffer=4744 dma-data=8127 write-data=1111)` beside every "no recorded GPU writer", and the address-watch banner names the armed recorders, so `color=0` is visible in the line itself rather than reconstructable from a second run and a code read. **Two limits a count still cannot express, and both had to be written down explicitly**: a SKIPPED dispatch records nothing however the switches are set, and a kind can read non-zero while having DISCARDED the write that matters (`DmaData`/`WriteData` drop writes below 256 bytes / 64 dwords unless full retention is on) — an armed-but-not-recording case no summary reveals, and worst over exactly the few-dword windows these queries are used for. **So: when you find a trap, ask whether a future reader could hit it while doing everything right; if yes, the row is necessary but not sufficient, and the instrument owes them a self-report.** Also check the tracker for the instrument you are about to trust, not only for the code you are about to change — #2111 had the gate, the file:line and the fix written down before I started. Cousin of 116 (same mechanism, opposite lesson: 116 is how to read the instrument, 121 is that reading it correctly cannot be the only defence). #2111 / #2132 / #2143 |
| 122 | **A fuzz whose generator could not construct the case it reported absent — and a positive control that passed, certifying the model was LIVE rather than that it could express the case** | Reviewing #2150 I had to decide whether completing a truncated tracker snapshot widened `win_protect`'s accept set anywhere beyond the single case the PR body disclosed. Rather than argue it from the diff, I modelled the whole validation walk from source — both slicers, the reservation check, and the region walk including its `next_slice_base` scan — and fuzzed old-against-new over **225,502** random configurations of host regions, tracker entries and span placement, classifying every accept/refuse difference by the branch that produced it. It reported exactly two deltas and **confirmed the PR body precisely.** Suspecting nothing, I ran a positive control anyway — and **it passed**: the population constructed the hole-then-committed-slice precondition shape **41,206** times and produced **17,659** narrowings, exactly the evidence I would cite to prove the discriminator works, against **0** widenings. All of it was void. I had placed the model's module aperture flush against the **top** of its address space, so `region_end - 1` was always inside the aperture and the second widening class — the one in the `MEM_COMMIT` arm, where a completed vector shrinks `next_slice_base` and therefore shrinks the range put to the aperture test — was **structurally inexpressible**. Moving the aperture into the interior and re-running (330,179 configurations) surfaced **1,277** instances of the missing class immediately. **What separates this from traps 64, 116 and 121 is that the lever moved.** Those rows are about instruments that are unarmed, half-armed or scoped too narrowly, and in every one of them the discriminator is *silent*; mine was loud, and the noise is what made the zero look validated rather than void. **A live lever certifies that the model runs, and nothing whatever about whether its domain contains the case under test.** The control failed to catch it for a reason worth stating on its own: **a positive control drawn from the same generator as the null it validates tests the discriminator, never the domain** — it can only ever confirm that the machinery fires on cases the generator can build, which is the assumption in question. *Tell:* a categorical zero for one class sitting beside a large non-zero for another **in the same run**; real behaviour is rarely that tidy, so read a clean zero first as a bound on the generator and only then as a property of the subject. *Concrete, reusable form:* **a synthetic model of an address-space walk is an instrument, and where you put its apertures, page boundaries and region edges decides which deltas it can express at all** — flush-against-an-edge geometry silently deletes every case that has to straddle that edge, and edges are exactly what these walks are about. *Fix:* **before believing a fuzz's clean zero, construct one positive instance of that class BY HAND, outside the generator, and confirm the model can represent it at all.** A generator that cannot produce the defect reports its absence in the same voice it would use to report a real one. Two general points, in decreasing obviousness: a null from a model is a statement about the model until the model is shown to span the case; and **"my instrument agrees with the claim I am checking" is the reading most in need of an adversarial pass, not least** — this null would have confirmed the PR body exactly, which is the whole reason it deserved a second look. #2150 / #2144 |
| 123 | **A test assertion with no lever in the code under test — it passed on whether glibc happened to grow a block in place, and what decided that was the HLE registry's own size** | `test_libc_alloc` asserted that `realloc` preserves a `memalign` block's **256-byte** alignment. **Nothing in prosper can make that pass or fail:** on POSIX `guest_realloc_portable` is `return realloc(p, size)` (`hle_libc.cpp:183`), and `h_realloc` is handed a pointer and a size and **never an alignment**, so the arm measured glibc's placement rather than any behaviour of ours. Instrumenting both arms pins the mechanism exactly. Unperturbed, `memalign` returns an address and `realloc` returns **the same address** — the block grew **in place**, which keeps the original alignment for free. Adding **four inert, fabricated, never-imported NID registrations** to `register_service_hle()` on otherwise unmodified master puts the block somewhere the growth cannot be satisfied in place, `realloc` **relocates** it, and the new address is 16- but not 256-aligned. The contents half passes in both arms; only the alignment half moves. So **any PR that registers a NID could turn this file red without touching it**, with a message naming the allocator — and it sits directly under the 904-NID census, where every fix registers NIDs. *Recognition cue:* **an assertion whose subject function was never told the property being asserted.** *Second trap inside the first, which is why this row is long:* three synthetic in-test perturbations built to demonstrate the sensitivity — 65 pre-arm allocation counts, 9 blocker sizes placed between the `memalign` and the `realloc`, and 41 counts injected before `register_builtin_hle()` — **all returned a clean zero**: 0 relocations and 0 failures across 115 configurations. Every one is **void, not negative.** None of them moves the `memalign` block at all (it sits at page offset +256 in all 115), because the registry's real effect is an `unordered_map` **bucket-array rehash** — one large reallocation at an unpredictable point — which uniform small `malloc`s never reproduce. Read on their own they would have "proved" the test sound. This is trap 122's shape at a different injection point, and what saved it is that rule's applicable half: **the hand-constructed positive instance existed before the model**, so the model's zero could be recognised as void instead of believed. Do not hand-tune such a model until it agrees — that fits the instrument to the answer. *Fix:* assert what the contract actually promises. C17 7.22.3p1 gives contents plus `alignof(max_align_t)`, and 6.2.8p3 makes 256 an **extended** alignment that nothing promises to carry across a resize; the platform agrees rather than merely permitting it, `libSceLibcInternal` exporting **`reallocalign`** (`OGybVuPAhAY`) as a function separate from `realloc` (`Y7aJ1uydPMo`) precisely because a two-argument resize cannot know an alignment. #2165, #2185 |
| 124 | **An env var that names the right concept and controls a different store — so a "per-lane isolated" run silently inherited a peer's save data, and the guest took a different ROUTE** | `PROSPER_SAVEDATA_DIR` sets the save-data-**memory** store (`hle_service.cpp`, `savemem_path()`; the line numbers this row used to cite were stale by the time anyone followed them, so it names the function instead). The mounted `/savedata0` area — what a title actually reads and writes — is `PROSPER_SAVE0` (`hle_file.cpp`, `save0_base()`), which at the time was **`/tmp/prosper-savedata0` by default, one directory shared by every lane and every title on the box.** Four Sonic Frontiers investigation arms set `PROSPER_SAVEDATA_DIR` to a private path and were read as isolated; every one of them mounted the shared default. That is not merely untidy — Frontiers writes `option/`, `arcade/` and `challenge/` there on first boot and **probes for a PS4 save to import only after reading them back**, so whether the boot reaches the wall's real cause depends on what some earlier run left on disk. A `PROSPER_PROGRESS_UNIMPL` census run on the empty-save route reported "3 distinct unimplemented NIDs, 5 calls, none polled" and was quoted on the issue as evidence that no missing NID was involved; the populated-save route reports a **fourth called 1,319 times**, and it was the entire blocker (#2023). *Recognition cue:* **a diagnostic env var whose name matches your intent better than it matches its own `getenv`** — grep the variable, not the concept, and check the default it falls back to. *Half of this is now fixed and half is not:* since #2734 both roots are namespaced by title id and both variables are ROOTS rather than final directories (`docs/SAVE_DATA_LAYOUT.md`), so a run can no longer inherit **another title's** saves — but the two variables are still different stores, and a run still inherits **the same title's** earlier saves from a shared root, so the trap's actual lesson is untouched. *Corollary the save case makes concrete:* on a shared box, persistent guest state is an **input** to the route. Record which save area an arm used the way you record the binary's revision, and give a first-boot arm its own directory. #2023 |
| 125 | **A straight-line interpreter reporting register values from a path the wave cannot be on — every field in the line honest, the conclusion a phantom that survived four investigations** | prosper's scalar const-fold walks a shader's decoded instruction stream in order and models no control flow. On *Sonic Racing: CrossWorlds*' NGG vertex stage that produced `[dyntrace] SMEM op=0x0 load sdst=s20 sbase=s18 base=0x0` followed by `addr 0x0 unreadable` — read, correctly, as *"the guest's constant buffer holds a null bindless-table pointer"*. It does not. The dword is `1.0f`, ordinary uniform data; the fold had executed `s_buffer_load_dwordx16 s[16:31], s[8:11], 0x60` at `pc=45` and `pc=113`, which sit inside the block that the `s_cbranch_vccz` at `pc=21` **skips** whenever the block reading `s[18:19]` at `pc=173` runs, and those loads overwrote the stage's two seeded direct pointers. **Four lanes hunted the writer** — a GPU-side writer (8,589 recorded writes, none overlapping), a skipped compute dispatch (8/8 programs, 138 ranges, zero coverage), a stale/wrong buffer (#305's comparison came back equal), and finally "the null is legitimate and we should fold that arm" — and each negative was *correct*, because the question was wrong: nothing was ever supposed to write it. *What makes this one hard:* the trace has no field that is false, no rate limit, no unarmed recorder, and no missing positive control — the instrument reported exactly what the interpreter did, and the interpreter is what was wrong. It is trap 122's shape one level down: there a generator could not *express* the case; here an interpreter could not *represent* the control flow, so the value it printed was outside the domain the reader assumed. *Tell:* a chain whose inputs are self-consistent and whose conclusion requires the guest to have done something senseless — a shader dereferencing a float constant as an address is a statement about the model, not the guest. *Fix, and it generalises:* **when a fold prints a register value, ask which basic block it came from before asking who wrote it.** Disassemble the stage and check whether the writing instruction dominates the reading one — the branch encodings settle it in minutes and no amount of memory-watching can. Validate a hand disassembly by requiring it to land a known instruction on the pc the emulator itself reports (here the MUBUF at dword 211); a linear `llvm-mc` decode of the same bytes drifted 26 dwords and would have put every branch target in the wrong place. **And the sting in the tail, from this row's own fix being rejected:** the CFG proof written to repair it ran over the fold's *compacted* instruction stream — which drops ordinary VALU/EXP while preserving PCs — so "the previous instruction is an unconditional branch" was really "the previous *retained* instruction is", and a dropped block between them was invisible. **A CFG proof is only as good as the stream it walks; if that stream was filtered for another purpose, adjacency is not adjacency.** Check physical adjacency (`prev.pc + prev.len_dwords == target`) rather than list position, and count predecessor edges in *both* directions — a tally that skips backward branches is not a tally. Two dead conditions in the first version (testing for `s_endpgm` on a stream that stops at `s_endpgm`) were the tell that the author had the wrong stream in mind. #2132 / #2202 |
| 126 | **A verification build whose `GAME_DUMP` does not resolve silently runs a SMALLER SUITE, and the only tell is the test COUNT** | `CMakeLists.txt:148` sets `HAVE_GAME_DUMP` from `EXISTS ${GAME_DUMP}/eboot.bin`, and `GAME_DUMP` defaults to `${CMAKE_SOURCE_DIR}/../PPSA24651-app0` — which resolves **inside the worktree**, where no dump lives. A worktree-local build therefore skips every dump-backed test, and `ctest` reports **212 passed** where a configured build reports **217**. Both are `exit 0`; both print `100% tests passed`, because it is a percentage of what ran. Measured 2026-08-06: an orchestrator quoted "212/212 green" as the merge gate for a whole session, including for a change to shared descriptor resolution — the absent tests being `boot_reaches_first_syscall`, `game_compute_exec`, `game_compute_exec_no_adaptive_storage_result` and `real_shader_render`, i.e. precisely the ones that execute real recompiled shaders. *Why it survives:* CMake **does** announce it — `Game dump not found at … skipping dump-backed tests` — but once, at configure time, in a log nobody re-reads, and never again on the fifty builds that follow. *Recognition cue:* **a peer's test count HIGHER than yours is evidence about your build, not about their staleness.** That same session twice diagnosed a lane's larger number as a stale base: correct once (a branch genuinely behind) and wrong once, where the lane's 217 was right and the orchestrator's 212 was the defect. *Fix:* set `PROSPER_GAME_ROOT` (CMake now derives `GAME_DUMP` from it, and the configure step WARNs rather than whispers when the dump does not resolve — #2893), or pass `-DGAME_DUMP=<DUMP_ROOT>/PPSA24651-app0` explicitly in any worktree build, and quote the COUNT, never the exit code alone — the count is the only falsifiable half, which is why #2188 required it. #2202 |
| 127 | **Two frontends returned OPPOSITE verdicts on whether a change fixes a title — and the obvious explanation was excluded by the runs' own arithmetic** | A candidate fix for #1226 (`PROSPER_POST_SUBMIT_VISIBILITY=1`) was scored on ArcRunner's established `boot_trace` route: **0 of 3 faulted**, 788-838 delivered video frames against a default arm's 29-32, and a 260 s run rendered the title screen. The same head, same dump, same env, under `tools/screenshot`: **2 of 2 faulted**, at 35 and 42 delivered video frames. Had either frontend been the only one used, the session's whole conclusion would have flipped. *The tempting explanation is wrong, and checking it is what makes this a trap rather than a note:* `screenshot` reads back a 4K frame per sample, so "the readback perturbs a timing-sensitive title" is instrument trap 104 exactly and fits the shape — except both runs died **before their first sample interval elapsed** (`--seconds 8` and `--seconds 20`, deaths at ~9 s), so no readback had happened. Whatever differs is in the frontends' setup, not their sampling. *Recognition cue:* **a frontend is an apparatus, and a progression claim inherits it.** `tools/screenshot` is what the project uses for evidence and `boot_trace` is what lanes iterate on, so the two are routinely treated as interchangeable views of one run; they are not, and no row above covers a case where the *frontend* is the variable. *Fix:* name the frontend in every survival or progression claim, and when a lever's verdict matters, run it under both before writing it down — it is one extra arm and it is the difference between a fix and a phantom. Filed as #2217; the ArcRunner evidence in `ARCRUNNER_STATUS.md` § 2026-08-07 states its frontend for exactly this reason. #2217 / #2219 |
| 128 | **One diagnostic tag over two roles — a fast path's ROUTING DECLINE logged identically to a terminal failure, so counting the lines renamed a whole frontier** | *Sonic Racing: CrossWorlds*' eight skipped compute programs were recorded on #2013 and in the title's status doc as failing on **control flow**: "21 `[cfg-recompile-reject]` and 26 `[compute-struct-reject]` lines per boot, on `s_cbranch_vccz`/`vccnz` and a forward `s_cbranch_execz`, one of them a `backward else` shape (#590)". Not one of them fails on a branch. **Seven of the eight stop on a single arithmetic, SDWA or MIMG instruction** (`v_mov_b32_sdwa … sext(…)` ×3, `v_rndne_f16_sdwa`, `v_ceil_f16_sdwa`, `image_atomic_add dim:2D_ARRAY`, and an `s_flbit_i32_b64` whose opcode is implemented and whose *scalar source is untracked*); the eighth is 12,916 dwords of zero and is not a shader. Settled by A/B rather than by re-reading the log: implementing two SDWA families and nothing else restored **four of the seven**, leaving their branch shapes untouched. *The mechanism, stated precisely because a loose version of it is also wrong:* `[compute-struct-reject]` is emitted from **21 sites in two different roles** — **14** inside `detect_forward_ifs`, the narrow structurizer, where it is a **routing decline** (the recompiler then runs the CFG dispatcher, which on these programs accepted the same shapes and carried on to the arithmetic op), and **7** inside the structured emitter, where it is terminal and *is* the cause. So "read it never as a reject" would itself mislead. Worse, 12 of the 26 lines are `structured emission stopped next-pc=N next-if=…`, which is **not a control-flow complaint at all**: `next-pc` is the blocking instruction — matching the `[cfg-recompile-reject]` pc exactly — and `next-if=4294967295` states in the diagnostic's own fields that no `if` is pending. The answer was in the output the whole time, in the field nobody read. *And the routing reading has a precondition:* a `detect_forward_ifs` decline is only a routing decision when the dispatcher is actually selected (`cfg_dispatch_safe` and either `exact_compute_wave_cfg` or `cfg_branches > 2`); otherwise `cf_rejected` clears the loop list and the branch genuinely does block. *Recognition cue:* **two reject diagnostics from one program describing different causes — ask which emitter ran LAST, not which line explains more.** The convincing one here was the wrong one for three compounding reasons: more numerous (26 vs 21), more specific (branch pcs, targets, merge points), and it matched an issue that already existed (#590), so it read as corroboration rather than noise. *Generalisation:* wherever a translator has a narrow fast path and a general fallback, the fast path's refusal is a routing decision logged at the same severity as a real failure — and if the two share a tag, no amount of grepping separates them. Attribute a reject to a **program** before attributing it to a **cause**: `PROSPER_SHADER_DUMP` plus a `pc → (fmt, op, words)` listing gives a 1:1 mapping, and `tools/re/disasm_words.py` names the instruction from its bytes. *What it cost:* the lane was briefed that the 8 skipped compute programs fail on control flow and that #590 was the frontier; the actual fix was two SDWA decode admissions, and it halved the population (8 → 4) in one session. #2013 |
| 129 | **A test suite's own arm count was a stale snapshot, so "my two new arms did not run" was read off an instrument that structurally could not have counted them — and the wrong conclusion sent the investigation back to the start** | `test_check_build_revision` prints `N arms, M skipped`, and that count exists precisely so the suite's coverage is falsifiable from a log. `coverage` was a **local assigned partway down `main()`**, so every arm added below it went uncounted. Chasing the red Windows MinGW job I added two arms just above the summary print — which is exactly where a new arm gets added, and exactly where the assignment sat — and CI reported the **identical** `29 arms, 5 skipped` before and after. Read as *"the change did not reach the runner"*, which is the natural reading and was false: the arms had run and **passed**. It took an arithmetic check to see it — 29 reported + 2 unreported + 5 skipped is the 36 the file defines, and locally the same head printed `33 arms, 3 skipped`, also 36. *Why this belongs here rather than in the PR that caused it:* the failing suite and the counting suite were the same file, so the instrument under suspicion was reporting on itself, and the number that would have exposed the staleness is the number that was stale. *The compounding half is the reusable part:* it then happened a **second time, in the commit fixing the first**, because the remedy was "move the assignment down" — a position, and positions rot the moment anyone appends. Trap 121's lesson at a smaller scale: **when a stale reading comes from WHERE a value is computed, moving it is not a fix; make it uncomputable-in-advance.** `coverage` is a function now, so it reads the counters at the moment the summary asks, which is the only moment the number means anything. *Recognition cue:* **an unchanged count after adding arms is evidence about the counter first and about the runner second** — and more generally, a metric that a code change should have moved and did not is a claim about the metric until the metric is shown to be live. *What it cost:* a correct fix (the `--show-cdup` change) was believed ineffective and a whole hypothesis was retracted before the real cause — MSYS2 brace-expanding `HEAD^{commit}` in the command line before git parsed it — was found, and it was found by making the failure print what the tool and git **actually did** rather than by reasoning harder. #2232 |
| 130 | **A diagnostic added INSIDE an unpartitioned region bills its own cost to the subject — and the more expensive the instrument, the more significant the phantom finding looks** | `res_buffer_ms` had no exhaustive partition: `acquire` and `copy` were added as **two candidate mechanisms**, and `render_runner.h` said so in as many words. A `PROSPER_BUFFER_CHURN` diagnostic was then placed just before the upload `memcpy` but **outside** `phase_copy`'s scope, to FNV-1a each payload and answer whether the bytes were content-unchanged. It hashed ~450 GiB per run, and every millisecond of that landed in the one part of the buffer branch nothing measured. The resulting stderr window read `buffer=332.08 (acquire=1.50 copy=25.82)` on Blue Prince gameplay — **304.76 ms/submit, 60.9% of a 500 ms frame, attributed to the renderer** — and was written up and posted as the dominant term, with the recommendation to stop optimising the copy path. Single-variable A/B on one binary: `PROSPER_BUFFER_CHURN` set → `buffer=360.15 other=+324.72`; unset → `buffer=40.41 other=+3.23`, with `copy` 27.82 vs 31.33 and `acquire`, `create` and `hash` unmoved. The whole 321 ms delta is the instrument. *Why it survived scrutiny:* the enclosing line **self-checks to +0.00** — `texture + buffer + descriptor + other == resources` exactly — so the partition looked audited at the level anyone would audit it, and the defect was one level below, where no partition existed to fail. This is the scale-invariance finding from #2245 with a number on it: an identity constrains the level it is written at and says nothing about the level beneath. *Recognition cue:* **an unmeasured region does not read as unmeasured — it reads as somebody else's cost.** So a large remainder inside a bucket you just instrumented is a claim about your instrument until an arm without it says otherwise, and that arm costs one environment variable. *Fix, and it is structural rather than a rule to remember:* partition the region exhaustively and print the remainder **signed**, so `other=+324.72` is visibly anomalous against a healthy `other=+3.23` on the very first run. The generalisation is trap 116's, inverted: there a silent instrument under-reported, here a loud one over-reported, and both were invisible because the quantity that would have exposed them was the quantity nobody had partitioned. *What it cost:* one confident cross-lane finding published and retracted the same day, plus a nominated prime suspect (`create_transient_storage_buffer_upload`) that the partition then showed **never fires at all** — `create=0.00` in every window of two runs. *The limit, worth stating with it:* an exhaustive partition protects **only the region it partitions**. `pass_control`, `readback` and `prelude` are still single numbers with nothing below them, so a diagnostic placed inside one of those gets exactly the free ride this one got. **An added measurement belongs inside a named bucket, or it becomes the residual.** #2246 |
| 131 | **A claim conditional on a platform NO REVIEWER RUNS gets single-author scrutiny however many agents read it — and the count of agents is what makes it feel checked** | Two agents review each other's PRs here, one on Linux/AMD and one on Windows. `h_reallocf` (#2203) shipped a comment saying `guest_realloc_portable(p, 0)` *"already frees p and returns NULL on **both platforms**"*. The tree builds for **three**, and macOS is the one that differs: `realloc(p, 0)` there returns a valid minimum-size block and frees nothing, which C17 7.22.3.5 permits — all three conform. **Three passes could not have caught it.** The author wrote the comment; the reviewer read it and *restated it in a review as though it were a check*, with the borrowed authority of two adjacent claims that reviewer had genuinely verified from source (the NID against `../PS5-3.20_Libs/libSceLibcInternal.c:17893`, and all five sibling rwlock handlers); the regression test then encoded the same two-platform assumption as an assertion. Every one of the three was reasoning from the same two platforms, so agreement between them carried no independent information. macOS CI falsified it within the hour. *What makes this a trap rather than an oversight:* **the shipped code was never wrong.** `if (!r && p && a1)` keys on the RESULT rather than on an assumption about the platform, so it is correct under all three without a branch — established by diffing the implementation separately from the comments and finding the function body byte-identical across both heads. The defect lived entirely in a comment and an assertion, and the assertion is the artefact the next reader would have trusted. A wrong comment beside right code is not inert; it propagates into tests. *Recognition cue, and it is cheap:* **a platform count stated in prose — "both platforms", "on either OS" — in a repository that builds for more than that number.** One `grep` over the CI matrix settles it, and neither agent ran it. *The structural point:* cross-agent review adds real independence only where the reviewers' environments differ. Here it covers Linux and Windows genuinely well and macOS **not at all**, so the effective review depth for a macOS-conditional claim is one, while the process reports two. Know which platform-conditional claims your reviewer pool cannot see, and treat those as unreviewed regardless of how many approvals they carry. #2203 / #2247 |
| 132 | **A share of a bucket is a function of the RENDER PHASE, and the misleading run is the one whose residual looks clean** | `build_R`'s descriptor-reflection leaf was measured twice on *Blue Prince* (`PPSA25009`), same binary, same instrument, same host, minutes apart. A default launch reaching the **title screen** at **10.2 draws/submit** reported `build_resources 4.69 [build_R=4.64 (texture=4.42 buffer=0.04 reflect=0.14 other=+0.03)]` — reflection **3%** of `build_R`, remainder `+0.03`, a bucket that reads as fully explained and not worth touching. The fresh-save route to **Day One gameplay** at **2,103 draws/submit** reported `build_resources 66.46 [build_R=57.16 (texture=10.92 buffer=11.19 reflect=30.37 other=+4.68)]` — the same leaf at **53%** of `build_R` and 9.3% of the frame. A **200x** difference in the answer, and #2256 would have been closed as not-worth-fixing on the first. *What makes it a trap rather than an obvious sampling error:* the misleading run is the more **convincing-looking** one. Its residual is near zero, so every audit a reader can perform on it passes, and a partition that self-checks to `+0.03` is exactly what trap 130 taught people to look for. Cleanliness of a partition says nothing about representativeness of the workload it was measured on. *Why the phase moves it so far:* per-draw fixed costs scale with the draw count while per-target and per-frame costs do not, so every leaf's SHARE re-ranks between phases — the two runs above are not the same measurement with more samples, they are different workloads. *Recognition cue:* **a percentage quoted for a renderer bucket with no draw count beside it is unusable.** This is the charter's decoded-draw-census rule applied to timing rather than to draws, and the same day produced a second instance across hosts: a lifetime total at `draws=351.1` (8.48 ms) placed beside a window sample at `draws=2106.8` (48.95 ms) as though the two were comparable — shares transfer between aggregations, totals do not. *Fix:* print the draw count on the same line as the partition, name the phase and the route in any quoted share, and reach real gameplay before believing a leaf is small. A title screen is not a cheap approximation of gameplay; for this bucket it is a different subject. #2256 / #2259 |
| 133 | **A pixel guard and a unit invariant are each structurally blind where the other sees — and both were satisfied while something was wrong** | Routing index buffers through the shared arena (#2253/#2258) was checked two ways, and each instrument passed over the other's defect. **Direction one: pixels right, invariant violated.** `blue-prince-hall` returned OK — 145,178 colours, 96 frames — while `test_multidraw_render`'s `pool.misses == before + 1` was red. That assertion is named *"two logical uploads populate one persistent host-buffer arena"*, but the equality also required the arena to be **empty on entry**, which is a property of the FIXTURE and not of the renderer; indexed draws now leave a partially-used arena, so the two uploads fit inside it and allocated nothing. Property satisfied, proxy violated — and had the author shipped on the guard alone, as the snapshot-is-a-release-gate rule invites, nothing would have said so. **Direction two, and it is the sharper one: invariant satisfied where it cannot discriminate.** The suite's indexed-draw arms (`px_three_indexed == px_three` and every draw in `test_indexed_render`) each render a *single* draw per pass, so each gets a fresh arena and binds at **offset 0** — the one value that stays correct if the bind offset is dropped from the code entirely. Those arms read as covering the index path and validate it only at the point where the bug they would catch is invisible. Confirmed by mutation: hardcoding `0` at both `vkCmdBindIndexBuffer` sites left the whole suite green until an arm with **two** indexed draws in one pass was added, whose second slice is non-zero by construction. *Recognition cue:* **ask which VALUE of the parameter under test the existing arms actually take.** A test that exercises a feature at its identity element — offset 0, scale 1, empty list, single element — is testing that the feature exists, not that it is computed. *And the payload half:* the second draw's indices must DIFFER from the first's, or both slices render the same picture and the arm is void whatever offset it binds. *Fix:* pair them deliberately — a content guard for what pixels must look like, a unit invariant for what the machinery must account for, and for each, name the value that would make it blind. Neither subsumes the other, and a green pair is not a green product. #2258 |
| 134 | **`ld: cannot open dependency file …/link.d` means the build TREE is gone, not that the link failed** | Mid-build, every remaining link began failing with `/usr/bin/ld.bfd: cannot open dependency file CMakeFiles/test_cond_clock.dir/link.d: No such file or directory`, repeated across a dozen unrelated targets. That reads as a link defect in the change under test, and the natural first move is to debug the code — especially when the change touches `prosper_core` and therefore relinks every test binary, so *many targets broke at once* looks like a real consequence of it. It was neither: a batch `git worktree remove --force` sweep had deleted the worktree **while a build was running inside it**. The compiler processes survived on open file descriptors, so only newly-created paths failed — the build kept *progressing* and died only at each link step, which is why it looked like a link problem rather than a missing tree. *Tells, cheapest first:* the message names **a path the linker wanted to create**, not a symbol — a real link failure says `undefined reference to …` and a truncated archive says `no more archived files` (trap 119); "cannot open X: No such file or directory" for a file **the build itself owns** is an environment failure. It hits **every** target, including ones whose sources were untouched. And `ls -d <worktree>/build` settles it in one command. *Rule:* before debugging a link error, confirm the build directory still exists, then check `df -h` — an exhausted filesystem produces the same class of message. *The other half is for whoever is sweeping:* a worktree with a running build looks idle from outside, so check for processes rooted in the tree before removing it. Nothing was lost here only because the work was already committed and pushed — that is the lane's discipline covering the sweep, not the sweep being safe. #2179 |
| 135 | **An `until ! pgrep -f '<pattern>'` wait loop MATCHES ITS OWN SHELL and waits forever** | The obvious way to serialise against another lane's build — `until ! pgrep -f 'cmake --build'; do sleep 30; done` — never terminates, because `pgrep -f` matches **full command lines** and the waiting shell's own command line contains the literal string it is searching for. Confirmed by reading the matches: `pgrep -af 'cmake --build'` returned three of the author's own waiters, each keeping the others alive. *Why it survives:* the failure is silent and **looks exactly like a slow build**, which on a contended box with another lane running `-j10` is entirely plausible, so it outlives several rounds of "just wait a bit longer". It also **stacks** — each new waiter makes the condition permanently harder to satisfy, so the more you retry the worse it gets. *Rules:* never wait on a pattern that appears in the waiting command itself — match the real process (`pgrep -x cmake`), a PID captured when the build started, or a sentinel file the build writes on exit; and if a wait loop has outlived any plausible build, run `pgrep -af <pattern>` and **read the matches** before concluding the build is slow. This is the wait-loop form of the well-known `ps \| grep` self-match, and it is less recognised precisely because the loop hides the evidence. *Simplest fix of all:* do not serialise — separate worktrees have separate build directories and contend only for CPU. #2179. **Third consequence, and the one that accumulates: the self-match can disable the loop's own ABORT GUARD.** A waiter written defensively as `until <condition>; do if ! pgrep -f "lane-cobra" >/dev/null; then echo "BUILD PROC GONE"; break; fi; sleep 10; done` contains `lane-cobra` in its own command line, so the `pgrep` always matches **itself**, the `break` can never fire, and the guard added specifically to stop the loop running forever is what guarantees that it does. Measured 2026-09-05: one such waiter survived **2.4 hours** with its cwd pointing at a worktree that had been deleted under it, and three more were found in another lane the same night. Note the three consequences are distinct and the third is the expensive one: a plain self-match **stalls** the loop (above), a self-match in a kill loop **terminates the wrong process** — including, in one case, the very shell running the kill — and a self-match in an abort guard makes the loop **immortal**. Immortal waiters are what later make a `pgrep -f` census lie about whether the box is free, so they corrupt the next lane's scheduling rather than only their own. *Corollary worth stating in full, because two of the three instances were by an author who already knew the rule:* **a pattern smart enough to exclude today's watchers will match tomorrow's — including the watcher doing the excluding.** Match a PID captured at spawn, or `wait` on it, or a sentinel file; never a name. **And bound every waiter with an iteration cap regardless**, because a bounded loop with a broken guard still terminates. #3348 |
| 136 | **A `--draw-steps` ladder from an EXTRACTED single submit is not evidence about where a frame loses content** | `gpu_replay --bundle-extract-submit N` then `--draw-steps` on the collapsed CrossWorlds frame produced a tidy, plausible ladder: a downsample pyramid to 60x34, back up to `1920x1080` carrying `visible-px=14003`, then a final `3840x2160` operation at `visible-px=0`. That reads as a located defect — "the 4K composite drops the 1080p content" — specific enough to file against an operation. The **same procedure on the KNOWN-GOOD bundle** (the one replaying to a correct SEGA logo, busiest submit, 29 operations) reports `nonzero-px=0 visible-px=0` for **every** operation. So by this metric the broken frame outscores the working one, which is impossible: an extracted submit replayed in isolation does not carry the frame's accumulated state (bound targets, prior contents, resources produced by earlier submits), so its ladder describes a fragment out of context. Whether a submit shows activity tracks how self-contained it happens to be, not whether the title renders. **The danger is that it fails in the FLATTERING direction** — the output does not look empty or broken, it looks *informative*, complete with a villain operation, so nothing prompts a second look. Cousin of the same-source-control warning in `CLAUDE.md`, but inverted: there the control was corrupt, here the *experimental* arm was seductive and only a hand-built known-good control exposed it. Rule: a `--draw-steps` claim about lost content REQUIRES the same ladder from a frame known to render, printed beside it. #2013. |
| 137 | **A positive control at 236 calls validates nothing about detecting 9 — match the control's RATE to the rate of the thing you are looking for** | A CrossWorlds `hle_calls` census over 400-tick windows reported **no `libSceVideodec2` handler at all**, and it carried a positive control — `g_vo_flipstatus`, present in both windows — so the null looked earned. It became a `## Ruled out` row: *"the post-logo wall is not a stalled CRI Mana movie"*. **The wall was a movie.** `PROSPER_SVCLOG=1` over the same phase shows `sceVideodec2CreateDecoder` plus eight `sceVideodec2Decode` calls, and implementing VP9 decode carries the title through it (#2281). The control was firing at **236 calls**; the traffic being looked for is **nine** one-shot calls at the moment the movie starts. A sampling histogram over 400 ticks exists to show steady-state work, and nine scattered events are exactly what it discards — so the control established that the instrument fires on a high-rate handler and could not, even in principle, establish that it would catch a nine-call event. This is the CLAUDE.md same-source-control warning specialised to **rate**: the control and the target must be comparable in *how often they occur*, not merely drawn from a different source. A cheap discriminator exists — name a handler known to fire a handful of times and confirm the census retains it. **The cost is what makes this worth a row**: the false row sat directly over the answer for two days, and a wrongly-closed hypothesis stops people looking while wearing evidence. #2013, #2281. |
| 138 | **`PROSPER_DUMP_ATLAS` filters its own input three ways, so an empty dump directory is not evidence of anything** | It writes a texture only when `tw <= 2048 && th <= 1024` **and** the resource decodes to RGBA8; it writes **24-bit BMPs, dropping alpha entirely**; and enabling it **switches the renderer onto the CPU RTT-copy path**, so the run it observes is not the run being debugged. Each filter is individually defensible and none is visible in the output. The general rule: **a diagnostic that silently narrows its own input set converts "I looked and saw nothing" into "I did not look"**, and the two are indistinguishable from the artifacts. An empty output directory from this tool means nothing at all, and any alpha-dependent conclusion drawn from its BMPs is void by construction. **The cost is what earns the row**: this was hit during #1946, whose defect *was* an alpha-blending one — the atlas turned out crisp and correct, and the real cause was the whole title rendering with blending disabled because prosper offered the RT0 blend key only for SDK >= 13 while the title asks for 12. The instrument most likely to be reached for was the one least able to answer. #1946, #2031, #2059. |
| 139 | **`import` and `xdotool` cannot see or drive a Vulkan window under Xwayland, and a black `import` reads exactly like a black frame** | `import` returns **pure black**; `xdotool key --window` uses `XSendEvent`, which **never reaches SDL**. So a scripted route that appears to deliver no input and a title that appears to render nothing are *the same observation* through these tools. On this project that is the single most consequential false negative available, because a large share of open investigations are about black frames — the failure mode and the measurement artifact are the same image. Use the frontend's own paths: the `screenshot` tool or F9 in the windowed frontend for capture, and the frontend's own input path for routing — never an X11 screen-grabber, never `XSendEvent`. Found while diagnosing #1946, where it made routed verification unreliable for as long as it went unnoticed. #1946, #2031, #2059. |
| 140 | **A gdb hardware watchpoint on guest memory can report ZERO writes while the watched byte demonstrably changes — no error, no failed arm** | Tracing Sonic Origins (#1905, #2093), `watch *(unsigned char *)(mgr + 0x11C)` armed successfully and the script ran to completion recording **zero writes**. In the *same run*, software breakpoints on the two candidate stores (`eboot+0x55b428`, `eboot+0x55b480`) caught three writes to that exact address, and independent reads showed the byte going `0 -> 1 -> 0 -> 1 -> 2 -> 0`. Whether it failed to arm in hardware, armed on the wrong thread, or something else was never determined — **and that is the point: nothing in the output distinguishes those from "no writes occurred"**, which is what the zero reads as. A lane with only the watchpoint would have concluded the byte was never written and gone hunting a stale-read or aliasing explanation that does not exist. **Any gdb `watch` zero on guest memory is VOID, not negative, unless the same run shows the watchpoint firing on a write you caused or independently observed.** Distinct from the `PROSPER_HWWATCH` / `perf_event_paranoid` dispute — different mechanism, and that one reports an errno. Adjacent to trap 113 (a breakpoint on `mov reg,[mem]` reports the STALE register, because gdb stops before the instruction runs): same debugger, same investigation, opposite direction — 113 gives a confident wrong VALUE, this gives a confident wrong ABSENCE. #1905, #2093, #2100. |
| 141 | **`gpu_replay`'s `operations=A/B` is a replay SETTING over a DECODE count — it can never report a skipped operation, and it sits one line away from the number that can** | Every submit prints `[gpureplay] bundle-submit=N operations=A/B output_bytes=… hash=…`, and on a *Sonic Racing: CrossWorlds* frame every one of the 34 submits read `26/26`, `27/27`, `7/7`. That is not a realized-over-attempted ratio. `gpu_replay.cpp:1701` prints **`operation_limit` and `replay.operations.size()`** — how many operations the replay was told to run, over how many were decoded — so `A < B` happens only when the *user* passed `--through-operation`, and a dispatch that the backend declines leaves both numbers untouched. **There is no run in which this field reports a failure.** Read as a success ratio it says every operation realized, which was false on that exact frame: the capture records **four** declined dispatches per frame (three `shader-recompile`, one `descriptor-contract`), and the full-screen one among them is #2265. *What makes it worse than an ordinary misread:* the replay's log **also** stays silent — the `[compute] skip …` lines are emitted by the live executor and are not re-emitted on replay, and the one skip line that did appear (`layered image deferred to #657`, trap-free and real) works as a false positive control: it proves the compute path runs and can log, which is exactly the assumption a reader needs, and it is unrelated to the declines in question. Trap 122's shape — a *loud* control laundering a null. *Fix, and it is one flag:* realization failures are recorded **in the capture at capture time**, not derived at replay, so extract the submit and ask for them — `--bundle-extract-submit N out.prgcap` then `--inspect-only out.prgcap`, which prints `failure[i] … reason=<descriptor-contract\|shader-recompile\|…>` or the explicit `failure-diagnostics: none`. That line is falsifiable in both directions; `operations=A/B` is falsifiable in neither. *Recognition cue:* **an `A/B` pair in which you have never seen `A < B` is not a ratio you have confirmed, it is a ratio you have never tested** — find the `fprintf` and read both arguments before quoting it. #2265 |
| 142 | **A title WAITING FOR INPUT presents a perfectly healthy renderer, so every graphics diagnostic agrees the subject is fine while nothing progresses — and none of them can say why** | *Sonic Racing: CrossWorlds* sat at rung 1 for months as *"the SEGA logo renders; the composite then goes uniform"*. In one day this lane ruled out, with evidence, the compute inventory, a polled unimplemented NID, PlayGo content availability, a stale cached SPIR-V, and the layered image atomic (#2265 — implemented, merged, and restoring it moved **not one pixel**). Every one of those negatives was correct and every one was beside the point. **The title was waiting for a button.** With a pad route it walks SEGA -> Unreal Engine -> CRIWARE -> licensor -> auto-save notice -> **title screen** -> player-profile menu, all correct at 3840x2160 (#2358, #2360). *What makes this a trap rather than an oversight:* the evidence of health is real and complete. 0 dropped pipelines, 0 unresolved descriptors, 0 `[exec-recompile-reject]`, a clean unimplemented-NID census (12 distinct, top one 127 benign `pthread_setschedparam` calls), and a presenting loop at ~10.8 flips/s with `draws_cum` climbing ~425 every 5 s. Nothing is broken, nothing is stalled, and **a renderer drawing one correct static screen forever is indistinguishable from a renderer that cannot draw anything else.** Every instrument in the graphics toolbox measures the pipeline; none of them has an opinion about whether the guest's state machine is advancing. *Two mechanisms nobody would guess, both of which cost an arm here:* **(a) a held button is not a press** — holding Cross from frame 0 advanced nothing over 170 s *with delivery confirmed from the guest's own read* (`pad_read_state call#512 connected=1 buttons=0x4000`), because the guest needs a neutral->pressed transition; and **(b) the route's own shape can be the wall** — the first committed route ended in a long hold and stopped at the licensor screen, a limit the route created rather than one the title had. *Cheap discriminator, and it is title-independent:* `PROSPER_PADLOG=1` answers **does the guest poll the pad at all**, which is one run and settles whether input is even in play. If it polls, drive it with `PROSPER_PAD_SCRIPT` **pulses** before spending another session on the renderer. *Related but not the same:* trap 101 is a diagnostic whose *window* was empty; this is a subject that is genuinely, correctly idle. #2013 / #1895 / #2358 / #2360 |
| 143 | **`PROSPER_RENDER_TIMING`'s per-submit line mixes WALL-CLOCK totals with THREAD-SUMMED component times, unmarked — so `component / total` from that line is not a share, and three of its components are individually larger than the total** | One *Blue Prince* window, verbatim: `total=464.05 realize_draws=230.55 tables=918.47 shader_lookup=532.45 ... table_parts: metadata=516.13 fold=243.83 ... parallel: threads=8.0 wall=228.63`. `tables`, `shader_lookup` and `metadata` each **exceed** `total`, because draw realization runs on 8 threads and those components are summed across them while `total` is wall. Nothing in the line says which is which; `wall=228.63` is the only field labelled. *Why it is a trap and not an obvious inconsistency:* the impossible values sit at the END of a long line, and the ones a reader reaches first (`realize_draws`, `backend`) genuinely ARE wall and genuinely DO compose to `total` — so the first arithmetic you try works, and confirms the habit. *Cost, measured:* this produced **six** wrong shares from one lane in a single day — `buffer copy` at 2.4%, `gpu-device` at 43% 'of the frame', `draw_setup` at 61% 'of the frame' (it is 61% of the backend, ~21% of the frame), a frontend share derived by subtracting two accountings that do not compose, and the headline **30-39 fps** that was a menu present-rate — and it is the same shape as the other lane's retracted *'54% of the thread is copying a hash map'*. Two agents, same unmarked line. *Recognition cue, and it is free:* **before dividing, check that the components you are dividing sum to no more than the total** — here they sum to over 2,000 against 464, which is visible in one glance and settles it. *What IS safe from that line:* `realize_draws` (~230.55) and `backend` (~233.45) are both wall and do compose; `submits per presented frame` measured 1.04, so per-submit figures are per-frame on this title. Everything else needs its denominator established before it is quoted. #2215 / #2276 |
| 144 | **`PROSPER_PAD_SCRIPT` is recorded as METADATA in two files that never inject anything, and the real consumer is SILENT by default** | The natural check after a scripted run -- `grep pad <log>` -- returns **nothing on a route that is firing correctly**. `grep -rn PROSPER_PAD_SCRIPT prosper/src` shows it in `gpu_capture.cpp:4829` and `gpu_timeline.cpp:205,1081`, where it is stored into capture metadata and drives no input at all; the actual consumer is `src/input/pad.cpp`, which logs only under **`PROSPER_PAD_SCRIPT_LOG=1`**. So the two greps most likely to be reached for both point at the wrong thing. **The failure is that it looks like a located defect rather than a dead instrument**: a GTA V Story-Mode run reached ~2880 frames and crashed with zero `[pad]` lines, which reads exactly as "the route never fired, so this crash is at the menu" -- a specific, plausible, wrong conclusion that would have sent the crash investigation to the wrong screen entirely. With the flag on: 31 events, Cross at frames 600/800/1000, route confirmed, crash genuinely downstream. **Rule: set `PROSPER_PAD_SCRIPT_LOG=1` on every scripted-input run as a matter of course**, and never infer route failure from absent `[pad]` output. Same family as trap 138 -- an instrument whose silence is indistinguishable from a negative result -- and a cousin of the vkval build-directory trap, where the tool answers truthfully about something other than what you asked. #2396. |
| 145 | **A clean zero needs THREE checks, not one: was the instrument armed, is it measuring the object you think, and can the space it searches express the case** | The general form behind trap 144 and several others, written after both lanes hit it from different directions in one day. **(1) ARMED.** Most `PROSPER_*` diagnostics are `getenv`-gated, so an un-set variable makes "not instrumented" and "not happening" print identically -- zero. Measured instances: `PROSPER_MEMLOG` gating a placeholder-repair log made a working fix read as *"the repair never fired on master"*; `PROSPER_DBG` gating the reject census made a Windows run report `smem-reject: 0, mubuf-raw-unresolved: 0`, which reads as *"this platform does not have the descriptor-resolution defect"* -- with the gate open it is 98 and 488, and the false version would have sent the other lane hunting a second cause that does not exist. **The cheapest check is a CONTROL LINE you know should be present** (`grep -c memhle` over the whole run); a grep filter that omits the pattern you later count is the same failure one layer out. **(2) MEASURING THE RIGHT OBJECT.** An instrument can be fully armed, fully truthful, and answering about something else: `vk_validation_scan.py` reads the **build directory**, not the checkout, so a branch switch without a rebuild reports the other branch's binaries. No control line catches this -- the fix is checking what the tool READS. **The nastiest measured instance is a `git grep` PATH FILTER that matches nothing, because it returns ZERO rather than an error and there is no artefact to distrust** (2026-08-10): run from `<worktree>/prosper`, `git grep -c '<string>' -- prosper/src` resolves the filter to `prosper/prosper/src`, matches no files, and reports 0 for every pattern. It produced `recompile-reject 0`, `unresolved-operand 0`, `mubuf-unknown 0` and three more -- one step from reporting to the other lane that diagnostics the charter itself documents *do not exist*. Unlike the vkval case there is no stale build, nothing to rebuild, and the command is well-formed; the only surviving defence is **grep for something you KNOW is present, in the same command** (`nullpage` and `pad-script`, whose `fprintf` had been read an hour earlier, also returned 0 -- impossible, so the method was broken rather than the codebase). **The same day produced the INVERSE failure, and it is why the control must be chosen carefully rather than merely present:** a control grepping `VkDescriptorSetLayout` under `prosper/src` returned 0 and read as another broken grep, but the path was fine and the *control* was wrong -- those symbols live in `prosper/tests/fixtures/render_runner.h` and `prosper/frontends/shared/live/live_compute.cpp`, and `prosper/src` genuinely does not contain them. A control must be a string you know is present **in the path you are searching**; otherwise it cries wolf, and a real search and a broken one both come back 0. #2412. **(3) ABLE TO EXPRESS THE CASE.** The trap-122 shape: a positive control drawn from the same generator as the null passes while the class under test is structurally inexpressible in it, so the zero is real and meaningless. The three are independent and fail in that order of subtlety. Confirm all three before a zero becomes a `## Ruled out` row -- a wrong falsification is the most expensive artefact in this repository, because it stops people looking while wearing evidence. #2396, #2412, #2424.  |
| 146 | **A coverage/PEAK figure taken from an ANIMATED sequence measures WHEN the capture landed, not whether the frame is correct — and the instrument is armed, aimed correctly, and still lying** | Verifying master after ten merges, a rung-6 control (*The Messenger*, PPSA24651) scored **coverage 20.39%** against a **9.28%** reference from the same route, same trigger delay, same build family. That reads as a doubling — a regression or an improvement, either of which would have been reported. **Opening the image settled it in one look: two different frames of the same animated intro cutscene**, the wide blue-lit ruins shot versus the darker ninja scene. Sprites, flame and text all correct in both. *Why this is a distinct trap rather than an instance of 145:* trap 145's three checks all pass here.
