The instrument was **armed** (a real 6.2 MB frame was written), it measured the **right object** (that title's actual scanout), and the space could **express** the case. The number was accurate; what was wrong was the assumption that a scalar over a moving picture is comparable between runs. **Cost so far: five instances in one session across both lanes** — three on this side (this control, a CB_DISABLE A/B where 2.473% vs 0.180% turned out to be gameplay versus a prologue title card, and a phase-mismatched GTA V pair), and two on the Windows side, which published 84.2% / 47.5% / 56.8% as a trend before recognising them as run-to-run video position. *Rule:* **for any title phase that moves — cutscene, FMV, attract loop, camera pan — a coverage/PEAK figure is only comparable against a frame you have looked at.** Either open both images, or anchor the comparison to a state that does not animate (a paused menu, a HUD element, a title card held on screen). A single scalar per run is a valid *content* signal only where the content is static. *Recognition cue:* if you cannot say which frame of the sequence each number came from, you are comparing timestamps. #2412 |
| 147 | **A diagnostic or repair path can be STRUCTURALLY STARVED — something upstream consumes the trigger it waits for, so it never fires on the very path it documents** | Distinct from 145's first check (was the instrument armed?) because these are armed, correct, and still silent: the event they key on is eaten before it arrives. **Two instances in one day, from opposite directions.** *(a) A diagnostic that cannot fire on the path it describes.* The compute materialization log in `gpu_executor.cpp` was gated on `g_dyntrace_force`, which is set **only** around the `PROSPER_DYNTRACE_FAIL` replay — and that replay calls `resolve_dynamic_fetch` directly, never re-running the materialization loop the line lives in. So the diagnostic printed what the fold *recovered* and never what the materializer *did with it*, for the one stage where those can differ; the graphics equivalent prints during the real build and had no gap, which is exactly why a recovered-vs-materialized join was free on that side and impossible on this one. Un-gating it produced 1676/1676 `-> materialize` and redirected the whole GTA V investigation off textures. *(b) A repair path whose fault was already answered.* Windows resets the user `%fs` base on kernel transitions and prosper re-applies it **only when a `%fs` access faults** — but `PROSPER_NULL_PAGE`'s low-read emulator answered that exact fault first (`decode_low_read_dest` treated `0x64`/`0x65` as ignorable prefixes), zeroing `rax` and stepping over it. `guest_fs_reapply()` waited for a fault that never reached it; *Dragon Quest VII* died on the next instruction with `addr=0xfffffffffffffff0`, pointing at the guest rather than at us. **Recognition cue:** when a diagnostic or handler is silent, do not only ask *was it enabled* — ask **can this code path reach it at all, and does anything upstream consume the event first**. The tell in both cases was a *count*: exactly one null-page emulation in a whole boot whose rip was the instruction after the one it swallowed; and a log line that appears for one stage and never for its sibling. **The two were found by DIFFERENT routes, and both belong in the cue.** (a) came from comparing a stage against its own sibling — the graphics fail-replay prints during the real build and the compute one does not — which is cheap wherever a sibling exists. (b) came from reading the **ORDER of handlers**: `try_emulate_null_read` (`exec_image_win.cpp:1001`) runs before the `%fs` re-apply (`:1027`), so the question is not *is my handler correct* but **what else could answer this fault first**. *Do not reach for the sibling comparison on (b)'s shape:* a Linux/Windows sibling for the null page does exist (Linux maps a real zero page, Windows emulates the read), and conflating those two is exactly what produced #2112's WRONG original diagnosis — the sibling here points at the thing that misled. #2412 / #2112 / #2447 |
| 148 | **Reasoning about the WRONG DEFINITION of a symbol that exists twice behind a platform guard — the code compiles, links, tests green, and is DEAD on the platform you are debugging** | Distinct from a stale comment (text that no longer matches code) and from trap 147 (a live path starved of its trigger): here the code you read is real, correct, and *not the code that runs*. Measured 2026-08-10 on #2450. `hle_kernel_mem.cpp` splits at `#if defined(__linux__) \|\| defined(__APPLE__) … #else … #endif` and defines **`k_batch_map` twice** (`:2281` POSIX, `:5448` Windows). Reading the first hit and reasoning carefully about its control flow gave *"ENOMEM is reachable from exactly two ops, MAP_DIRECT and MAP_FLEXIBLE; everything else is excluded"* — **true of the POSIX definition and false of the Windows one**, where `UNMAP` (`ok = !start \|\| win_unmap(...)`) and `PROTECT` (`ok = win_protect(...)`) can also fail. The conclusion was published on a Windows-only bug, excluding the two ops that only fail on Windows. **More care would not have helped; only a different question would** — the reasoning was sound and aimed at the wrong object. The same session added a diagnostic to that first definition: it compiled, linked and passed **179/180** while being dead code on the platform under test. *Detection is mechanical and takes one command* — after building, ask whether your string is in the object file, **with a positive control** so a broken grep cannot masquerade as absent code: `strings <build>/…/<tu>.cpp.obj \| grep -c '<your string>'` returned **0** while the control returned 2. An object that recompiles cleanly and does not contain your string is code the preprocessor removed. *Recognition cue:* before trusting any control-flow conclusion on a platform-specific bug, count the definitions — more than one means you must establish which you read. Helpers named `win_*`/`posix_*` far from your reading position are the ambient tell. #2450, #2454. |
| 149 | **Traps document the failure; REVIEW supplies the vantage — and this table's own length is an argument that reading it is enough, which the data contradicts** | Measured 2026-08-10: of **six** defects found in one day between the two lanes, **five required a second reader, the sixth was caught by an instrument that could not hide its own failure, and ZERO were caught by an author re-reading their own code.** Each author had personally documented the failure class **hours earlier, in this file**. Linux: a capability whose member comment asserted an inheritance that **nothing implemented and no struct field could carry**, so the flag stayed false on the shared path while reading as handled (#2458); and a starved control **inside the probe written to detect starvation** (trap 147) -- the periodic report sat after a first-sight early return, so it could fire only on a repeat observation. Windows: a `git grep` pathspec that matched no files and returned zero for six diagnostics that demonstrably exist (trap 145 check 2); control-flow reasoning about `k_batch_map` aimed at the **wrong one of two definitions** behind a platform guard (trap 148); and the same starvation defect again, in the census built to fix an attribution error -- a 5,000-observation threshold below which it printed nothing, its silence indistinguishable from never having run. **The sixth is the instructive exception**: a summary emitted from a **destructor**, in a probe whose own comment explained that a summary must not depend on an exit path. Its author caught it **unreviewed** -- because the run printed per-slot lines and **no denominator**, so the OUTPUT asked *"can this line be reached?"* on the author&#39;s behalf. That is the second route to the vantage and the only one available to a lone author: **not re-reading, which never worked once here, but observing an instrument built so its own failure is visible in what it prints.** **The mechanism is not that writing a trap down fails to inoculate — priming was never the missing ingredient.** The destructor bug was written by someone who had *just* reasoned about exit paths, i.e. maximally primed. **The author cannot occupy the position the check requires:** the author asks *"does this report the totals?"* while the reader asks *"can this line be reached?"*; and on the wrong-definition case more care would have produced a more confident wrong answer, not a right one. Only a different **question** helps, and a second reader is the only reliable source of one. *Rule:* **review is not a gate proportional to diff size.** Keep it mandatory on work of this class even when the diff is three lines, the author is confident, and the author has just written the trap row for the exact defect they are about to commit. *Corollary for this table:* a growing trap list quietly implies that reading it is sufficient protection. It is not, and never was — every row here records a failure a checklist did not prevent. #2447, #2454, #2456, #2458, #2465. |
| 150 | **An OBSERVER that perturbs a TIMING-DEPENDENT subject — and whose own health check reports success while the conclusion inverts** | Distinct from 145 (the instrument is armed, truthful and aimed correctly) and from 146 (the number is accurate; here the *subject* changed). Measured 2026-08-10 by the Linux lane on *Dragon Quest VII*: the documented routed capture holds its title screen (mean luminance 173-220 across frames 33-44), and **the identical command with `PROSPER_GFXLOG=1` added goes pure black** (mean 0.00, frames 33-39) on a clean master. `PROSPER_GFXLOG` emits ~160,000 lines over that route (159,619 `[render] item` counted) and slows it enough to shift a **time-dependent pad script** out of alignment with the sampled frames. **The lethal part is that the route's own health check passes:** `[pad-script] elapsed=30.049 frame=479 buttons=cross` confirms every press was delivered on schedule, so `PROSPER_PAD_SCRIPT_LOG` — the instrument that exists to catch a route failure — reports success while the game is in a different state by the time each screenshot fires. Both halves healthy, composite wrong, and the black frames are indistinguishable from a real regression. It nearly produced a phantom title-screen regression report; the only thing that stopped it was noticing `GFXLOG` was in the command and not in the documented recipe. The same effect is visible unrouted as a magnitude rather than a phase error: the Windows lane measured a **50% stall rate** under `GFXLOG`+`DBG` against **1 in 11** without, and could only label it an apparatus figure until this measurement named the mechanism. *Rule:* **every routed capture in this project is timing-dependent, so adding a logging variable to a scripted route invalidates the baseline — re-establish it under the same variable, or compare only runs whose variable sets match exactly.** *Recognition cue:* a capture that disagrees with a known-good baseline while every delivery and liveness check passes. Ask what you added to the command before asking what changed in the code. #2448, #2459, #2465, #2468. |
| 151 | **A defect class that NO check in the suite can see — invisible to the assertion on one platform and to the entire job on the other** | Measured 2026-08-10 across both lanes on the descriptor-array lift. A cached `VkPipeline` built under an arity-1 layout was replayed with an arity-3 layout bound (`VUID-vkCmdDrawIndexed-None-08600`, *"descriptorCount 3 doesn't match 1"*). **Two independent blind spots hid it.** (1) *The assertion cannot see it:* the stale pipeline still draws a correct green quad, so every pixel arm passed — confirmed by mutation, where reverting the fix returns the VUID byte-identical **while the test still passes**. Author verification, author review and independent review all went green on a spec-invalid draw. (2) *The job does not exist:* `test_descriptor_array_render` is triple-gated at `CMakeLists.txt` — `if(TARGET prosper_core)` `:208` -> `if(UNIX)` `:1283` -> `if(Vulkan_FOUND)` `:1416` — so the whole Vulkan-execution render suite (`multidraw_render`, `indexed_render`, `texture_sample_render` too) is **never configured on Windows**, and this class is invisible to Windows CI rather than merely unrun locally. `UNIX` is the operative gate: Vulkan is found in every Windows build cache, so **installing the SDK on Windows changes nothing** — the obvious fix attempt fails silently. **So: `ctest` green is not evidence about spec validity, on any platform. Run `tools/vkval/vk_validation_scan.py` deliberately for any change touching descriptor counts, layouts or pipeline keys** — it is the only check in the repo that sees this class, and the only reason this defect was ever found. Third leg, recorded because it is the reflex: a second **driver** could not have discriminated it either, since `08600` is a **usage** error raised by the validation layer rather than driver behaviour, so it is driver-independent close to by construction. *Recognition cue:* a green suite on a subsystem whose correctness is defined by a spec rather than by output — ask which job would fail if the code were wrong, and whether that job runs on your platform at all. #2471, #2476. |
| 152 | **A `file:line` citation invalidated by someone else's merge between writing and landing — and the stale number lands on a PLAUSIBLE neighbour** | Measured 2026-08-10, twice in one session, both times in a file **neither the author nor the reviewer touched**. A doc cited `render_runner.h:4469`-`:4478`, `:4484` and `:4558`; an unrelated PR then merged 155 lines into that file, 18 of them above the region, and every number was silently off by +18. Nobody was careless — the author cited correctly, the reviewer verified correctly, and a **third** change invalidated both between review and merge. **The `Docs` CI gate structurally cannot catch this**: it validates table structure and numbering, never whether a `file:line` resolves to what the prose claims. **What makes it dangerous is where the stale numbers land.** They do not point at nothing — `:4484` landed on `available_… \|= kFragmentSubgroupShuffle`, subgroup-shaped and adjacent, so a reader spot-checking it sees something plausible and moves on **having verified nothing**. *A citation that lands somewhere obviously wrong is safer than one that lands somewhere convincing.* This is trap 151 one level up: there the gate could not see the defect class, here the gate cannot see whether its own evidence still resolves. **So: re-resolve every `file:line` against the head you are MERGING INTO, not the head you wrote against** — find the construct by name (`grep -n '<the exact code>'`) and read the line back, never add the offset, because an offset is right only if the insertion was contiguous and above every citation. *Recognition cue:* any PR citing a line in a file it does not modify, merging more than an hour after the citation was written, into a branch that has moved. #2459, #2471. |
| 153 | **A mutation arm that perturbs a DIFFERENT SITE than the fix tests the assertions, not the code -- and the giveaway is that it FAILS, loudly and correctly, on the wrong object** | The stronger form of the positive-control rule in `CLAUDE.md`: there, a control drawn from the same source as its null tests the discriminator rather than the domain; here the control is aimed at the wrong *object*, and **it responds**, which is what launders it. An inert arm invites suspicion; an arm that goes red on demand closes the question. Measured 2026-08-10 on #2487 (descriptor-array arity). The fix changed a **layout call site**, `lb[i].descriptorCount = r.written_descriptor_count()`; the mutation arm perturbed the **accessor** `written_descriptor_count()`, which the arm's own assertions call directly. Both mutations were run, and they diverge:</br></br>`accessor  -> descriptor_arity()   FAIL x2   -- detected, arm goes red`</br>`call site -> descriptor_arity()   PASS      -- NOT detected, 232/232 green`</br></br>**So the arm proved it could fail, while the fix's own site was never tested at all** -- and the accessor mutation *responding* is precisely what concealed that. *The correct mutation was measured only after review pushed back, and it inverted the author's published reasoning:* with the arm corrected, reverting **only the call site** gave `exit 139`, the suite at 0%, and vkval reporting **pool exhaustion plus `dstSet` NULL twice**. **The mechanism is the part neither lane predicted, and it is why the wrong arm looked adequate to both:** the pool reserves 1 sampler, the mutated layout declares 3, `vkAllocateDescriptorSets` asks for 3 from a pool of 1 and returns no set, `vkUpdateDescriptorSets` is handed `VK_NULL_HANDLE`, and the process segfaults. **The class is visible through the POOL, not through pixels** -- so the claim that ctest could not see it and only the validation layer could was itself wrong, and was retracted on measurement. Reconciles with #2471 rather than contradicting it: there the pool arm genuinely was unverifiable, because RADV over-allocates and absorbs the mismatch; a pool can be over-allocated into, but it cannot invent capacity for a descriptor **type** it was never sized for. *Rule:* **state the mutation as a diff against the FIX HUNK and revert exactly that -- if you cannot point at the line your arm perturbs and show it is a line the PR changed, the arm is testing something else.** *Recognition cue:* the arm mutates a helper the assertions call rather than the site the diff touches. **A red arm is not evidence the arm is aimed correctly**, and that is the whole row: the reassuring outcome and the diagnostic outcome are the same colour. #2487, #2471, #2476. |
| 154 | **Trap 148 catches REVIEWERS harder than authors, because the wrong definition APPEARS TO CONFIRM the claim being checked** | Trap 148 records reasoning about the wrong one of two definitions behind a platform guard. This is its reviewer-side form, and it is worse: an author reading the wrong copy writes something false, but a reviewer reading the wrong copy **manufactures corroboration** -- and a verified claim is exactly the artefact this project treats as settled. False negatives get re-checked; false confirmations do not. Measured 2026-08-10 on #2486, reviewing a Windows-only diagnostic in `hle_kernel_mem.cpp`. The reviewer's first look landed on the **POSIX** copy's `[batchmap-fail]` -- pre-PR `:2373`, against the Windows copy at pre-PR `:5582` (post-PR `:5611`) -- carrying its own `if (!ret) ret = 0x8002000Cull;` three lines below. **Verified against the pre-PR tree rather than asserted, and reproducibly** (`5e7ff0ab`, the parent of the #2486 merge): the two eleven-line logging blocks at `:2368-2378` and `:5577-5587` are **byte-identical**, not merely equivalent: `cmp` reports no difference, which reproduces under any framing, and for a fixed constant `sha256[0:16]=79df2ea630d95434` each **over the 11 lines with no trailing newline** (the obvious `sed -n '2368,2378p' > f` appends one and yields `99a4b5e4e6f6d18c` instead -- stated because a verification constant needing an unstated convention is one step from being a stale figure, and the reader's conclusion would be "the hash is wrong" rather than "my framing differs") -- and the single difference anywhere near them was a trailing comment (`// ENOMEM on a failed map`) on the POSIX side's following line. So checking a Windows claim against the POSIX half would have produced a confident `APPROVED` citing a real line containing the real construct, and would have survived any spot-check that did not ask which side of `#else @2704` the line sat on. Only that question caught it. **Compounding tell:** trap 148 itself cites `:5448` for the Windows copy, which has since drifted to `:5490` -- the row documenting this hazard already carries a stale line number for it (trap 152), and this row's first draft repeated the error by pairing a pre-PR line number with a post-PR one. The review survived that drift because the *claim* was stated as **"`GetLastError`/`SetLastError` appear nowhere before the platform `#else`"** -- a construct-and-boundary claim, checkable by counting (28 lines total, **0** before `:2704`) and immune to every line shifting. *Rule:* **when reviewing a platform-specific change, establish the boundary line first, then locate every copy of the construct relative to it -- and state findings as constructs and counts rather than line numbers.** *Recognition cue:* you found what you were looking for on the first grep hit, in a file with a platform `#if`. First hit and correct hit are different things, and a duplicated file makes the first hit *plausible*, which is the trap. #2486, #2450, #2454. |
| 155 | **A check whose two failure directions are ASYMMETRIC, automated anyway -- and the harmless direction is the one you get to observe** | The general shape, which is not specific to any repository: when a check can fail toward *blocking something valid* or toward *admitting something invalid*, the first announces itself and the second is silent by construction. Observing the safe direction misfire therefore tells you nothing about the rate of the dangerous one, and it *feels* like evidence the check works, because you caught it. Instance, measured 2026-08-10, and the reason `CLAUDE.md` forbids automating this particular read: every agent here operates as the repository owner, GitHub refuses `--approve` on your own PR, so the review **state** is always `COMMENTED` and `reviewDecision` stays empty -- for approvals and rejections alike. The verdict lives only in the body, which invites a substring match. A `jq` filter testing whether a body *contained* `REJECTED` reported an **APPROVED** review as a rejection, because the prose discussed an earlier rejection. **That direction fails loudly and stops a merge.** Its mirror does not: a genuinely `REJECTED` body containing the word `APPROVED` -- *"this is not yet APPROVED"*, *"I will move to APPROVED once..."*, a quoted prior verdict -- reports as an approval. **That is the #2026 shape exactly**, where a daemon counting `reviews > 0` merged a PR with two blocking findings outstanding (revert: #2044). The filter had been running all session and happened never to bite that way. Reading the **leading token** rather than searching the body narrows the window; it does not close it, since a review may open by restating the verdict it supersedes. *Rule:* **a machine may LIST candidates and flag staleness; a reader decides the verdict.** The genuinely mechanical checks are the ones with no prose in them -- is this review attached to the current head (`commit_id` versus head SHA), and if not does `git diff <reviewed-sha> <head>` show a content change. *Recognition cue:* any predicate of the form "body contains `<VERDICT>`" -- ask what it does with a sentence mentioning the other verdict, and which of the two answers you would ever find out about. #2026, #2044, #2465. |
| 156 | **PROXIMITY READ AS IDENTITY: an address near a known structure, or a diagnostic line near a known header, attributed to the program you are investigating.** Three times in one session on one program (GTA V `0x413ce6000`, 2026-08-12). (a) A `user-sgprs=11` grep matched an `s10=0x0e` dispatch of `0x413dd6e00` five lines from a ce6000 header and nearly became evidence about ce6000. (b) A 24-byte constant buffer at ce6000's own `s[0:1]` was read as a bound on its access, when its `pc=` field named `0x413dc6700`. (c) Eighteen read-before-write resources were attributed to ce6000 because they sat at `s0 + 0x114` for three observed ce6000 base pointers -- and ce6000 appears in that dependency graph **zero** times, necessarily, because every one of its dispatches failed and failed operations never become graph nodes. **Two programs sharing an arena is ordinary; an address inside a structure does not name the program that reads it.** (a) and (b) were caught by hand; (c) was caught only by adding `producer-program=`/`writer-program=` to `gpu_replay --graph`, which falsified the claim of the person who wrote the tool. Attribute from a program-tagged field, never from an address or a neighbouring line -- and when the tool has no such field, that is the thing to fix first. | #2481, #2516 |
| 157 | **A rate-limited diagnostic quoted as a census — it records a program's WORST moment, not its state** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. `[compute] skip unsupported program 0x…` named 15 programs, and the frontier was reported as 15. Retrying each offline against its own captured resource table showed **16 of 20 recompile cleanly**: the line is capped at one per program and fires on the *first* dispatch, before caching and descriptor state settle. The same shape had already appeared as `[submitorder]`'s `if (k < 32)` print cap being read as a rate. **Before quoting a diagnostic's population, find its rate limit and its trigger point.** The offline `--retry-failed-stage` verdict is the census; the skip line is a pointer. |
| 158 | **A grep for a verdict that matches a DIFFERENT field containing the same word** | 2026-08-14. `grep -E 'recompiled\|rejected'` over `--retry-failed-stage` output reports "rejected" for shaders that compiled, because `[compute-cfg] … cf_rejected=0` contains the word. Six false failures were published from it. **Match the verdict line (`^\[retry-failed-stage\] N:0 …`), never the word** — and when a census disagrees with a spot check, suspect the pattern before the subject. |
| 159 | **Selecting "the big pass" by draw count selects the SHADOW atlas, and its state reads as a defect** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. A capture filtered on `MIN_DRAWS>=100` in the black window returned 427 draws whose every resource showed `target-mask=0 effective=00` — colour writes fully masked, the exact shape of #1946. It was a **512x512 depth-only shadow pass**, where that is correct. Its main-view submits carry 43-72 draws, and both render into the same 3840x2160 target, so `TARGET_DIM` cannot separate them either. **Read the viewport before reading the render state**; the candidate census now reports it. |
| 160 | **A changing frame CRC plus millions of non-zero pixels is not evidence of rendered content** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. Frames measured 0.00% non-black at a threshold of 8/255, yet their CRC changed every second and 12.7-13.5% of pixels were non-zero at luminance 1-2 — over a million each. That reads exactly like a world rendering at ~1/128 brightness, i.e. a collapsed exposure. Amplified 64x it is **uniform dither with no structure, edges or geometry**. The animated grain is what changes the CRC. **Amplify and look before concluding a dark frame contains a scene.** |
| 161 | **Every render-target seed prosper retains can be a PLACEHOLDER, so an offline replay is not a visual oracle until you check them** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. `--draw-steps` over a 72-draw main-view capsule reported **100% non-black, max=255 at every step**, composing a blue gradient with a horizon-shaped band — apparently the sky rendering correctly offline while the live frame is black. It is the seed-miss gradient: it is already 100% non-black at step 4, before most draws run, and never changes. A second seed dumped 29.4% non-zero and proved to be the **yellow R+G diagnostic fill** from #2014. Extends the existing colour-metric trap to the replay path: **check `RTT-seeds` content before treating any replay image as evidence.** |
| 162 | **A tool flag that FILTERS where you read it as TRANSFORMS, and its silence looks like a broken tool** | 2026-08-14. `gpu_replay --draw-steps-target WxH` dumps only steps whose output is exactly that size; it does not downscale. Passing `480x270` against a 3840x2160 capture skipped every step, wrote zero images, and exited 0 — which was written up as a tooling gap blocking the investigation. **A tool that plans N outputs and writes none is a filter that matched nothing at least as often as it is a bug.** |
| 163 | **A diagnostic that prints only SUCCESSES cannot distinguish "declined" from "never offered", and one without an identifier cannot be attributed at all** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. `[indirect-pointer-relocation]` logged only valid proofs and carried no program address, so several hundred records belonged to no shader and a declined analysis was invisible. `0x413d14100` is byte-for-byte the StaticFootprint analyzer's own 386-dword target and still rejected, and nothing said whether the analyzer had declined it or matched and failed later. Adding the address **and the decline** answered it in one run: 229 of 234 succeed, so relocation was never its blocker. **Log the negative case and the subject's identity, or the diagnostic answers a different question than the one asked.** |
| 164 | **A LEVER THAT DID NOT MOVE, reported as a falsification — verify the artefact changed before reading the result** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. `PROSPER_NATIVE_COMPUTE_MULTIWAVE=1` still hung, and that was published as killing the emulation-cost hypothesis. The emitted module is **byte-identical** with and without the switch — `d04fd09b13408f9b4da7287fae34f692` in both arms and in a capsule from hours earlier — so the two runs were the same run and the result was void. What made it credible was `[subgroup] cs=… native=64 … multiwave=1 disabled=0`: that line reports the resolved **config**, and it was read as reporting the emitted **lowering**. Third time in one thread that a diagnostic reporting an input was read as reporting an output. **Hash the artefact your switch is supposed to change, and check the hash BEFORE you read the outcome** — with dump-and-skip (trap 166) it costs nothing. |
| 165 | **Interleaving failure REASONS read as interleaving successes, killing a correct hypothesis** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. Device loss was rejected because "a loss would give a monotone tail" and the capsule's reasons alternated (`declined -> indirect-dependencies -> declined -> shader-recompile -> …`) across 22 transitions. The tail **was** monotone: the last realized compute is source 38 and every source from 39 on is a failure. Only the *reasons* vary, because the *operation kinds* vary — after `device_lost`, `fail_closed_items()` refuses each dispatch while the indirect latch refuses each indirect draw. The hypothesis was correct and was thrown away. **Test the property you actually named: for "monotone failure", grep for the last SUCCESS, not for a run of equal reasons.** |
| 166 | **Dump-and-skip: a diagnostic skip ordered BEFORE the dump blinds every other instrument to the thing under suspicion** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. `PROSPER_COMPUTE_SKIP_PROGRAM` first returned above `trace_compute_item` / `maybe_dump_traced_compute_spirv`, so skipping a program also prevented dumping it — exactly backwards for its purpose, since the program worth skipping is the one that hangs the GPU. Reordered below both (the rule the timing and authority censuses already follow: observe, then return). The combination is what makes a recompiler change checkable against a hanging program at **zero device losses**, and it is what caught trap 164. |
| 167 | **Citing a STACKED/MERGED working tree into a PR whose base is `master`** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. Three separate review findings in one PR stack traced to one habit. I read `gpu_executor.cpp` on a local branch with five open PRs merged in, then pasted its line numbers into PR bodies and commit messages based on `master` — where the same code sits ~270 lines earlier (`:9598/:9609/:9354/:9422` should have been `:9287/:9358/:9118/:9187`). The same habit put `RealizationFailureReason::ComputeExecutionDeclined` into a shipped source comment, a PR body and a merged status doc: that enumerator exists only on the #2536 branch, and `git grep` on the PR's own head returns exactly one hit — the new comment itself. Every number was a line I had actually opened; the tree was wrong, not the reading. **Verify a citation against the PR's own base (`git show origin/master:<file>`), never against your working tree** — and note the failure is invisible to CI, to `diff --check`, and to the author, because the citation is *true somewhere*. |
| 168 | **A hardware watchpoint is PER-THREAD, so its zero says nothing about a multithreaded writer** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. `PROSPER_HWWATCH_ABS=0x20f848a240` armed cleanly (`[hwwatch] armed W-watch … (fd=13)`) and fired **zero times** over a 120 s route that reaches gameplay — which reads as "no CPU writer touches this address". It is not evidence. x86 debug registers are per-thread state, and this arms them on **the main guest thread only** (the source comment says so: *"owner = this = the main guest thread"*). A guest worker thread, and prosper's own renderer thread doing the compute write-back memcpy, are both structurally invisible to it — and the write-back is *known* to write that exact address ~20 times per run, so the instrument missed a writer it was pointed straight at. **Before believing a watchpoint zero, establish that the thread you armed is the thread that could write.** A same-thread positive control does not help here: it confirms the debug register works, which was never the doubt. |
| 169 | **"the program's binding N" is not a stable identity — the SAME program at the SAME fetch PC resolves to DIFFERENT buffers per dispatch** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, and it produced a fully-published wrong root cause. `--dump-compute-resource 37:8` was read as "the hanging dispatch's traversal table". `compute[37]` is source **38**, which *completed*; the hang is source **39**, and its binding 8 at the same `pc=0x5b` resolves to a **different address** (`0x20f848417c` vs `0x20f848a240`). A whole causal chain — 183 cyclic indices, an offline/live divergence, a "183 must become 0" oracle — was built on the buffer belonging to the dispatch that worked. The hanging dispatch's table is acyclic with a longest chain of **11 steps**. Nothing flagged it: both are 8252 bytes, same program, same fetch PC, same binding number, and the census returned a plausible non-zero answer. **Index a capsule by the OPERATION you mean (`failure[N]` vs `compute[N]`) and check the resolved ADDRESS matches, every time** — a program dispatched repeatedly with different arguments makes binding numbers meaningless as identity. |
| 170 | **A device loss names the submission that OBSERVES it, not the one that caused it** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, and it misdirected an entire multi-day investigation. `[compute] fatal Vulkan device loss stage=queue-submit … program=0x413dc6700` reads as "this program hung the GPU". It means `vkQueueSubmit` returned `VK_ERROR_DEVICE_LOST` — the device was **already** dead, so the named program is the first one *after* the reset. The check that settles it is the one nobody ran for days: **count the fence-wait timeouts.** `execute_item` waits 30 s and reports a timeout as `queue-wait`; across every routed run there were **705 waits, 705 completions, 0 timeouts, 28 queue-submit losses**. No compute dispatch ever hung — the hang is in another queue entirely, and compute merely discovers the corpse and disables itself, which is what then drops every indirect draw. Corroborating tells that were visible all along: the named program varied run to run (dispatch 39, dispatch 40, a different program), and one of the accused was a 753-dword module with **zero loops** running 42 threads. **Before attributing a hang to the named submission, prove that submission failed to COMPLETE — a loss at submit time is evidence about ordering, not about the culprit.** |
| 171 | **A zero TIMEOUT count is not a zero LATENCY count — measure the duration, not the threshold** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, and it reversed a conclusion I had already published. Having established that a device loss names the submission that OBSERVES it (trap 170), I counted compute fence-wait timeouts to find the real culprit: **705 waits, 705 completions, 0 timeouts**, and concluded no compute dispatch hangs and the fault must be in the graphics path. The count was right; the inference was wrong. The timeout is **30 seconds** and the pathological event is **2 seconds** — a dispatch can be three orders of magnitude slower than every other one and never trip it. Logging the wait DURATION, and reporting any wait over 100 ms *even when it succeeds*, produced exactly one line in a whole route: `SLOW fence wait 2045.2 ms result=0 program=0x413dc6700 dispatch=39`, immediately before the loss. **A threshold instrument answers only "did anything cross my threshold"; when the question is "is anything anomalous", record the distribution.** |
| 172 | **A field's LABEL is part of the instrument — and a VARIABLE'S NAME is not evidence of what it stores** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. The `PROSPER_CFG_TRIP_BOUND` witness stores four dwords and the host printed the third as `last-block`. A hit reporting `14` was read as "basic block 14", mapped by hand onto the block holding the guest's loop body at pc88..97, and published as *the guest's loop is spinning*. **Then the correction was wrong too, in the opposite direction.** The emitter's variable is called `pc_var`, so it was renamed `next-guest-pc` and the loop-body claim withdrawn as if the number were a program counter — on the strength of the identifier's NAME, without opening the store sites. Every write to `pc_var` stores `dispatch_for_block[...]`: a dispatcher switch-case **ordinal**, neither a block index nor a pc. So the first reading made an ordinal a block, the second made it a pc, and both were published. The number never changed and was never wrong. **Read the STORE SITES — all of them — before naming a diagnostic field, and treat a variable's name as a hypothesis exactly like a printf's label.** Fixed by reporting `next-dispatch` and having each phase print its ordinal -> guest-pc map, so the mapping step that produced both errors no longer has to be done by hand. |
| 173 | **A racy record publishes a NARROWER result than the truth, and narrow reads as strong** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. A device-side witness recorded the span of dispatcher ordinals a runaway loop visited. Every invocation that hit the cap wrote the same slots with plain `OpStore`, so the host read one invocation's local extremes: **`dispatch-range=6..9`**. Four adjacent ordinals over 4,096 iterations is a vivid picture of a tight cycle, and it was published as one. Republishing the same fields through device-scope `OpAtomicUMin`/`UMax` — a true reduction over every invocation — gives **`0..9`**, a ten-ordinal span that shows nothing of the kind, because ordinal 0 is the program entry every invocation passes through. **The conclusion survived and the derivation did not:** what actually establishes the loop never exits is the CEILING (max ordinal 9, so no lane ever reached the code after the loop), which the racy record also happened to report correctly. Note the direction of the error — last-writer publication produced a TIGHTER, more striking interval than reality, and a tighter interval is the direction that reads as better evidence, so nobody questioned it. **Reduce per-invocation extremes on the device; a record whose fields come from different invocations is not a record, and its most quotable form is the one least likely to be true.** |
| 174 | **A token that records INTENT is not evidence that anything happened — and shared guest storage is not isolated by proving ONE program does not use it** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, caught in review. A diagnostic wrote a five-dword witness into the internal GDS buffer, and the host gated reading and CLEARING those dwords on a token set from the selector state plus "does the selected program access GDS". Two holes, both invisible from the token's name. First, it was computed **before translation**, so a program every selector accepted but that emitted nothing — a structured loop, or a phase ordinal the program does not have — still read as instrumented, and the host then reported whatever guest data occupied those dwords and destroyed it. Second and worse, the buffer is **one persistent allocation shared by every dispatch**: proving the instrumented program cannot read it says nothing about the program that runs next and does, so the instrument could perturb a different program's state while claiming single-program isolation. Fixed by carrying a real emission result out of compilation, and by save/prepare/report/**restore** so guest memory is byte-identical afterwards. **Gate on what was EMITTED, never on what was SELECTED; and for shared mutable storage, preservation is the property to test, not exclusivity.** |
| 175 | **An execution test can be INVALID and still print "passed" — run the focused tests under the validation layer** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, caught in review. Module-shaped assertions had already proved too weak twice, so the trip-bound test was strengthened to actually EXECUTE a bounded dispatcher and assert the truncated result. That arm dispatched a pipeline whose layout omitted the binding the module statically uses for its witness — `VUID-VkComputePipelineCreateInfo-layout-07988` and `VUID-vkCmdDispatch-None-08114`. The focused test does not make validation messages fatal, so it printed **all passed** while measuring undefined behaviour; the expected value may even have been produced, but nothing was established. **Fixing it introduced a second one:** the added buffer was never destroyed, so every `run_compute` call leaked it past `vkDestroyDevice` (`VUID-vkDestroyDevice-device-05137`, eight instances) — a defect invisible to every assertion in the file. **Run focused GPU tests under `VK_INSTANCE_LAYERS=VK_LAYER_KHRONOS_validation` and require ZERO errors, especially when the test was added to make a weak assertion strong: an execution arm that is itself invalid is the most convincing wrong answer available.** |
| 176 | **An assertion is unverified until a mutation of the LINE IT NAMES makes it fail — feature-level mutation is not enough** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, found by mutating my own fix. A reviewer showed that a predicate deciding whether the host may write guest memory matched a witness slot NUMBER rather than the witness RESOURCE. The fix was correct: resolve the atomic through the variable decorated set 0 / binding 127. The arm written to prove it moved that decoration from 127 to 126 and asserted the module was no longer recognised — which passes, and proves nothing about the base check, because a module with no binding-127 variable is already rejected by an earlier `no such variable` exit. **Deleting the base check left every assertion green.** The case that isolates it is a module that HAS the binding-127 variable and performs the slot atomic through a DIFFERENT one — built by repointing one word of the real emitted module — and that arm fails the moment the check is removed. Two assertions can sit under the same heading ("resource identity") while only one of them is covered. **Mutate the specific line an assertion names, not the feature it belongs to; and be most suspicious of an arm you wrote to satisfy a reviewer, because it is shaped by the finding rather than by the mechanism.** |
| 177 | **Enumerate the conditions in the DIFF, not the ones in the review — a fix is usually broader than the finding that prompted it** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, and it is trap 176 failing on its own author. A review said a predicate must resolve an access chain's BASE to the right resource. The fix did that and, in the same edit, tightened the site to a canonical shape: exact word count, member-zero first index, slot second index — **four conditions where the finding named one.** An arm was written for the base, the commit message stated that per trap 176 each new check had been reverted individually to confirm its arm bites, and that sentence was true of the three checks its author had in mind and false of the three the same commit introduced. A reviewer relaxed the shape back to `len >= 5` and deleted the member-zero check: **the entire focused test stayed green.** The gap is not ignorance of the rule; it is that the rule was applied to the checks being thought about rather than to the checks being shipped, and an arm shaped by a finding stops exactly where the finding stopped. **Before claiming per-condition coverage, list the conditions the DIFF adds and revert each one; the breadth a fix gains beyond its finding is precisely the part nobody has tested.** |
| 178 | **A new instrument on the WRONG code path reports a clean zero — anchor it to a line already proven to run on the route you are measuring** | GTA V, 2026-08-14. `PROSPER_COMPUTE_ADDRESS_WATCH` was added to answer "which other programs touch this buffer", and it printed **nothing for a whole route**. Not because nothing touches the buffer — because it went into `realize_compute_dispatches()` while the live route realizes through `realize_retained_compute()`. Two realization paths; the instrument was on the unused one, so its silence was structural rather than informative, and "no other writers" is exactly the conclusion it would have supported. **The zero was also the answer that agreed with what I had just concluded** — a retraction had moved suspicion away from the known writer, and "no other writers" would have pushed it back, which is precisely why it deserved distrust rather than acceptance (cf. the charter's note on corrections that RAISE a claim getting checked least). What caught it was arithmetic, not suspicion: 352 lines in the log, zero from the watch, on a buffer another instrument was reporting by address in the same file. **Anchor a new watch beside a call site with known output on that exact route, and before believing a null, confirm the instrument produced ANY output at all.** |
| 179 | **`cmake … \| grep \| head` can report RC=0 on a FAILED build — and a false zero survives its own fix** | GTA V, 2026-08-14, and it put a non-compiling commit on a branch. A scripted edit left a stray `}` in `gpu_executor.cpp`. The build was checked with `cmake --build … 2>&1 \| grep -E 'error:' \| head -4; echo RC=${PIPESTATUS[0]}` — `head` closing early SIGPIPEs the pipeline, so the construction printed **RC=0 on a build that failed**, and the commit was pushed. The charter's own rule ("a pipeline's exit status is its LAST stage's") had been violated all session without consequence until the filter hid a real failure. **The compounding part:** the same instrument had ALREADY produced a false zero from being on an unused code path (trap 178), so when it reported zero again after the placement fix, the natural reading was "placement was not the cause, look elsewhere" — a false zero that survives its own repair falsifies the repair. What caught it was neither the exit code nor the failing instrument but `strings <binary> \| grep -c <symbol>`: **present in the source, absent from the binary**, a contradiction no build status can explain away. **Redirect build output to a file and read the status directly; before running an instrument, confirm its symbol is IN the binary.** |
| 180 | **`PROSPER_TARGET_WATCH` parses base-0, so an address without `0x` silently watches a DIFFERENT one — and reports a confident zero about it** | GTA V, 2026-08-16. The switch answers "is this address EVER a colour target, in any of the eight MRT slots", exact and unsampled, which is exactly the question behind the absent scene-colour producer. Invoked as `PROSPER_TARGET_WATCH=2063380000,20431c0000` it prints `watching 2 address(es)` and then `addr=0x7afcae20 draws=0 ... NEVER A COLOUR TARGET` — `strtoull(p, &end, 0)` read the un-prefixed digits as **decimal**, so 2063380000 became 0x7AFCAE20 and the run confidently reported nothing about an address the title never had. The failure announces itself only if you read the echoed address rather than the verdict, and the verdict is the line you went looking for. **Write `0x` on every address, and check the address the instrument echoes back against the one you meant.** *Second half, and the more expensive one:* I only found the pre-existing switch because my own duplicate of it collided — I had searched `getenv("PROSPER_...MAP` and the file I was editing, never the whole tree for an existing colour-target watch, and was ~40 lines into building a worse version (sampled by nothing, but only reachable through the dimension-filtered colour-state trace) when the collision surfaced. The existing one is better: it states in its own comment why a 1-in-32 sampled census cannot answer an "ever" question (a ten-draw writer is missed 73% of the time). **Grep the tree for an existing instrument before writing one; the in-tree version usually carries the reasoning you are about to re-derive.** |
| 181 | **A bloom smear inflates `nonblack_rgb_pixels` and `distinct_rgb_colors` exactly as a gradient does — and can rank the WORSE frame higher** | GTA V, 2026-08-16. Three depth/stencil arms were scored on the presented frame with two controls agreeing to 0.15%, so the numbers were real and the spread was not noise: `PROSPER_NO_DEPTH=1` +43.5%, `PROSPER_NO_STENCIL=1` +7.4%. Recorded as "depth rejection is the dominant loss of presented content". **Opening the images retracted it.** Both arms are a large overexposed white blob plus blue haze with **no world geometry**, and the depth arm additionally **loses the radar** — plainly worse to look at than the control it beat by 43%. The counter rose because bloom flooded the frame. The reverse also holds and is the sharper half: `PROSPER_RTT_ALIAS`, the one arm that genuinely shows the bank interior with correct perspective, scores **19,623 distinct colours against the control's 30,262** — *more world, fewer colours*. So the metric is not merely noisy here, it is **anti-correlated with the thing being optimised**, and a ranking built on it points away from the fix. This is the colour-metric trap already on this list wearing a new costume (there a gradient, here a bloom smear), which is why knowing that entry did not prevent it: the earlier form was about a *placeholder* fooling a metric, and this one is real rendered output. **Never quote a frame counter without opening the frame, and be most suspicious when an arm you expected to help produces a big number.** |
| 182 | **A REDUCED fixture reproducing a known bug is a positive control for the reduction, not for the bug** | #2149 audit, 2026-08-17. Building the split-gate scanner, four fixtures were written from four measured instances, and all four passed on the first run — then the tree scan found **none** of the two that are still live. Each reduction had removed the property under test. `hle_agc.cpp:1774` is `dump_call_log(stderr)`, a call with no format string, and the fixture used an `fprintf`, so a printf-anchored rule matched the fixture and could never match the original. `gpu_executor.cpp:6402`'s real guard is `if (log \|\| g_dyntrace_force)`, where the second alternative is a plain **global flag** set only inside env-gated blocks two files away, and the fixture used `if (log)` — a direct `getenv`. Both fixtures were faithful to the *description* of the bug and unfaithful to its *mechanism*, which is what a reduction does by design. The tell was available and nearly missed: the fixtures passed **first try**, and a discriminator that needs no debugging on a hard case usually is not exercising it. **Reduce the surroundings, never the mechanism: keep the exact call form, the exact guard expression, and the exact indirection the original used — and confirm the rule fires on the ORIGINAL file:line before believing the fixture.** This sits beside trap 122: there a same-source control laundered a null, here a hand-built control laundered one, so "construct the positive instance by hand" is necessary and not sufficient — the hand-built instance still has to be able to express the case. |
| 183 | **`git stash push -- <file>` with nothing to stash creates NO entry, so the paired `git stash pop` pops ANOTHER agent's work** | #2149 lane, 2026-08-17. Re-running a mutation arm that had worked earlier in the session: `git stash push -- src/hle/graphics/hle_agc.cpp` (output suppressed), run the checker, `git stash pop`. The first time this was correct, because the fix was uncommitted. The second time the fix had since been **committed**, so push printed *"No local changes to save"* and created nothing — and pop then took `stash@{0}`, which belonged to a concurrent lane on a different branch. It conflicted, leaving `UU prosper/tools/gpu_replay/gpu_replay.cpp` in my tree. Two things saved it: a conflicting pop **keeps** the entry (`git stash list` still showed it, 41 insertions intact), and the conflict was loud. **A clean apply would have been silent** — their changes would have landed in my worktree, their entry would have been dropped, and the first sign would have been someone else's code in my next `git add -A`. The charter already says the stash stack is shared; what it did not say is that the *push* half can no-op, which is what decouples the pair. **So: never pair a blind push with a blind pop — check `git stash list` before popping and pop by name (`git stash pop stash@{N}`), or avoid the stash entirely.** For reverting a COMMITTED hunk, the stash is the wrong tool anyway: `git show <sha>^:<path> > <path>`, test, then `git checkout HEAD -- <path>` touches only your own file and cannot reach a shared stack. |
| 184 | **`gh pr checks` prints `pending 0` for a job that has been RUNNING for forty minutes, identically to one that never started** | #2149 lane, 2026-08-17. Reporting CI status on a saturated runner pool, I wrote that four jobs were "stuck in the runner queue, all at 0 s elapsed". One of them, `Linux` — the job carrying ctest, i.e. the one being waited on — had been executing for **over half an hour**: `gh api .../actions/jobs/<id>` showed `status=in_progress`, `started_at=08:01:09`, and six of eight steps already `success` with `Test` in flight. `gh pr checks` computes elapsed only for COMPLETED jobs and renders every other state as the single word `pending` with `0`, so "queued, never dispatched" and "running, nearly done" are the same two characters. The failure is the one this lane is about, aimed at me: a well-formed zero from an instrument that was not measuring the thing I read it as. It also produces the wrong ACTION — a queued job means wait or investigate capacity, a running job means wait and read its steps. **Use `gh api repos/OWNER/REPO/actions/jobs/<id> --jq '.status, .started_at'` (or `.../runs/<id>/jobs`) before describing a job as not started, and quote a step name rather than an elapsed time.** |
| 185 | **A premise witness that does not fire is VOID, not negative — and the cause can be a different NID that prosper answers CORRECTLY, short-circuiting the chain upstream** | *Earthion* (`PPSA28061`), 2026-08-17, #2186. #2167 changed `sceNpTrophy2GetGroupInfoArray` from the dispatcher's `0` to an error, and the A/B designed to check the one call site its evidence did not cover was built to verify its own premise: the "before" arm reverts the registration, so the NID must appear in the unimplemented table and must vanish in the "after" arm. **It appeared in neither.** The instrument was armed, on the right route, and correct; the *subject* was unreachable. The title's only call site is step 5 of a six-step trophy-init chain in which every step short-circuits the rest on a negative return, and step 4 has answered `0x80551500` since **#231** — `sceNpTrophy2GetGameInfo`, a **registered** NID roughly 1900 PRs older than the change under test, and therefore invisible to an unimplemented-NID table, which can only report absences. Every explanation a missing witness naturally suggests — too short a run, no input route, wrong frontend, dead code — points at the experiment, and **not one of them could have made the witness fire.** What resolved it was the charter's own rule applied to reachability: unregister the **upstream** gate to construct the reachable case by hand, outside whatever produced the null. That arm called the site exactly once (matching a static read in which the guest pre-zeroes its own out-count), and the real A/B could then be run where the code is actually reached — both arms pixel-identical. **When a premise witness does not fire, map the call chain to the import that gates it BEFORE touching the route** (`tools/re/stub_nid_map.py` names the import behind a stub address, which is what makes this a lookup rather than an afternoon); a correct answer upstream silences a downstream witness just as thoroughly as a broken instrument, and leaves no trace in any absence-based diagnostic. |
| 186 | **A line-based read of a registration table counts BOTH arms of an `#if`, and every duplicate line, as separate Sony entry points** | HLE registration census (#2070), 2026-08-17. The question was "how many Sony NIDs collapse onto one prosper handler?", which a runtime `hle_calls --values` histogram structurally cannot answer because it keys on the handler. A grep over `src/hle/**` reported **41** shared gated rows on *Sonic Origins*, with `s_ok` serving 15 names. Both figures are apparatus. `hle_kernel_mem.cpp` defines `register_kernel_mem_hle()` **twice**, ~3,200 lines apart, in the two arms of one `#if defined(__linux__) \| defined(__APPLE__)` (a two-pipe OR), so a platform-blind read sees every kernel-memory handler registered for two names and promotes it to "shared" — five handlers that answer exactly one Sony function each (`k_dmem_size`, `k_virtual_query`, `k_alloc_dmem`, `k_mtypeprotect`, `k_mprotect`) became collapses that way. Separately, `scePthreadAttrSetaffinity` is registered to `k_attr_noop` at both `hle_kernel.cpp:4539` and `:4588`, so counting *sites* rather than *distinct Sony names* inflates any handler with a duplicate. The true figure is **36**, and platform-blind per-site counting reproduces 41 exactly — the artifact is fully explained, not merely suspected. `s_ok` serves **14** on every platform: its "15th", `sceNpCheckCallback`, is registered to `s_ok` only in the `#else` (Windows) arm at `hle_service.cpp:4596`, and the two arms **swap** a name rather than adding one, so the platform-blind count is wrong on Linux and Windows alike. **The direction is the danger: both errors inflate, and an inflated collapse census reads as diligence rather than as a broken instrument.** `tools/re/hle_handler_map.py` now evaluates the arms per `--platform`, counts distinct names, and is reconciled on every ctest run against the registry the compiled binary actually builds (`tools/re/hle_registry_dump.cpp`) — the compiler is the only authority on which arm won. **When you parse source to count anything, ask what the preprocessor would have deleted, and count the THINGS you mean rather than the LINES that mention them.** |
| 187 | **`gh run rerun` re-executes inside the SAME run, against the ORIGINAL merge ref — after the base branch moves it reports a stale answer wearing a fresh timestamp** | CI verification, 2026-08-17 (#2604). A one-line CI PR's `Linux` job was rerun *after* the PR it depended on had merged, on the assumption GitHub would recompute the merge commit against the new `master`. It does not. Run `32019332338` was **created 10:15:52Z**, the dependency merged **10:58:59Z**, and the rerun executed inside that same run at **~11:00** — so it rebuilt a tree 45 minutes stale and failed with precisely the symptom the merged fix removes (`Cannot access memory at address 0x1149`). **Nothing on the job page says the tree is old**: it shows a new timestamp, a fresh log and a red X, which is why the failure was briefly read as "the fix is incomplete" rather than "the instrument answered about a different tree". The fix is to force a genuinely new merge ref — **rebase onto current `origin/master` and push** — and the reading rule is that any `rerun` after the base moved is **void, not negative**. Cheap check before believing one: compare the run's `createdAt` against the merge time of anything it depends on (`gh run view <id> --json createdAt`). |
| 188 | **A flaky test bisects EXACTLY like a real regression, and the run that would clear it is the one nobody performs** | macOS/Rosetta CI, 2026-08-17, #2427 / #2617. `sce_pthread_encoding` SEGFAULTed on two of three attempts of one identical job, on a branch touching only GPU code, and a CI bisect reported every earlier head green and only the last one red. That shape is the *definition* of a regression, so a full investigation cycle went into a diff that was not at fault. It was a **3.0% flake**: measured across 502 completed attempts of that job (2026-08-08..17), 15 died here — 71% of every failure the job produced — and a 2-of-3 cluster on a 3% base rate is unremarkable. **The trap is that a bisect over a flake is not noisy, it is CONFIDENTLY WRONG.** Each head is a single sample, a green head is weak evidence of nothing, and the experiment that falsifies the result — re-running an EARLIER head until it fails — is precisely the one nobody runs, because those heads have already "passed". `--output-on-failure` was already enabled on this job and its output held the answer in plain sight (every failing log stopped at the same line), but no two failures were ever compared, because each was read as a one-off. **So: before bisecting an intermittent symptom, measure its base rate over recent runs of that job, and re-run a GREEN head enough times to bound it — a bisect is valid only where a single run is a reliable oracle.** The rule has a counterpart for the fix: this one was closed by deleting the row that spawned an unjoined thread, so the race became *impossible* rather than rarer. A clean run count is worth exactly the N you derive from the **measured** rate, and the two rates here differ by an order of magnitude — 0 of 2000 is overwhelming against the 3% CI rate (`0.97^2000` ≈ 3e-27) but only ~1e-3 against the 1-in-300 rate the local reproduction actually had, which is the one that governs. Derive N before quoting a clean count, and prefer a structural argument, which needs no N at all. |
| 189 | **A GREEN required check on a PR is a statement about a merge that may no longer exist — and unlike a red one, nobody ever re-derives it** | Trap-numbering, 2026-08-17 (#2211, #2089). A `pull_request` job validates `refs/pull/N/merge`, which GitHub computes **when the event fires** and never recomputes as the base moves; trap 187 records the same staleness for `gh run rerun`. That entry is about a **red** result, and the direction nobody looks is the other one. #2581's `Docs` job was **green** against a base predating #2574, so nothing warned either author that both had written instrument-trap row **182** — the collision was found by simulating the merge by hand (`git merge-file` → rc=1, then running the gate on the merge result), never by CI. The asymmetry is the whole trap: a red check is investigated, so its staleness surfaces within minutes, while a green check is *acted on* — it is the thing that authorises the merge, and an author who has just been told they may proceed has no reason to ask what tree the answer was about. **On any PR touching a shared-key file (a numbered table, a registration list, a manifest), run the gate against the MERGE RESULT against fresh `origin/master` before merging** — `git merge --no-commit` or `git merge-file`, then the check — because the gate at head time cannot see a concurrent append by construction. Cheap tell: compare the check's completion time against `git log -1 --format=%cI origin/master`. |
| 190 | **A NEGATIVE control cannot be validated by "require the arm to show a failure" — so a setup step that dies leaves it printing the exact green it was supposed to print** | #2588 lane, 2026-08-17. Validating the new ASCII-output gate, three by-hand mutation arms were run against a file that **did not exist** (`src/loader/loader.cpp`; the tree has `linker.cpp`). Arms 1 and 2 were positive controls and self-reported: the mutation script raised `FileNotFoundError`, the gate stayed green, and an arm expected to go red printing `RC=0` is unmissable. **Arm 3 was the negative control** — an em dash in a *comment*, which must NOT fire — and it printed `all checks passed`, `RC=0`, which is character-for-character the result a correct run produces. Nothing in it was wrong except that the subject was never mutated, and the `cp: cannot stat` above it scrolled past in a log whose verdict lines were the part being read. Traps 98 and 153 both prescribe *"an arm that cannot show its lever moved is no result"* — that defence is **structurally unavailable here**, because a negative control's whole claim is that it stays green, so there is no failure to demand. **The fix is to make the arm assert its own precondition and print it beside the verdict**: each arm here now emits `MUTATION PRESENT: True` from a re-read of the file on disk before the gate runs, so "green because the rule permits it" and "green because nothing happened" stop looking identical. Generalises past mutation arms to every experiment whose expected outcome is the null: **when the hypothesis predicts silence, the apparatus must say out loud that it was pointed at something.** #2588. |
| 191 | **A fixture smaller than the code's own chunk size cannot exercise the loop it is guarding — and the suite stays green saying so** | AvPlayer guest file-replacement reader (#1955 / #1974 / #2618), 2026-08-17. `avp_add_source` reads the guest's media in **1 MiB** chunks; the regression fixture that shipped with the reader serves **5,000 bytes**. So `test_avplayer`'s read loop had only ever run **one iteration**, and multi-chunk assembly, an honest short read, and the position arithmetic that carries across a chunk boundary were *structurally inexpressible* against it — the same shape as trap 122, with the fixture's SIZE rather than a generator's geometry as the thing that removes the case from the space. Nothing about the passing suite distinguished "the loop is correct" from "the loop ran once". Refilled at 2 MiB + 12,345 bytes behind a reader capped at 700,000 bytes per call, with `read_calls >= 4` asserted so the loop must demonstrate it iterated; a mutation treating a short read as a failure then kills three checks that the old fixture could not have expressed. **When a test guards a loop, check the fixture against the loop's own step size before believing the green** — and assert the iteration count, because that is the one property a too-small fixture cannot fake. |
| 192 | **A test fixture at a fixed path in the shared build directory — the failure text names the SUBSYSTEM, never a filename, and "the box was busy" is the diagnosis that fits every observation and is still wrong** | Test infrastructure, 2026-08-17 (#2582, #2592, #2632). `file_binary_roundtrip` failed 16 sub-checks under concurrent load and passed alone, so #2582 concluded "the only variable left between the passing and failing arms is **concurrent machine load**" — supported by a runtime spanning 0.01 s on CI to 861 s here. **Load is not the cause and cannot be fixed.** The pre-#2592 binary reproduces all 16 on an **idle** box in 29 s against one competing writer: `while :; do rm -f prosper-test-file-binary.tmp; sleep 0.05; done &` next to a run in that directory. Load only widens the overlap between two ctest invocations sharing one build directory. Three arms pin it — fixed-name binary under that deleter **FAIL 16**, scratch-rooted binary under the **same** deleter **PASS**, scratch-rooted binary with the deleter re-aimed at its **actual** scratch fixture **FAIL, the identical 16** — so the pass is the path moving and not the lever going dead, which the second arm alone could not have shown. **What makes this class expensive is that the failure never mentions a file:** the 16 name `sceKernelChmod`, `sceKernelUtimes`, `sceKernelDup` and binary reads, so it reads as an HLE defect; the same day, 6 concurrent `spv_validate` sharing one output directory printed `spirv-val REJECTED it: Invalid SPIR-V magic number` — an accusation against the **recompiler** — and 6 concurrent `test_runtime_prx_load` sharing one root failed 4 with `no module was loaded`, an accusation against the **loader**. Every one is a filename. Both controls used distinct paths at identical concurrency and passed 6 of 6. **Before believing a test failure that appeared under load, re-run it against a single competing writer on an idle box; if it reproduces, the defect is the path, not the load.** **And the one observation the load story actually rested on turns out to have a cause of its own — load and failure here are correlated through a COMMON CAUSE, not causally linked, which is what made the wrong hypothesis rational rather than careless.** The 0.01 s on CI against 861 s here is not the fixture race showing up as elapsed time: `tests/hle/test_file_binary.cpp:173` calls `sceKernelSync`, and `src/hle/fs/hle_file.cpp:1731` implements it as a bare `::sync()` — a whole-filesystem writeback barrier whose cost scales with **machine-wide dirty data**, i.e. with every other lane's build traffic (#2651). Read off the live processes rather than inferred: during a 4-concurrent arm every running process sat in `syscall=162` (`sync`) in state D for minutes, with `/proc/<pid>/wchan` reading `rq_qos_wait`, `wb_wait_for_completion` and `sync_inodes_sb` — measured twice, by the reviewer and by a second lane, different pids, same three wchans. A busy shared box therefore produces **both** observations independently: it makes `sync()` slow **and** it makes a second ctest invocation against the shared `build-linux` likely. So load tracked the failures perfectly while causing none of them, and #2582's arm 5 — arm 3 repeated once the box quietened — coming back fast *and* green is one fact instead of two coincidences. **A runtime that inflates alongside a failure is not evidence that the load caused the failure. Find out what the slowness is made of before promoting the correlation: a third variable driving both is exactly what a perfect correlation looks like.** Fixtures go through `tests/fixtures/test_scratch.h` (per case, per pid, removed at exit) — its header carries this reproduction. |
| 193 | **A diagnostic that perturbs the subject: arming a software WATCHPOINT made a PRODUCTION permission check answer wrong** | The inverse of the usual entry on this list, and the reason it belongs on it. `PROSPER_WATCH_LABEL`/`_ABS`/`_HOT` and `PROSPER_WATCH_COMPANION` work by mprotect-ing a live guest page down to `PROT_READ` and back. `guest_writable`'s range cache (#2389) and `guest_readable`'s memoize POSITIVE permission answers and drop them only when `host::guest_mapping_generation()` moves, and the handler advanced nothing -- so while a watch was armed `guest_writable` answered TRUE, from cache, for a page the instrument itself had just made read-only. **Any observation of write-guard or write-refusal behaviour taken with one of these watches armed, before #2393, is void rather than negative.** Before #2389 the Linux arm re-parsed `/proc/self/maps` and saw `r--p`, so the perturbation is younger than the instrument. The general rule: **an instrument that MUTATES the subject's state owes every cache of that state an invalidation**, and the audit that finds those consumers is cheap next to the class of bug it prevents -- here, six of the eleven further sites in `guest_write_watch.cpp` were inside the fault handler, where the obvious fix (the mutex-taking `notify_guest_mapping_*`) would have traded a stale cache for a deadlock. Fixed: every protection change in both files now routes through one notifying wrapper, and `tests/host/memory/test_watch_mapping_generation.cpp` asserts mechanically that none is added without one. #2393, #2601. |
| 194 | **`git merge-base --is-ancestor` and `git branch --contains` both answer NOT MERGED for squash-merged work, because the reviewed branch tip never becomes an ancestor** | Merge tooling, 2026-08-17 (#2639, #2610, #2614). A squash merge writes a **new** commit; the tip that was reviewed and pushed is not in its history, so every ancestry test aimed at that tip answers "no" about work that is entirely on master. Reproduced on #2639, squash `b56ffda1`: `git merge-base --is-ancestor b56ffda1 origin/master` → **yes**; the same test on the reviewed tip `e08894b9` → **no**; `git branch -r --contains e08894b9 \| grep -c origin/master` → **0**; and `git show origin/master:prosper/tools/screenshot/capture_manifest.cpp \| grep -c early_stop_armed` → **1**. The work was on master throughout. **It fired twice in one day, on two lanes, and both conclusions were confident and acted on.** One lane read a merged dependency (#2610, branch `fix/issue-2089-trap-numbering`) as unmerged and nearly re-briefed four PRs on that basis. Another compared its own pushed branch against #2614, found a byte-identical paragraph of its own prose in #2614's tree, established that its commit was "not an ancestor" of #2614, and concluded a peer lane had **copied file content out of its branch** — raising a "clean merge, broken file" alarm about a divergent snapshot going stale. False: #2614 had rebased onto a master that already carried the paragraph, its merge-base `f46a8e58` containing the squash, so it had inherited the text legitimately and in order. **Test the MERGE COMMIT or test by CONTENT (`git show origin/master:<path>`) — never the branch tip.** `merge_commit_sha` alone is not enough: GitHub populates it on **open** PRs too, as a test-merge object, so read `--jq '[.merged, .merge_commit_sha]'` and use the sha only when `.merged` is true. Measured on the open #2602: `merged=false`, `merge_commit_sha=31dc4719`, and `git merge-base --is-ancestor 31dc4719 origin/master` exits **128** (object not in the clone), not 1 — so a script testing `rc != 0` reads a bad object as "not merged" and is right by accident until it is not. The tell is ancestry saying no while content says yes. **Ancestry is sound about the COMMIT and silent about the CONTENT**, which is the distinction that matters and is stronger than "it never lies optimistically": it never reports a commit that was never merged, but a **revert** makes it report one whose work is gone. #2026's squash `f36fe758` **is** an ancestor of master while #2044 ("Revert #2026") removed its 437 lines — ancestry answers "merged" about content that is not there. The content test is the only one sound in both directions. What makes the trap durable is that the one question a sceptical reader thinks to ask, "could this be too optimistic?", is the single direction the instrument does not fail in. |
| 195 | **`gh api .../files` without `--paginate` returns ONE PAGE, and a truncated list read through `grep` produces a clean, wrong "nothing there"** | #2639 review follow-up, 2026-08-17 (#2614). `gh api repos/OWNER/REPO/pulls/N/files` answers with the API's default page — **30 items** — and nothing in the output says it is a prefix. Ask "does this PR touch any file I changed?", pipe it to `grep`, and a PR larger than one page yields an empty result that reads as **no overlap**. Measured on #2614 the same hour: unpaginated → **30** filenames, of which **0** matched `screenshot\|AGENTS`; `--paginate` → **59** filenames, of which **2** matched, and both were files the asking lane had edited (`prosper/tools/screenshot/screenshot.cpp`, `prosper/tools/AGENTS.md`). The conclusion drawn from the short list — no shared files, so merge order is irrelevant — was the reverse of what the data said, and it was reached in one command with no error and no warning. **Pass `--paginate` on any `gh api` list you will draw a NEGATIVE conclusion from** (`files`, `commits`, `comments`, `reviews`, `issues`), and cross-check against the count the API itself reports: `gh api repos/OWNER/REPO/pulls/N --jq .changed_files` returned **59** here, contradicting the 30 on its own. That makes the check an experiment that detects its own invalidity, rather than one relying on a reader noticing that 30 is a suspiciously round number. Same family as the charter's "absence from grep is not evidence", aimed at a paginated API rather than a file tree: the instrument answered a narrower question than the one asked, and said so nowhere. |
| 196 | **Killing a `distrobox enter` wrapper does NOT kill the build inside the container — the log stops growing, which is indistinguishable from a hung build, and restarting is the response that costs** | Shared-box build tooling, 2026-08-17. A lane's 10-minute Bash timeout killed its `distrobox enter` wrapper while `ninja` kept compiling **inside** the container. **Nothing container-side died** — a probe on this box SIGKILLed the wrapper and found both the `podman exec` client and the container-side `bash` still running, with the marker file appearing on schedule 34 s later. What went away was the thing *reading* the output, so the log simply stopped updating — the exact signature of a stall, and the tempting reading. (Do not go looking for a dead podman client to confirm the trap: you will find it alive and conclude the trap does not apply.) Restarting on top of it produced **two builds in one directory**: three concurrent `ar` processes writing one `libprosper_core.a`, a truncated archive, and about an hour of link failures that read as code defects rather than as apparatus. **After a `distrobox enter` wrapper dies, wait on the CONTAINER-SIDE process instead of restarting on top of it** — `pgrep -x ninja` / `pgrep -x cc1plus` before concluding anything — and never start a second build in a build directory that may still have one running. **A log that stopped updating is evidence about the STREAM, not about the BUILD.** Note the wait loop itself has a trap: `pgrep -f` matches the shell running it (trap 135), so use `pgrep -x` on the program name, or check the process's `cwd` under `/proc`, rather than a `-f` pattern. Closest relative is **trap 135**: a wait state that looks exactly like a slow build. The resemblance is the whole row, not just the `pgrep` clause — in both, a process that is fine is read as a process that is stuck. |
| 197 | **A broad `pkill -f` crosses lane boundaries on this shared box — and a killed `ctest` does not look killed, it looks like a test failure** | Shared-box process hygiene, 2026-08-17, reported independently by two lanes the same day. One ran `pkill -f "ctest --no-tests=error"`, a pattern matching **every** worktree's run rather than its own; another had its `ctest` SIGTERM'd twice mid-run — once at **265/266 with exit 143**, once killed before any exit line was written at all. Both disclosed it, which is the only reason either is recordable. **The cost is not the lost run, it is the hour spent on a defect that does not exist:** a signalled `ctest` yields a partial pass count, or a non-zero exit that reads as a genuine failure. The second form is the nastier one, because there is no exit code to be suspicious of — the run simply ends. **Never `pkill -f` on a pattern that could match another worktree's process: scope to your own PID tree, or match on your worktree path.** **Before believing a red `ctest`, confirm the run was not signalled — exit 143 is SIGTERM, not a test failure, and a run with no exit line at all was killed rather than completed.** Cross-reference `pgrep -x ctest` before concluding the box is idle, remembering that `pgrep -f` over-counts by matching its own shell (trap 135) and that a padded `ps` column can under-count. Same family as **trap 192**: a shared-path failure whose text accuses the wrong subsystem — there an HLE or loader defect, here a test failure — on the same shared-box substrate of one build directory and two `ctest` invocations. Trap 196 is the sibling case where the apparatus reports a stall that is not there. |
