| 198 | **A checker's own test fixtures written into `$TMPDIR`, filtered by a rule that reads the ABSOLUTE path — so the CALLER's directory name decides what the checker can see** | `check_diag_gates.py` excluded build trees with `if "build-" in str(path)`, a substring test on the absolute path, while its `--selftest` writes fixtures into `tempfile.TemporaryDirectory()`, i.e. under `$TMPDIR`. This charter tells every agent to put `TMPDIR` next to the build, and the build directory the rest of it names is `prosper/build-linux`. So following that guidance discarded **every fixture**: all twelve positive arms printed `got=[]` under the banner *"the scanner's own signatures are broken"*, while the twelve **must-not-match arms passed vacuously** — loud in the half that cannot mislead, silent in the half that can. Three lanes hit it within five minutes on three unrelated tasks, and one read its `259/260, 1 failed` as a real regression in a diagnostic gate. It reproduces only inside a full `ctest` run, where the harness sets `TMPDIR`, and passes under `ctest -R diag_gate_selftest` by hand — which reads as flakiness rather than as an environment dependence. The trigger was one keystroke wide: the recipe as literally written said `build/tmpdir`, no hyphen, and was safe. **A path filter is a statement about a subtree, so express it as components RELATIVE to the root you own** — an absolute-path substring cannot distinguish "inside the build tree" from "underneath a directory that happens to be named like one", and the second is the caller's business, not the tool's. Two further rules the fix carries. A self-test whose fixtures resolve to **zero collected files** must report APPARATUS rather than report its arms, because "no findings" is also exactly what a broken signature produces. And the regression arm must construct the hostile path **by hand** rather than by setting `TMPDIR`: `tempfile` falls back to `/tmp` without a word when `$TMPDIR` names a directory that does not exist, so an arm built out of the environment goes green on an environment it never had — trap 122's rule, applied to a fixture root. #2658, #2660, #2661. |
| 199 | **A tool's refusal to arm itself, printed once at the top of a long log and read as an armed instrument** | GTA V, 2026-08-18. `PROSPER_CFG_TRIP_BOUND=N` plus `_PROGRAM=` emits **no bound at all** without the third variable `_PHASE`, and says so exactly once via `std::call_once`: *"PROSPER_CFG_TRIP_BOUND_PHASE is REQUIRED and is unset: no bound emitted"*. In a 1,200-line filtered run log that single line is functionally invisible. Three runs were made, analysed and written up as bounded; none of them had a bound — they differed only by an unrelated flag, so an A/B that read as bounded-vs-unbounded was **unbounded on both sides**, and a "the bound changes nothing" conclusion was drawn from two identical arms. The requirement was **also** already stated in `docs/GTA5_STATUS.md`'s own env table, so the failure was an unread warning rather than a missing one — twice over. Distinct from trap 172, which is about that same witness's field *labels*: this one is about whether the witness exists. There is a **fourth** silence behind it: the refusal notice is emitted *after* the `_PROGRAM` filter, so a mistyped address yields neither a bound nor a notice — while the dispatch-map print is gated on the bound variable alone and fires for every program the dispatcher lowers, which is precisely what makes the log read as armed. **Grep for a tool's own refusal line before trusting an arm**, and do not read a neighbouring line's presence as evidence the arm took; a `call_once` notice is silent at log scale. |
| 200 | **A control read before it reached the stage it controls, manufacturing a discriminator out of nothing** | GTA V, 2026-08-18. The guest flip counter read `present/frame` **1.00** for the `SKIP` arm against 0.22–0.32 for the others at t=60 s — apparently clean evidence that the declined compute program caused the stall, and it was written up as such. Run to completion the same arm reads **0.19 with a 300 s contiguous stall**: indistinguishable. The entire difference was that the control had not yet reached the stalling stage. In the pair measured, both arms lost the device — at different times, from different programs — and the stall merely follows the loss. Two other runs declining `0x413dc6700` alone did not lose it. Whether that is nondeterminism or simply a window that ended before the next program's loss is **not established** — neither of those runs finished the route, and this title's own status doc holds that a zero-loss run which is not a route completion does not close the search. **A partial trace is not a weaker version of the full one — it can point the opposite way**, and a control is only a control once it has passed the phenomenon it is controlling for. |
| 201 | **A reporter that speaks on one branch only, whose silence spans several different states** | GTA V, 2026-08-18. `[structured-wave-reject]` fires when `exact_compute_wave_cfg && !structured_compute_wave_cfg`. Its silence for a program therefore means the decision was never reached, **or** was reached with `exact_wave` false, **or** was reached and structured emission *succeeded* — and the third would overturn the mechanism the whole investigation assumed. A device loss was spent on a probe designed around that null, which could not discriminate between the three. The question was answerable without any run: `emitted_loop_trip_bound()` has a single call site, inside `emit_cfg_state_machine`, so an already-recorded witness count in the title's own status doc proved dispatcher emission. **A diagnostic that cannot report its positive outcome cannot be read negatively** — and before designing an experiment around a null, check what a null could mean. Filed as #2702. |
| 202 | **A first-wins classifier over a multi-entry collection, naming the first failing reason rather than the reason belonging to the entry that was actually rejected** | GTA V, 2026-08-19. The sampled-depth bridge's miss classifier set `extent_bad` from **any** mismatching sibling at an address and tested it before every validity reason, so `0x20945c0000` reported `extent (T# wants 1024x1536, retained image is 3840x2160)` **1403 times** while the cache simultaneously held a 1024x1536 `D32_SFLOAT` entry **at that very address**. One address routinely holds several entries — a live surface plus a stale sibling at another size — so the two reasons differ as a matter of course, not rarely. The true reason was `depth-invalid`. **The mislabel cost a whole investigation**: an issue filed, chased, and self-corrected twice before the classifier itself was read. Two things made it durable. It was **specific** — it named both extents and a plausible mismatch — and specificity reads as having been checked, the same authority-transfer `CLAUDE.md` records for a cited claim. And it pointed **away from the record that would have stopped it**: `depth-invalid` is falsified ground in `GTA5_STATUS.md` with a strong arm behind it, while `extent` was unexplored, so the wrong label was also the more interesting one. A second, independent copy of the same defect sat in the detail string, which keyed off `extent_bad` rather than the classified reason — so the printed text could contradict the counter it was filed under. **When a lookup iterates several candidates, the reported reason must belong to the candidate that lost, not to the first one that failed any test** — and a diagnostic whose reason and whose counter are computed from different expressions will drift. Fixed in #2724, whose regression arm reproduces the production string character for character; the classification is now a pure function so it is testable without arming the env var that gates the log (which trips `cached_env_arming_logic`). Nearest relative is **trap 199**: there the instrument never armed, here it armed and answered confidently with the wrong field. |
| 203 | **A live run-log filter whose regex anchors on a tag's closing bracket, dropping the `-suffix` variant of that tag — so input the guest demonstrably received reads as input never delivered** | Asterix & Obelix: Babylon Mission, 2026-08-19. This charter requires long run logs to be filtered as they stream (`PROSPER_FILELOG` alone reaches gigabytes, and `/tmp` is a shared RAM tmpfs), so nearly every lane writes one of these patterns. The filter contained `\[pad\]`, which matches `[pad] …` and **cannot** match `[pad-script] …` — and `[pad-script]` is the tag `PROSPER_PAD_SCRIPT_LOG=1` uses for the one thing being verified, the state transitions the guest actually observed (`src/hle/input/hle_pad.cpp:340`; the plain `[pad]` tag belongs to `padlog()`, gated on the *different* env var `PROSPER_PADLOG`, `hle_pad.cpp:81-82`). The filtered log therefore showed **zero** pad lines for a run in which **278** transitions occurred, 63 of them the button the route turned on. The obvious reading is that the route never loaded — which sends the next hour into a mechanism that is working, and, worse, invites 'fixing' it. **What made the null credible was that the rest of the filter worked**: the same pattern captured the scene-load sequence perfectly, so the log looked healthy and complete, and only the one absent signal was absent. That is trap 122's shape at the level of the log filter — the loud, working part of an instrument launders the silence of the broken part. Three rules. **Write tag patterns open-ended** (`\[pad`, not `\[pad\]`), because a tag family with a hyphenated variant is the norm here, not the exception. **Before believing that a diagnostic produced nothing, grep the source for the string it actually prints** rather than the string you would have named it — `CLAUDE.md`'s standing rule that absence from grep is not evidence applies to your own filter first. And **prefer a filter that can report its own emptiness**: a per-tag count printed at the end of the run turns a silent zero into a visible one, which is the difference between a null you can act on and a null that acts on you. |
| 204 | **A census that reports only at power-of-two totals and never at exit — so a program that starts failing LATE is missing from its own inventory** | UE4 survey, 2026-08-19. `PROSPER_COMPUTE_PROGRAM_CENSUS=1` prints only when the running total is an exact power of two and at least 512 (`gpu_executor.cpp:2114`), with no teardown report, so a run's last block is the last boundary it crossed and everything after is discarded — up to 65,535 decisions between 65,536 and 131,072. *Little Nightmares III*'s volumetric-fog program `0x30114c0000` appears in **73** `[compute-table]` lines and in **zero** census rows of a 316 s arm, because its first skip landed past 65,536; a 501 s arm of the same route on the same binary put it in the census at `executed=0 skipped=172`. The failure is silent and one-directional — it can only under-report, and it under-reports exactly the programs belonging to the phase you routed to. It also makes two arms of one A/B incomparable for reasons unrelated to the change: a 70k-decision and a 130k-decision run both report "at 65,536". Filed as #2746; the adjacent filter gap is #2745. |
| 205 | **A buffer size read as a resource IDENTITY — the froxel factorisation of a full-screen buffer is exact, so the arithmetic "confirms" whichever reading you brought to it** | UE4 survey, 2026-08-19. A UE volumetric-fog froxel volume is `(W/16) x (H/16) x 64 x 8` bytes, which for a 4K view is `16,588,800` — and that is **also exactly** `3840 x 2160 x 2`, a full-screen 2 B/px image. Likewise `7,372,800` is `160x90x64x8` **and** `2560x1440x2`, and `8,294,400` is `240x135x32x8` **and** `3840x2160x1`. The survey's detector flagged *Sonic Racing: CrossWorlds* `0x25c0056e00` as fog on the size alone; it is a **reduction**, cleared by the rest of the evidence — a flat 1-D 69,120-thread dispatch whose two storage outputs are **512 bytes**, where fog writes a volume of its own input size. **Identify a resource from the dispatch shape and the whole binding set (N sampled inputs plus one same-sized storage output), never from one number that factorises the way you hoped.** #2747. |
| 206 | **A shell script edited while it is running — bash reads a script incrementally, so the edit lands as a byte-offset shift and mangles a command line that already ran correctly for minutes** | UE4 survey, 2026-08-19. A 342 s `PPSA05143` census run was in flight when its driver was edited to widen an unrelated title's window. Bash resumed the file at its old offset, parsed the tail of `--allow-guest-fault > "$RUN/run.log" 2>&1` as a fresh command (`line 58: -fault: command not found`), and **the mis-parsed redirect truncated the 64 KB run log the completed run had just written** — the census output was gone while 35 screenshots and a healthy manifest remained, so the artifact set looked fine. Nothing warns. **Freeze a copy of any driver before launching it** (`cp driver.sh .driver-$$.sh && ./.driver-$$.sh`), or do not touch it until the run exits. |
| 207 | **A containerised run that never started, whose empty result reads as a clean negative** | `distrobox enter` (podman) fails with `Error: beginning transaction: database is locked` when another agent on the box is using podman, and then *offers to create the container* — so the wrapper sits on a `[Y/n]` prompt and the command inside it never runs. Measured 2026-08-20 while verifying #2734: a positive control seeded a foreign save payload, ran a 200 s `boot_trace`, and reported the file **unchanged** — which is exactly what the PASSING outcome looks like. The emulator had not executed a single instruction. *Recognition cue:* **the artifact you are about to interpret is the one you wrote yourself, and "nothing happened to it" is the result you were hoping for.** Check the launcher's exit status before the artifact, every time: a real 200 s capped run exits **124** (the `timeout` cap), while this failure exits **1** within seconds. A second tell is an output file that does not exist at all rather than existing and being short. Two mitigations, both cheap: bound any container run with `timeout` and assert the 124, and prefer an arm whose PASS is a positive change ("the file I expected to be written exists") over one whose pass is an absence. On this box the container-built binaries also run directly on the host for CPU-only work such as `boot_trace`, which is the fastest way around a contended podman. #2734, #2759 |
| 208 | **A `CLAUDE.md` claim quoted from the session's own context snapshot, which is loaded once at start-up and never reloads — so the charter an agent "reads" can be a fortnight older than the one on disk** | Four agents, 2026-08-20. All four independently reported that this file's docs-gate recipe spells the removed `--sequential`; `origin/master` line 533 has spelled `--ordered --baseline` since #2089, and its only `--sequential` is the line recording the removal. Two routes produced the same false claim, and **the second survives every obvious fix**. (1) The shared main checkout sat **438 commits / 13 days behind**, and its copy genuinely did say `--sequential`; a worktree taken from the stale local `HEAD` inherits it. Fixed at source by fast-forwarding — but that does not reach agents already running. (2) **The snapshot itself.** One reviewer created every worktree explicitly from `origin/...` refs, and its own working tree already said `--ordered`: it was quoting *context*, not any file. It had even run `git show origin/master:CLAUDE.md` in an earlier round — **the 37 KB output was truncated to a preview**, so it read the preview and fell back on the snapshot. The cost is not the flag. It is that the claim arrives with a reviewer's authority, aimed at an author's evidence: one round asked an author to re-quote a `--ordered` result that was already correct, and the only `--sequential` invocation anywhere near that PR was the reviewer's own, which **exited 2 having checked nothing** — the `ctest`-that-ran-no-tests class, inside the very check meant to catch it. **Verify at the moment of citation, not at session start or worktree creation, and make the check targeted so it cannot be truncated:** `git show origin/master:CLAUDE.md \| grep -n -- '--ordered\|--sequential'`. Never `git show` a large file and read its preview. Nearest relatives: trap 41 (taking a whole file from a stale branch) and this file's own rule that a cited claim reads as a verified one — here the stale source is the context window rather than the disk, which is why re-reading the repository does not clear it. |
| 209 | **A rate-limited log sampling a low-duty-cycle signal reports its ABSENCE with perfect consistency — and "the guest never sees our input" is the most load-bearing negative a route derivation can get wrong** | *Sonic Frontiers* route work, 2026-08-20. `PROSPER_PADLOG` prints pad calls 0-7 and then every 512th (`hle_pad.cpp:83-91`). A route pressing a button for 4 flips out of every 200 has a ~2% duty cycle, so every sampled line read `connected=1 buttons=0x0`, over 3,584 calls, across two arms. That is exactly what a broken input path looks like, and the conclusion on offer — prosper is not delivering scripted input to this title — would have sent the lane into `hle_pad.cpp` instead of into the menu. Input was fine: the samples and the presses simply never coincided. **The control is a hand-built maximum-duty instance of the same class, not more of the same route:** one entry, `f1000-4000:cross`, made every sample from call#1024 to call#3584 read `buttons=0x4000`. Note that no amount of *additional* low-duty pressing would have produced it — a second route arm draws from the same generator and inherits the same duty cycle, which is this file's same-source-positive-control failure in its cheapest form. *Recognition cue:* a periodic instrument, a bursty subject, and a negative you are about to act on. Check the instrument's sampling rule against the subject's duty cycle **before** believing the zero. #1891 |
| 210 | **The `CLAUDE.md` in an agent's own context — it is whatever the shared checkout held when the session started, and the rules most likely to be stale are the ones recently corrected because they were wrong** | Measured 2026-08-21. The session's project instructions carried the pre-#2089 docs-gate recipe `check_numbered_table.py --sequential …`; running it errors with `THE CHECK DID NOT RUN`, so I filed it as a charter defect (#2828). It is not one. Master's `CLAUDE.md` already says `--ordered`, already passes `--baseline`, already states **"Gaps are legal"** and already cites #2089 — and is more thorough than the fix I proposed. The `--sequential` text came from the **shared main checkout at `f7cfaf50`**, ten hours and several hundred commits behind master's `70d879ed`; that checkout is what gets loaded as the project instructions. **The trap is that running the command CONFIRMS the false conclusion.** The flag really is dead and the charter really does say `--ordered`; both are true simultaneously because they are two different versions of one file, so an author who verifies by execution gets a genuine error that appears to corroborate the report. Verification by *doing* is normally the strong move, and here it is precisely what launders the mistake — the check that would have worked is the cheap textual one. This is the charter's own "check a claim against the source, never against anything downstream of the thing being checked" rule, hit while writing a bug report about the charter. *Recognition cue:* **a rule that looks wrong, in a file nobody edited on your branch.** A charter is inherited without verification by design — that is what it is for — so staleness in it is silent and the agent follows it faithfully and is wrong. *Fix:* before acting on a surprising `CLAUDE.md` rule, and always before reporting one as a defect, run `git show origin/master:CLAUDE.md` and diff it against the session's copy; treat the checkout's age (`git -C <shared> log -1 --format=%ci HEAD` against `origin/master`) as the staleness bound for every rule you inherited. #2828 |
| 211 | **A screenshot interval that DIVIDES the title's own cycle period, read as a frozen title** | *Earthion* (`PPSA28061`), 2026-08-21. A 900 s probe sampled every 12 s reported the *identical* narration frame seven consecutive times — same CRC, same content metrics, same bounding box — with every other frame black. The natural reading is that the title is stuck on one screen. It is not: the picture cycles with a **~60 s period**, and 12 s divides 60, so the grid only ever visits five phases and one of them happened to hold that frame. Every sample was correct; the *set* of samples was structurally incapable of showing the other 55 seconds. The tell is the one that generalises: **content recurring at an exact multiple of your sampling interval is a statement about the instrument until you have re-measured off-grid.** Two cheap defences, both applicable to any `tools/screenshot` sweep: choose an interval that is not a divisor of any plausible cycle (a prime number of seconds), and before believing a stable state, re-run at a different interval and check the state survives. Related to the aliasing family only superficially — the failure here is not lost resolution but a *sampled subset that cannot express the phenomenon*, the same shape as trap 122's structurally-inexpressible positive control. Also on this title: the frame CRC distinguishes states that are the same picture, because the renderer-drawn CRT bezel carries a small animated indicator outside the game area — crop to the bezel interior before calling two frames different. #1880 |
| 212 | **A generated document that DOCUMENTS its own format — a selftest asserting on `render()`'s whole output matches the worked example in the HEADER, not the row it is checking** | Writing the `FPS record:` column for `PROGRESS_TRACKER.md`, 2026-08-21. `gen_progress_tracker.py`'s `render()` returns HEADER + table + FOOTER, and the header I had just written explains the new column with a worked example: `**3.4** / 59.8 fps · 4K · gameplay · screenshot · 2026-08-21`. My selftest asserted `"**3.4** / 59.8 fps" in render([record])`. It passed — and it passed **unchanged** under a mutation that made the cell print only the presented rate, which is the single reading that column exists to prevent (#2783). The absence arms were void the same way: the needles `\| - \|` and `\| none \|` are produced by the Guard, Oracle and Open-blockers columns of the very fixture under test. Three arms, all green, all measuring prose. *Recognition cue:* **a substring assertion over the output of a function that also emits documentation, a legend, boilerplate or an example.** The better the documentation, the more reliably the test passes for free — and it generalises past docs to any needle that also appears in a header, footer, log preamble or `--help` string. *Fix:* extract the cell **by position** (split the data row on the column separator, take the ordinal) and compare it whole, then re-run the mutation to prove the arm moved. Note what caught it: not review and not reading — the mutation arm. Read back, all three arms looked entirely reasonable. #2843 |
| 213 | **`pkill -x <name>` — the form recommended as the SAFER alternative to `pkill -f`. It fixes matching accuracy and does nothing about scope, so it still kills every lane's process of that name** | 2026-08-21, disclosed by the lane that did it. A Plucky Squire lane ran `pkill -x screenshot` to clear its own run and killed a **concurrent Earthion lane's** run mid-flight — a coprime-interval sweep at 36 of 64 frames. That sweep was the arm that would have settled whether the title's ~60 s cycle is an attract loop failing to draw or a sequence restarting; without it the Earthion lane recorded that question at `CONFIDENCE: MED` and handed it on. **The lost measurement is the cost, and it landed on a different lane than the one that paid it.** The trap is that `-x` is exactly what trap 197 and the accumulated guidance point you toward: `-f` over-matches on substrings and self-matches its own shell (trap 135), so `-x` reads as the careful choice. It *is* more accurate — and accuracy is a different property from **ownership**. `-x screenshot` means "every process named screenshot on this box", which on a shared box with three lanes is three runs. Neither flag has any notion of whose process it is. *Recognition cue:* **any kill whose predicate mentions only WHAT the process is and never WHOSE it is.** *Fix:* scope to your own PID tree (`pkill -P $$`, a stored PID from `$!`, or a process group you started with `setsid`), or match on your own worktree path (`pkill -f "$PWD"` after confirming `$PWD` is your worktree). Before any kill on this box, run `pgrep -a -x <name>` and read the argv of every hit: if more than one appears and they do not all point into your tree, you are about to take someone else's run. Same family as 197, narrowed to the case where the recommended mitigation for 197 is itself the hazard. #2839 |
| 214 | **`tools/screenshot --timeout` cannot bound a BOOT, so a title that hangs in `boot_program()` must be killed from outside — and an externally killed run looks exactly like a defect** | SGW Contracts 2 lane (`PPSA03130`), 2026-08-21. A first boot attempt produced no `[shot]` line, an empty manifest and a five-line log ending at renderer registration, with the outer shell `timeout` firing before the tool's own `--timeout 300`. That reads as "the guest is stalled" or "the tool is broken"; it is neither. `screenshot.cpp` calls `register_live_renderer()`, then `boot_program(...)`, and only then enters the `while (saved < count)` sampling loop where the deadline is checked — **so the tool's `--timeout` is structurally unreachable until `boot_program()` returns.** And `boot_program()` ends by calling `run_guest_inits()`, which executes the guest's own module initialisers, so a title can sit inside it indefinitely. Two consequences. **Set the outer and inner limits to DIFFERENT values and quote which one fired**: the tool's own `[shot] done:` / `[shot] timeout after Ns` lines are the only proof it reached an exit path, and a run with neither has been killed, so its silence is not evidence (same family as traps 197 and 213). **And the phases are now visible**: `PROSPER_BOOTPHASE=1` prints one stderr line per boot phase, so the last line names the phase a stalled boot is inside — which turned this lane's "no output at all" into "deadlocked in `GUEST_INITS_RUNNING` at +81 ms" in a single run. Note the diagnostics that `printf` to **stdout** (`linked N modules ...`) are block-buffered when redirected and are lost entirely when the run is killed; the phase log goes to stderr and flushes per line for exactly that reason. #2867. |
| 215 | **A symlink farm built to A/B one file silently invalidates prosper's bounded-file reads, inventing two defects that belong to the apparatus** | SGW Contracts 2 lane, 2026-08-21. To test whether one bundled PRX was wedging the boot, the lane built a dump tree of symlinks to the real dump with that single file omitted — zero-copy, and the right shape of experiment. The run then reported `[appparam] no usable sce_sys/param.json (unreadable, oversized or malformed)`, `[addcontent] invalid install manifest`, and `[savedata] this application declares no usable titleId`. **None of those are true of the dump.** `hle_addcontent.cpp:130` walks each path component with `symlink_status` and rejects **any** symlink outright — deliberately, so a dangling or redirected manifest cannot authorise foreign add-content — so a symlinked `sce_sys/` or `dlc_emu.ini` reads as malformed. The trap is that the messages are *specific and plausible*: they name real parse failures, and a lane chasing a rendering defect will happily write "this title's param.json is malformed" into a status doc. *Recognition cue:* **a defect that appears only in the arm you constructed, in a subsystem your variable has nothing to do with.** *Fix:* make every path component of `sce_sys/param.json` and `dlc_emu.ini` a real file (copy them; they are tiny) and symlink only the bulk assets — or better, prove the fix in the loader and re-run on the untouched dump, which is the only arm that is evidence about the title at all. #2867. |
| 216 | **A byte counter reporting "no I/O" during heavy I/O — `/proc/<pid>/io`'s `read_bytes` is BLOCK-DEVICE traffic, so a warm page cache reads it as an idle process** | SGW Contracts 2 lane, 2026-08-21. A three-way sampler (CPU climbing = executing, bytes climbing = starved, neither = stuck) was validated on three hand-made controls and worked. Then it called a healthy run **STUCK**: `cpu+0.14s`, `disk-read+0.00MB`, 18 threads all sleeping, over several 20 s windows. The reading was reinforced by a detail that made it persuasive rather than suspicious — exactly one thread, Wwise's `AK::BankManager`, sat in `k_mutex_lock` while every other thread waited on a condition or a semaphore, which is what a deadlock with a named culprit looks like. **It was neither stuck nor deadlocked.** The run resumed and reached a 4K present loop ~30 s later; the earlier run of the same title had done the same at t≈229 s. `read_bytes` counts bytes fetched **from the block device**, and the second run's assets were already in the page cache from the first, so a process reading hard showed zero. The tell was in the same sampler, in the column nobody was reading: `syscr` — read *syscalls* — climbed 38 → 378 → 680 per 20 s while `read_bytes` stayed flat at 0. *Recognition cue:* **a "no I/O" verdict on a second run of anything you just ran**, or on any box with RAM to spare. *Fix:* treat `read_bytes` as a *lower* bound on I/O and never as the liveness signal; key liveness on `syscr`/`syscw` plus CPU delta, and print all three so the disagreement is visible rather than averaged into one verdict. Generalises the family in traps 122 and 190: this instrument was loud, self-consistent, and validated — its controls all passed, because none of them had a warm cache. A control that cannot reproduce the subject's *state* validates the machinery, not the domain. #2867. |
| 217 | **`gdb`'s backtrace through GUEST frames is fiction — no CFI, so it stack-scans, and the plausible-looking chain it prints can name a function that cannot be on the stack** | Khazan (`PPSA20447`) lane, 2026-08-22. A boot stalled with all 79 threads blocked in a syscall. `gdb -p <pid> -batch -ex 'thread apply all bt'` gave the guest main thread as `k_usleep` <- `eboot+0x7964f09` <- `eboot+0x15657da`, which reads like a call chain and is not one: **`eboot+0x7964ee0`, the function containing `0x7964f09`, has no sleep call anywhere in it** — it calls one function, tests the result against `-1`, and returns. It is the function the loop CALLS at `0x796eb5f`, one instruction before the `sceSaveDataGetEventResult` call -- a callee that had already **returned**, whose frame gdb then scanned and reported as a caller. Stale stack residue reported as a call chain. Host frames in the same backtrace were correct and symbolised, which is what makes it convincing: the top four frames (`__syscall_cancel_arch` -> `nanosleep` -> `prosper::k_usleep`) are real, so the two below them inherit their credibility. *Recognition cue:* **any `??? ()` frame under a prosper symbol** — gdb has run out of unwind information exactly there and everything past it is a guess. *Fix:* do not read guest frames off gdb. Scan the HLE handler's own frame for guest code addresses instead (`__builtin_frame_address(0)` + `prosper::guest_va_in_module_code`, the technique `hle_file.cpp`'s `[apr] guest-ra[N]` diagnostic already uses); it filters by range rather than guessing, is stable across repeats, and here produced a chain whose first frame disassembled straight into the real wait loop. Cheap corroboration that a chain is real: print it on several separate calls and require it to be identical. #2908, #2909. |
| 218 | **`grep -r` without `-a` returns a confident, clean ZERO on a pattern that is present -- binary files are skipped silently, so a whole-corpus census reads as a negative result** | Khazan (`PPSA20447`) lane / PR #2910 review, 2026-08-22. A blast-radius census asked which of 54 game dumps import the NID `j8xKtiFj0SY`. `grep -rl 'j8xKtiFj0SY' PPSA*-app0/` returned **zero hits across every dump**; the NID is in **22** of them. `grep -c` on one eboot prints an empty line where `grep -ac` prints `1`. Nothing warns: there is no "binary file matches" notice with `-l`/`-c`, the exit status is an ordinary "no match", and the result is exactly the shape a real negative has. The same census then failed a **second** way for a reason `-a` does not fix: it globbed `eboot.bin` only, and every Unity title's save code lives in `Media/Plugins/SaveData.prx`, so 16 carriers were structurally invisible -- including four reviewed rung-6 guards (`PPSA24651`, `PPSA13579`, `PPSA02664`, `PPSA25009`). The published figure was 5 of 54. *Recognition cue:* **any grep over a dump, a binary, or a `.prx` that returns a round zero** -- and any census whose file list is a single well-known filename. *Fix:* always `-a` when grepping dumps, enumerate `eboot.bin` **and** every `.prx`, and **validate the instrument on a known-positive before believing a zero** -- one dump you have already proven carries the pattern turns a silent-skip into an immediate, loud failure. This is the trap-122 family with the loudness removed: not a control that passed for the wrong reason, but no control at all behind a number that looked decisive. #2910. |
| 219 | **A reject line that prints the DECODER'S DEFAULTS for the field it is refusing — `[recompile-reject] ... sdwa=6/0/6/6` described an instruction that was not there** | Beast of Reincarnation lane (`PPSA29343`), 2026-08-22. The title's scanout pixel shader was rejected on `words=7e040ef9,00061402 fmt=7 op=0x7 ... modifier=1 dpp=0 sdwa=6/0/6/6`. `6/0/6/6` is `dst_sel=DWORD, dst_unused=PAD, src0_sel=DWORD` — a **semantically inert** SDWA wrapper — so the line reads as "the wrapper does nothing, therefore the opcode itself is unimplemented", and `v_cvt_u32_f32` is not a plausible thing to be missing. Both halves were wrong. `llvm-mc -arch=amdgcn -mcpu=gfx1030 -disassemble` on the two printed dwords gives `v_cvt_u32_f32_sdwa v2, v2 dst_sel:WORD_0 dst_unused:UNUSED_PRESERVE src0_sel:DWORD` — `4/2/6`. The opcode was implemented; the **WORD-destination shape** was not. The mechanism: `decode_operands`' VOP1 SDWA arm assigns `sdwa_dst_sel`/`sdwa_dst_unused`/`sdwa_src0_sel` only inside the per-opcode branches that **accept** a shape, so those fields do double duty as "what the instruction says" and "what the recompiler agreed to model", and a refused instruction keeps the struct defaults — which the reject printf then prints as though they had been decoded. *Recognition cue:* **any diagnostic field that is also a decision, printed on the path where the decision was NO.** The give-away here is that the same line carries `modifier=1` (a modifier word is present) beside selects that say the modifier does nothing — an internal contradiction, if you read both. *Fix:* re-derive the fields from `in.words[1]` at the reject site for display only, and leave the accept-only assignment alone — relaxing the decoder is what keeps `has_modifier` honest. Same family as trap 122: the instrument was loud and specific, and the specificity is what made it credible. #2912, #2917. |
| 220 | **A negative input-gating result, read as a property of the TITLE rather than of the DEPTH it was measured at -- and measured on an ASSET ORACLE, so it could not have seen the thing it was quoted against** | Sonic Origins lane (`PPSA05325`), 2026-08-22. On 2026-08-06 a ~12,000-read input probe was run against this title and recorded, correctly, that its post-logo state *is not input-gated*: the probe arm resolved the same 162 APR paths as an input-free arm and neither ever requested `ui_mainmenu*`, `stage_title*` or any `.rsdk`. That row then sat in `GRIS_SONIC_COBRA_BRINGUP.md` for sixteen days as the reason nobody re-tried input, while three fixes (#2571, #2901, #2910) moved the boot four screens further on. On current master a **default launch reaches the game's own "This title supports auto save." modal at t=200 s and holds it to the end of a 420 s run** -- ten consecutive samples at a per-pixel mean delta of 0.03-0.12/255, `guest=running`, 39 of 42 samples pixel-distinct. Its only control is a `[X] Close` button. **One `cross` reaches the title screen, 2 of 2 runs.** Two compounding faults, and the second is the transferable one. (a) The finding was scoped to a state that no longer existed, and nothing in its wording said so. (b) Its instrument was a **resolved-path set**, not frames -- so it could only ever answer "did input change what the loader asks for", and a modal that is fully loaded and merely waiting changes nothing about the loader. The oracle it was quoted against (`stage_title.pac`, `ui_mainmenu.pac`) is *downstream* of the button, so its absence was the predicted consequence of not pressing it, re-read as proof that pressing it would not help. *Recognition cue:* **a negative result about input whose evidence is anything other than pixels, and any `## Ruled out` row about input that does not name the boot depth it was taken at.** *Fix:* date-and-depth every negative input row, and re-run it after any fix that moves the boot; when frames are still changing, the wall is not a wall. Fourth title here where input mapping impersonated a rendering wall, after `PPSA30490` (skip was Triangle), `PPSA19991` (OPTIONS gates, Yes/No defaulting to No) and Hi-Fi RUSH (confirm cursor starts on the right-hand option). #1871, #2919, #2920. |
| 221 | **A grep for a log tag that the code does not print, whose clean zero was reported as a property of the TITLE** | pad-routes lane (BALAN WONDERWORLD, `PPSA02058`), 2026-08-22. Asked whether BALAN's opening cutscene was engine-rendered or a decoded movie, the lane grepped the run log for `videodec\|videodec2\|sofdec\|criware\|ajm` **as bracketed log tags**, got nothing back for the video ones, and wrote *"the boot logs no video decode of any kind, so these are real GPU draws of a real 3D scene"* -- into a commit message, `COMPATIBILITY.md`, `BLOG.md`, the survey doc, a tracker comment, a route header and a route README before it was checked. **The tag prosper prints is `[vdec2]`.** The same run log holds two `access-unit H.264 decoder opened (VA-API)` lines, a `first picture: 3840x2160 NV12`, and 3072 access units / 3070 pictures, all beginning at t=125.6 s -- and every frame the lane had called an in-engine 3D scene postdates that line. What caught it was re-running the grep **unanchored**, which returned 664 `criware` and 339 `movie` hits the bracketed form had silently dropped; those turned out to be `.ini` paths, but they proved the pattern, not the title, had produced the zero. *Recognition cue:* **a negative claim resting on a grep whose pattern was written from memory of what the tag "should" be rather than copied from the emitting code** -- and the tell that it is dangerous rather than merely wrong is that the conclusion was the flattering one (in-engine 4K draws is a better result than a decoded movie), so nobody wanted to re-check it. *Fix:* before believing a clean zero from a log grep, `grep -rn` the **emitter** in `src/` for the literal it prints, and run the same pattern against a positive control -- here, any title known to decode video. Same family as traps 122 and 218: the instrument could not express the case, and its silence read as the title's. |
| 222 | **`pgrep -x a b c` — the multi-pattern "is the GPU free?" check — is a USAGE ERROR that never answers the question, and the common `\|\| echo "GPU free"` spelling prints the reassuring answer ON the error** | Shared-box run hygiene, 2026-08-23. `pgrep -x` accepts exactly **one** pattern; given three it prints `pgrep: only one pattern can be provided` to stderr and exits **2**. So `pgrep -x prosper-app screenshot boot_trace` has never once reported whether a peer lane was mid-run — and the two idioms built on it both fail *safe-looking*: `pgrep -x a b c 2>/dev/null; echo "(none above = free)"` prints nothing and reads as a clean box, while `pgrep -x a b c \|\| echo "GPU free"` prints **"GPU free"** precisely because the command failed. Note this is the opposite failure direction from the `ps \| grep` pair in the counting rule above: those miscount a check that *ran*, this one never runs at all, so no amount of scrutinising the number helps — there is no number. *Tell:* the check emits **nothing** on a box where you know something is running; run it once without `2>/dev/null` and the usage error is immediate. *Fix:* one ERE argument — `pgrep -x 'prosper-app\|screenshot\|boot_trace\|screenshot_snap'` — or the `for` loop already given under *Count with `pgrep -x`*, which is correct because it passes one name per call. Include `screenshot_snap` either way (see the note above): a `snapshot.py` run is invisible under the other four names. Recorded because it propagated into roughly ten lane briefs before anyone ran it without stderr suppressed, and because a GPU-free check that cannot fail loudly is worse than no check — it authorises the run it was meant to gate. Cousin of traps 88/100/135: those mislead about *which* process or *how many*, this one silently declines to look. #2948 |
| 223 | **A pass/fail rate that drifts machine-wide over minutes, so an A/B measured as "arm A for N runs, then arm B for N runs" is VOID rather than negative** | renderer-determinism lane (#2945, BALAN WONDERWORLD `PPSA02058`), 2026-08-23. One frozen `.prgbundle` replayed offline by `gpu_replay` fails intermittently, and the failure rate of the **identical command with the identical binary** ranged from **0 of 20** in one ten-minute window to **12 of 12** in the next, and back, all session. Interleaved arms flip together, which is the tell. Six separate "levers" were found and then unfound this way: `PROSPER_SERIAL_DRAW_REALIZE` measured 19/20 stable against a 10/20 baseline and is indistinguishable interleaved; `RADV_DEBUG=zerovram`, `RADV_DEBUG=nodcc` and `PROSPER_NO_BACKEND_PERSISTENT_TEXTURES` each measured 4-distinct-of-10 against a 10-of-10 baseline. What exposed those three was not suspicion but arithmetic: **two different levers produced byte-identical 10-run hash sequences**, which no real mechanism explains. *Recognition cue:* **any A/B on this class where the two arms were not run alternately, and any result whose arms were minutes apart** -- and note the second-order hazard, that a lane which knows the rate drifts will still trust a *large* difference measured sequentially. *Fix:* interleave run-by-run inside one loop, quote n and the modal fraction rather than a distinct-hash count, and re-run the baseline arm in the same loop even when you think you already know it. Same family as trap 122 (a control drawn from the source it validates): here the control is drawn from the same *time window* as the null. #2945. |
| 224 | **`cmake --build --target X` rebuilds ONLY X, so an A/B run against a different frontend executes the PRE-FIX binary — and its unchanged failure reads as a clean falsification of the fix** | Metaphor lane (`PPSA20800`), 2026-08-23. With a one-constant allocator fix in the tree, `--target boot_trace` was used to shorten the rebuild. `boot_trace` then showed the title getting six seconds and 63 frames further — and the next two runs, made with `tools/screenshot` to capture those frames, reproduced the ORIGINAL crash byte-for-byte: same faulting rip, same thread name, same non-canonical pointer, same corrupted memory window. That is exactly what a real falsification looks like, and the arm was seconds away from being written up as one — a lever tested in the same run (`PROSPER_NO_BACKEND_PERSISTENT_TEXTURES=1`) would have been recorded as having *restored* the bug. `screenshot` had simply never been relinked. *Recognition cue:* an A/B whose two arms used **different executables**, and whose "before" arm reproduces the old symptom with suspicious exactness — a genuine partial fix usually moves *something*, while a stale binary reproduces the baseline perfectly because it IS the baseline. *Fix:* after any source change, run `cmake --build <dir>` with **no `--target`** before any run you will quote, or check the frontend's mtime against the object you changed. Sharper than the parenthetical in trap 40 (a restored-but-unrebuilt test source), because here the build SUCCEEDED, the changed target really was rebuilt, and the stale one was a peer executable nothing in the build output mentioned. #2934 |
| 225 | **GPU load from another process INDUCES #2945's failing regime on demand — so a quiet-box null is weak evidence, and the prescribed free-GPU check cannot see the load that matters** | #2945 lane, 2026-08-23. Trap 223 recorded that this defect's rate drifts machine-wide over minutes and prescribed interleaved arms; this gives you a lever over the drift instead of waiting for it. Measured with `tools/vkprobe` on Linux/RADV: three heavy `gpu_replay` full-submit replays flipped a passing box into failing **within one round** of a 3-second detector loop, and it recovered ~15 s after they stopped — repeatable, and a second `vkprobe` used purely as load reproduces it more weakly, so the load need not be prosper. **The converse does NOT hold, and this is the half that was wrong when the row was first written:** an independent reviewer measured 13/20 and 8/20 failing on a box with no `prosper-app`, `screenshot`, `boot_trace`, `gpu_replay` or `vkprobe` running, verified through `/proc/*/fd`. So load is **sufficient, not necessary**, and a clean arm on a quiet box is weak rather than a clearance — the honest reading of any 0-of-n here is *undecided*. *Recognition cue:* an arm whose good half ran on a quiet box and whose bad half did not; and any n quoted without its wall-clock span, since 400 runs inside 173 s are one drift period rather than 400 samples of the thing that varies. *Fix:* put deliberate GPU load beside the measurement, quote the span as well as the n, and pair the arms inside one process so both meet the same window. **And do not trust the free-GPU check to tell you the box is quiet:** `pgrep -x 'prosper-app\|screenshot\|boot_trace'` — even spelled correctly per trap 222 — matches none of `gpu_replay`, `vkprobe`, `ctest` or a peer's test binaries. #2945 |
| 226 | **A correction that ADJUSTS a number instead of re-deriving it inherits the error it is correcting — and arrives wearing a reviewer's authority** | Metaphor lane (`PPSA20800`), 2026-08-23, #2951/#2956. A code comment enumerating an ABI said the guest read **four** fields of a struct. Independent review re-disassembled the range, found a field the list had missed, and corrected the count to **five** — by adding one. Re-review found the range holds **four read instructions covering six distinct 32-bit fields**, because two of the reads are qwords: neither the original number nor the correction counted anything that exists. The author verified the *new field* against the disassembly (correctly, per the charter's citation rule) and did not re-verify the *total*, because a total is not what a citation points at. **The repair is structural, not arithmetic: list the instructions and let the reader count.** A stated total is the thing that drifts; an enumeration cannot. Generalise it — whenever a review corrects a count, a size, a rate or a percentage, re-derive the quantity from the source rather than applying the delta, and prefer publishing the raw observations over the aggregate. |
| 227 | **prosper's `0xaf` poison fill reads as a float that PASSES a near-zero assertion, so a test checking `fabs(x) < eps` cannot tell "written 0" from "never written"** | Metaphor lane (`PPSA20800`), 2026-08-23, #2951/#2956. `0xafafafaf` reinterpreted as IEEE-754 float32 is **-3.196e-10**; as float64, `0xafaf…af` is -5.3e-79. Both sail through any "close to zero" tolerance. A test asserting a glyph landed at the surface origin therefore **passed against a buffer nobody had touched**, and only a mutation arm that removed the writer exposed it. This poison is unusual in that respect and that is what makes it dangerous — every other common fill fails such a check loudly (`0xcd` → -4.3e8, `0xcc` → -1.1e8, `0xdd` → -2.0e18, `0xfe` → -1.7e38, `0xbaadf00d` → -1.3e-3). **Assert on the raw bits, not the decoded float, whenever the field's untouched state is the thing you are ruling out**, and never give a byte-fill sentinel and a float-value sentinel the same name. Recorded again in `docs/VERIFICATION.md` where a test author will meet it. *(The -3.196e-10 above was itself published as "-3.2e-13" in the review, the PR and a shipped code comment before anyone recomputed it — trap 226, same day, same PR.)* |
| 228 | **A fixed READBACK WINDOW over a variable-position record — a CORRECT fetch reads back as all-sentinel and is indistinguishable from "the thing under test never ran"** | #2961 lane (RADV STRIX_HALO), 2026-08-23. `vkprobe`'s index-readback VS stores `records[16 + gl_VertexIndex]`, but the readback was taken at a fixed `--readback-dwords 16:3`. For identity indices that window is right; for `[3,4,5]` a **correct** fetch writes dwords 19–21 — so the fixed window read all-sentinel ×300 and the draw was written up as "RADV hands sequential vertex ordinals; the index buffer is ignored" (issue since closed falsified). The identity-list control could not discriminate: both hypotheses agree on `[0,1,2]`, so it proved the harness *drew* while saying nothing about *fetch*. An independently written bare-Vulkan probe (`tools/index_fetch_probe`, zero shared code) fetched correctly on the same box under Mesa 26.1.4 and 26.2.1, and re-running vkprobe itself with the window moved (`--indices 3,4,5 --readback-dwords 19:3`) printed `[4,5,6]` ×300 — the driver answering correctly through its own accuser. *Tell:* when records are keyed by OBSERVED VALUES, the reader must be positioned by those same values; a window pinned by one hypothesis only ever samples inputs where both hypotheses coincide. *Fix:* derive the window from the list (`--readback-dwords 19:3`), or key records by payload like the probe does — and treat "instrument agrees with me on the cases it was built for" as zero information about the others. |
| 229 | `gpu_replay --bundle` (offline `.prgbundle` replay), used to test an INDEX or VERTEX decode hypothesis | **A bundle replays PRE-DECODED draws, so an instrument upstream of the decoded draw never fires and its silence reads as a result about the guest.** The offline bundle is the project's recommended fast loop for a rendering bug, and for *which draw wrote this pixel* it is exactly right — but index and vertex decode happen before the draw is stored in the bundle. Measured on Tomb Raider (`PPSA16901`, #2990): the same `[idxtype]` diagnostic emitted **0** lines under `--bundle` and **508,688** on a live run of the same route. Nothing in the replay output distinguishes the two cases — it reports `operations=75/75` and a complete temporal closure either way — so the tool looks like it is answering. Test decode-path hypotheses **live**; keep the bundle for questions about the draws themselves. |
| 230 | **A screenshot of a game's own PRE-RENDERED LOADING PICTURE, mistaken for the rendered 3D world** | The most convincing false-progression evidence this project has produced, and it survived a PR, a `BLOG.md` entry and a merge. *Tomb Raider I-III* (`PPSA16901`, #2990) blits a full-screen 1920x1080 picture between levels; a capture of that moment shows Croft Manor through a brick arch in photographic detail — brickwork, leaded windows, the dolphin fountain, grass, individual leaves — and was published as *"Tomb Raider's world is textured"*. It is `2/PIX/HD/MANSION.DDS` blitted to the screen: pixel-compared against the checked-in capture it scores **mean abs diff 0.02/255 with 100.00% of pixels within 8/255**. The world was untextured in the pre-fix "before" shot this was published beside — but that shot came from the SAME commit, so it says nothing about the post-fix state, and a later capture shows textures do reach world surfaces. The blit argument stands on its own and needs no claim about what the world looked like afterwards. **The tells are all in the picture and all misleading in the same direction:** it looks *better* than the emulator could plausibly render, which reads as success rather than as the warning it is — soft shadows, ambient occlusion, depth of field, per-leaf foliage. It was even paired with a genuine untextured 'before' shot, so the A/B looked rigorous while comparing a 3D render against a 2D blit. **Check any unusually good frame against the dump's own picture assets before publishing it**: run `python3 tools/evidence/prerender_check.py <shot> <DUMP_ROOT>`, which does exactly this and exits 2 on a match. **A DOMINATED verdict is not automatically a retraction**: the difference is in the CLAIM, not the pixels. "The world is textured" is an assertion about 3D rendering that a blit cannot support at any overlap; "the title screen renders" is *supported* by a correct blit, because that is how a title screen renders. `tomb-raider-title-screen.png` (95.94% of `TITLE_EU.DDS`) and `tomb-raider-title-screen-tr2.png` (94.87% of `TITLE_US.DDS`) are DOMINATED, correctly captioned, and deliberately kept (the real one scores near zero and nothing else comes close — next best here 50.86; the 52.77 first reported came from an earlier luma metric and is corrected here, since the tool now compares per-channel maxima). A loading screen is rung-1 evidence at most, since displaying a 2D image requires no world rendering at all. |
| 231 | **A claim that arrives already labelled as checked — a reviewer's finding, a cited file:line, a "derivable" bound** | The charter's authority-transfer section records that a cited claim reads as a verified one. This adds what that section does not say: **which claims actually get questioned, and how little that depends on care.** Measured over one review (#3060 / #3077): two of a reviewer's phrases reached the author's tree underived — an analytic bound stated as `63.75` (the true bound is `69.75`; it dropped the matching pixels' own contribution, `0.75 x 8 = 6.00`) and the claim that real assets "cannot saturate" it (a caption panel alone reaches **85%** of the bound, and adding an ordinary colour-conversion shift takes it to **94%**). Both were caught by the same question asked twice about successive revisions of one paragraph — *does the replacement overstate in the other direction?*, then *in some third direction?* — and **not by the ordinary review pass over the same text: the reviewer approved the very commit that ADDED both `63.75` lines (`0e5d1ec6`, twice), two rounds after supplying the number.** A review read the diff introducing the wrong figure and passed it. **The uncomfortable part is what that question was aimed at.** Not the reviewer's numbers: both times it was aimed at the AUTHOR'S OWN rewrite. The reviewer's claims were re-read only because they happened to sit inside a paragraph being rewritten for an unrelated reason. **Two accidents were required: a background probe the reviewer had written off as dead completed anyway, and the error it surfaced happened to sit in the SAME PARAGRAPH as the bound.** The second carries the argument — had that count error been in another file, the bound would never have been re-read. Had the count been right, nobody would have reopened that paragraph and `63.75` would still be in the tree wearing the label *"derivable, belongs to nobody's sample"* — the strongest label a number can carry here, and the one that most discourages anyone from deriving it. So: **(1) ask the adversarial question exactly where a claim arrived pre-labelled as checked**, since that is where it feels least necessary; and **(2) compute any load-bearing quantity twice, by independent routes, and compare — this is the only one of the two that fires without anyone suspecting anything.** A third wrong figure from the same review never propagated at all because its author computed it two ways, got 43.6% and 1.27%, and the disagreement exposed an int16 overflow in their own numpy: caught before publication, where the questions caught theirs two rounds after. The first draft of this entry said three phrases propagated. The record said two. |
| 232 | **A batch edit script that fails ATOMICALLY while reporting PER-EDIT — so a commit message describes work the tree does not contain, and every gate stays green** | #3073 lane, 2026-08-27. A script applied two edits to one file: a comment rewrite and a `SDL_Log` fix. The second `assert` missed its anchor, so Python aborted the whole script *before writing anything* — and the traceback names only the failed anchor. Read as "the second edit did not apply", it is really "neither did". I fixed the log line separately by hand, then committed both under the subject *"scope the mechanism to the measured regime, **and** one record per line"*. Only the second half existed. **Every gate was green and none of them could have seen it:** the build passes (comments), 247/247 tests pass (comments), `diff --check` passes, CI passes, and the diff *is* internally consistent — it simply omits a change the message claims. An independent reviewer caught it by grepping the phrases the author's own report quoted (`while it is behind` 0, `REMAINDER` 0, `keeps up` 0). *Why it matters more than a stale comment:* `git log` then records the correction as **done**, so nobody re-derives it — the exact failure mode the correction was fixing, one layer up, and the commit message is the layer no test reads. *Tells:* a script with more than one `assert`-guarded edit and a single write at the end; a traceback you did not read to the last line; a commit message enumerating changes you applied in a different order or session than the commit. *Fix:* write each edit separately and **verify by the artefact, not the intent** — grep the file for a distinctive phrase from every claim the message makes, which is two commands and is what the reviewer did. Never let a multi-edit script's success message print before its write. |
| 233 | **`tools/screenshot`'s `source-distinct=N` footer, read as "N different PICTURES were published"** | #2932 lane (Unbound `PPSA03274`, RADV STRIX_HALO), 2026-08-29. The run footer prints `source-distinct=80 pixel-distinct=3` beside a present-layer line reading `119 distinct of 27227 published`. Read naturally, those say the renderer published many different frames while the capture only ever saw three — a flat contradiction, and the obvious explanation is that the readback path is reading a stale or wrong buffer. **It is not a contradiction, because the two counters do not measure the same thing.** `source_advanced` is `observation.source != previous_.source \|\| observation.source_seq != previous_.source_seq` (`tools/screenshot/capture_manifest.cpp:186`) — **buffer identity and sequence number, never content**. It goes up once per sample whenever the renderer hands over a fresh buffer, so on any live run it equals the sample count and carries no information at all. Only `pixel-distinct` compares bytes. *Why it is worth a row:* the word `distinct` appears in both lines, one of them IS content-based (`frame_content_signature`, which by contract cannot report identical bytes as new — `present_frame_rate.hpp:109`), and they sit two lines apart in the same footer. I had written the mismatch up as evidence for a harness defect before checking the definition. *Fix:* quote `pixel-distinct`, or the per-sample manifest's cumulative `distinct_frames`, for any claim about content; treat `source-distinct` as a liveness signal only. *And the payoff for reading it right:* that per-sample `distinct_frames` is cumulative and content-based, so its increments DATE each change — which measures a duty cycle directly instead of estimating it from how often a sampling grid happens to land on content. On this title the grid said 9%; the counter said **0.3%**. |
| 234 | **A SAVE DIRECTORY left at the shared default location by an earlier run — the title resumes into it and renders nothing, which is indistinguishable from a composite defect** | #2932 lane (Unbound `PPSA03274`, RADV STRIX_HALO), 2026-08-29. Every routed run of this title went black ~12 s after the first press and stayed black for the remaining 385 s. It survived every check I put on it: the guest was alive and flipping at 29/s, the renderer produced 60 frames/s with the GPU ~90% busy, an F9 bundle reproduced the black frame offline byte-for-byte, and **both present paths agreed** (`tools/screenshot`'s forced readback and `prosper-app`'s shipped GPU present), which is exactly the evidence that rules the harness out and points at the guest. It was a `PPSA03274` directory under `~/.local/share/prosper/savedata0/` dated three days earlier. Matched A/B on one commit: **4 of 20 samples carried content with the shared save, 17 of 20 with a fresh `PROSPER_SAVE0` + `PROSPER_SAVEDATA_DIR`** — and the clean arm plays the entire intro cinematic in full colour. *Why it is vicious:* it is **stateful across runs, lanes, titles and weeks**, so it does not reproduce for whoever wrote the route and does reproduce forever for whoever inherits the box; it is invisible to `git status`, to the run log and to every diagnostic; and it makes a WORKING title look like a broken renderer rather than the reverse. It also manufactures a **phantom regression**: the route README recorded a visible cinematic on an older commit, so I built that commit — and it reproduced the black, because it read the same stale save. Hours went into a bisect range that could never have contained a culprit. *Tell:* a title that renders correctly with NO input but collapses shortly after the first press, wherever that press falls (shift the press, the collapse shifts with it). *Fix:* isolate BOTH save variables per lane, as the DQ7 routes do; and when a route's recorded result will not reproduce, check what the previous run left on disk before suspecting the code. |
| 235 | **Reading LOG LINE ORDER as a timeline — stderr from different threads is interleaved, not ordered** | #3142 (Stray `PPSA02101`), 2026-08-30. I published "the pak read completes **1,428 log lines before** the sample, so the memory is still zero after a successful write" and built a whole investigation on it. Line distance is not a happens-before relation: the APR reader and draw realization are different threads writing to one stderr stream, so their interleaving says nothing about which executed first. The claim had to be withdrawn — and the retraction mattered, because the two candidate mechanisms it conflated ("the data is zeroed after the write" vs "the sample preceded the write") are different defects with different fixes. *Why it is vicious:* 1,428 lines of separation FEELS like overwhelming evidence, and the bigger the gap the more convincing the wrong inference gets; a two-line gap would have been questioned. *Fix:* stamp a shared monotonic reading on both diagnostics — `prosper::diagnostics::diag_now_us()` (`src/diagnostics/diag_clock.hpp`) exists for exactly this and needs no anchor, since `steady_clock` is process-wide. With it the same pair measured **+62.351 s** on the run that produced this trap, and **+65.735 s** on a later re-run with the corrected byte-identity verdict — two runs, not one figure quoted twice, which is the distinction a durable row has to make. Either settles the ordering the log never could. |
| 236 | **A verifier that compares POPULATIONS instead of CONTENT — it reports MATCH on entirely different bytes** | #3144 review, 2026-08-30. `PROSPER_APR_VERIFY` read a guest destination back and compared it against its source by counting non-zero dwords per window and testing `src_nz == dst_nz`. That is not the property anyone wanted: a destination holding completely different bytes reports MATCH whenever the totals agree, and a loss in one window cancels against a gain in another. On the strength of it I published "byte-exact when written" on #3142 — a claim the instrument had never tested. Caught in review, not by any run, because **every arm was green**: 21,217/21,217 MATCH, and the positive control fired correctly, since zeroing a window does change its count. *Why it is vicious:* a population check passes every test you would think to write for it, including a mutation arm — the mutation has to be content-preserving to expose it, which is precisely the case nobody constructs. It is also strictly weaker than the obvious alternative while looking more thorough, because it prints numbers. *Fix:* `memcmp` when both buffers are in hand, and keep the counts only as detail. Related: a zero-valued poison in a control is undetectable wherever the source is already zero — poison with a pattern, and assert the poison landed. |
| 237 | **A PROFILING HARNESS whose own readback is the workload you are measuring — `tools/screenshot` inflated tiling work 25x** | #3150 (Stray `PPSA02101`), 2026-08-31. A `PROSPER_TILECENSUS` sweep of the title-screen phase showed 158,381 MiB of tiling with `tile_surface` (writeback) apparently dominating at ~41%, which reads as a second, writeback-bound bottleneck sitting behind the one being fixed. It is the harness. `tools/screenshot` never calls `set_gpu_present_active`, so it copies every scanout frame back to the CPU, and that readback generates the tiling it then measures. The same phase under `prosper-app` (GPU-present, the shipped path) moves **6,254 MiB** — 25x less — and the row under investigation is unchanged between them. **A census taken under `tools/screenshot` is a census of `tools/screenshot`**; re-measure any tiling, copy, or readback figure under `prosper-app` before believing it, and note this is the same harness the charter already warns about for FPS. The finding was one command from being filed as an issue. |
| 238 | **A LINKER KILLED MID-WRITE leaves a truncated binary with a FRESH mtime — the build then reports success about a file it never rebuilt, and the damage surfaces as unrelated TEST failures** | #3150 (Stray `PPSA02101`), 2026-08-31. A background build task was killed by the harness, leaving orphaned `ld.bfd` processes wedged writing to a pipe whose reader was gone. Clearing them by PID left six test executables **88 MB and non-executable** — `ld` had not yet set the exec bit. Their mtimes were newer than their inputs, so the next `cmake --build` printed `[100%] Built target ...` and skipped every one of them. The failure then appeared two steps away from its cause: `ctest` reported **6 failed / 315** with `BAD_COMMAND`, all on *render* tests, which reads as a graphics regression from the change under review rather than as a build artifact. `rm` the six and relink → 315/315, no code change. **After killing a build, verify the artifacts rather than the build's own success line**: `find <build> -type f -perm -u+x -newer <a source>` or simply `ls -l` the targets — a build system cannot detect a file it decided not to rebuild. Related trap: the same kill also produced a `(Failed)` grep that returned 0 on a run with 6 failures, because ctest writes `***Failed` and `The following tests FAILED:`, never parenthesised. |
| 239 | **A MUTATION TEST that never ran, reported as a dead guard — a same-size edit leaves `__pycache__` valid** | #3153 review, 2026-08-31. Mutation testing a Python module by rewriting it and re-running its test file: `return None` -> `return True` is the **same number of bytes**, and CPython validates cached bytecode on `(mtime, size)`. Rewritten inside the same second, both match, so the interpreter re-imports the UNMUTATED bytecode and the mutant never executes. The arm then passes, and the reader concludes the test does not discriminate. **The failure direction is the dangerous one**: it reports a LIVE guard as dead, inviting someone to delete or weaken a test that works. A reviewer hit this while checking another lane's mutation claims and was one step from reporting a true claim as false; clearing `__pycache__` between arms gave the correct answer (1 failure, not 0). **`rm -rf __pycache__` between every mutation arm**, or copy the tree to a fresh path per arm. The same hazard applies to any cache keyed on size and coarse mtime, which is most of them. |
| 240 | **`gh pr view <N> --comments` FAILS on this repo and prints nothing — while `--json comments` on the SAME command works** | #3160 review, 2026-09-01. A reviewer established a negative ("this figure appears nowhere in the PR or its comments") by grepping the output of `gh pr view <N> --comments`. That command **exits 1 with 0 bytes on stdout** here, emitting 206 bytes of `GraphQL: Projects (classic) is being deprecated ...` to stderr instead — so the grep searched an empty string and the negative was guaranteed before it ran. Measured the same minute on the same PR: plain `--comments` rc=1 / 0 bytes; `gh pr view <N> --json comments --jq '.comments\|length'` rc=0 / **2**; `gh api repos/:owner/:repo/issues/<N>/comments --jq length` **2**. **The `--json` variant of the same subcommand succeeds**, which is what makes this insidious — two near-identical invocations, one silently empty, and the failing one is the shorter and more natural to type. Same family as the known-broken `gh pr edit` (also Projects-classic GraphQL), so treat any non-`--json` `gh pr` read here as suspect. **Use `--json`, or `gh api`; and never assert a negative from a command whose exit code you did not check** — the reviewer who hit this had raised a finding of exactly that shape earlier in the same review. |
| 241 | **A REJECT CENSUS that names an INSTRUCTION reads as a missing lowering — the field that says otherwise was only printed on the sibling path** | #3137 (Stray `PPSA02101`), 2026-09-01. `[recompile-reject] mode=unresolved-operand pc=57 words=e00c2000,6a010000 … stage=vertex` names a `buffer_load_format_xyzw … idxen`, and an issue was opened proposing the two things that line can support: a missing MUBUF lowering, or a stage arriving with no resource table. Neither was true — the opcodes are lowered and `[buf-op] … rt=1 reject-unresolved` shows five resources present — and the actual cause is the #305 user-data window, three files upstream. The trap is not that the line is wrong; it is that the FORMAT path printed strictly fewer fields than the RAW path (`[mubuf-raw-unresolved]`) beside it, omitting `rewritten=` and `pc_res=`, which are exactly the two facts that separate "the table has no entry" from "provenance was correctly suppressed because the shader assembles its own descriptor". Settling it needed the game re-run under `PROSPER_DYNTRACE_FAIL=1` plus `PROSPER_UDPROV=1`. **When two sibling diagnostics report the same class of failure, diff their format strings before trusting the poorer one** — an instrument's silence about a discriminator is not evidence that the discriminator does not apply. Both fields are printed now. |
| 242 | **A PR with MERGE CONFLICTS gets no CI runs at all — which looks exactly like Actions being broken** | 2026-09-01. A branch was pushed and **zero** workflow runs were created for the head SHA, while other branches' runs completed normally minutes earlier. Diagnosis went to Actions: permissions (`enabled=true allowed=all`), a `concurrency:` group (none), a `paths` filter (none), the workflow's triggers (`pull_request` with default types — correct), closing and reopening the PR, and finally force-pushing a fresh SHA. **All of it wasted.** The cause was one field: `gh pr view <N> --json mergeable` said **`CONFLICTING`**. Master had moved underneath while other PRs merged. A single rebase produced a run within a minute. Two independent lanes hit this the same afternoon; the second reported it as an "Actions backlog", which is the wrong conclusion from the same evidence. **Check `mergeable` FIRST when CI has not started** — it costs one command, and with several lanes merging, branches go conflicting constantly. The dangerous shape is not the delay: it is that "no checks reported" plus an approving review is indistinguishable from green to anything that counts failures rather than requiring a nonzero PASS count. |
| 243 | **Two builds against one build directory silently corrupt it — and the failure surfaces as phantom TEST failures, not a build error** | 2026-09-01, hit by three independent lanes in one afternoon. Symptoms varied and none named the cause: five Vulkan-execution tests (`indexed_render`, `multidraw_render`, `descriptor_array_render`, `gpu_execute`, `recompiled_shaders_render`) failing on a PR branch **and on `origin/master`**; a truncated `libprosper_core.a`; and a `build_rc=1` on a tree nothing was wrong with. In every case a **fresh `cmake -S prosper -B <new dir>` passed everything.** The master-too symptom is the trap's sharp edge: "it fails on master as well" normally EXONERATES the branch, so the reasoning feels rigorous while pointing at the wrong culprit entirely — one lane was a step from reporting a master regression that did not exist. Two ways in, both easy: running two builds against one build dir, and checking out a different branch in a worktree while a background build is still running there (the build is invisible at the moment you switch — it may not have printed anything yet). **A background build OWNS its worktree until it reports**, and verifying N PRs concurrently needs N worktrees. Before believing any surprising test failure, re-run it from a fresh configure. |
| 244 | **An instrument whose WITNESS is bound in one stage only, armed in another -- and its failure mode is indistinguishable from the answer you wanted** | Astro Bot, 2026-09-02 (#3193). `PROSPER_CFG_TRIP_BOUND` caps the CFG dispatcher's back edge and publishes a device-side hit record into the internal GDS buffer at descriptor set 0, binding 127. That buffer is bound, prepared and read back **only by the compute executor** -- `kComputeInternalGdsBinding` appears nowhere else in `src/` or `frontends/` except the compute test fixture. The selector, however, takes a program ADDRESS and happily accepts a graphics one, and the hang under investigation was in a **fragment** program. Arming it there would have emitted a storage-buffer declaration no graphics pipeline layout contains. **The damage is not the missing record.** A draw whose pipeline cannot be created never runs; the device then survives the run, and survival is precisely the signal the instrument exists to produce -- so the arm would have read as *"the bound fixed it"* while the bound never executed, which is trap 199's announced-but-inert shape with the announcement telling the truth and the CONSEQUENCE lying. Fixed by emitting the witness only in compute and printing, once per program, that a graphics arm is capped but publishes nothing, so a surviving device has to be corroborated by showing the draw still ran (`PROSPER_DRAW_PROGRAM_CENSUS`). *Recognition cue:* **a diagnostic that writes anywhere -- a buffer, an image, a counter -- has a binding contract, and that contract is usually owned by ONE execution path. Before arming it somewhere new, grep for who binds its destination, not for whether the selector accepts your argument.** The general form is broader than this instrument: a selector's acceptance is a statement about parsing, never about plumbing. #3193. |
| 245 | **A one-shot failure injection aimed at a site behind a process-wide CACHE never fires -- and stays armed into the NEXT test's control run, where it reads as that test being broken** | #3210, 2026-09-02. `test_render_object_create_failure` arms one Vulkan create site at a time and renders. The `shader-module` arm printed nothing, and two assertions in the *following* block -- a texture control run that should render red and stay silent -- went red instead. Neither symptom points at the cause. `render_draw_pass_rgba` calls `vkCreateShaderModule` only when the persistent graphics-pipeline cache MISSES, and the arm's own control run (identical shaders, identical state, run first, by design) had just populated that cache -- so the injected run never reached the site, the one-shot arm was never consumed, and it fired inside the next block's control render. **The control run that makes an arm trustworthy is the same run that can make it unreachable**, which is why this is not caught by adding a control. Recognition cue: a one-shot injection that produces NO report is not evidence the site is unreachable -- check whether it is still armed (`consume_...` returning true) before concluding anything, and disable any cache that sits between the render and the site. Generalises past pipelines: the same shape exists for the persistent texture-image, texture-binding, pipeline-layout and descriptor-set-layout caches in `render_runner.h`, each of which can skip the create an arm is aimed at. |
| 246 | **A diagnostic selector keyed on a resource's BYTE SIZE cannot tell a buffer from an image, and 3840x2160x4 collides with a great many things** | #3214, 2026-09-02. Hunting the producer of Astro Bot's 33,177,600-byte world-map light-record pool, `PROSPER_COMPUTELOG_SIZE=33177600` matched three compute programs -- and all three bound a *texture* of that size, because 3840 x 2160 x 4 bytes is the same number. The filter was not broken and its output was not wrong; it simply could not discriminate, and a reader who stopped there would have named three innocent programs. **Prefer a selector keyed on the guest ADDRESS** -- `PROSPER_COMPUTE_BINDS=0xADDR` named the real producer (`0x5006e8500`) in one run, with its outcome attached. Recognition cue: a size filter that returns several unrelated programs on a full-screen-derived size is answering "which resources are 4K-shaped", not "which resources are this one". The same hazard applies to any per-title size a screen dimension can reconstruct -- 8,294,400, 2,073,600, 129,600 and 32,400 all appear on this one route as both pixel counts and unrelated buffer lengths. |
| 247 | **`git stash` inside a dedicated per-agent worktree -- the one piece of state that the "work in your OWN worktree" rule does not isolate** | #3174, 2026-09-01. Two lanes (`fix/issue-3051-spirv-flat`, `fix/issue-3068-sem-init-return`) each did exactly what this project prescribes -- their own `.claude/worktrees/<slug>` tree, their own branch off one master commit -- and collided anyway, because a worktree isolates `HEAD`, the index and the working tree but **not `refs/stash`**, which is a single ref in the shared `.git` directory. Lane A stashed one file so it could rebuild without it and confirm a regression arm failed; lane B stashed in its own tree a moment later; A's `git stash pop` took the LIFO **top**, which was B's, applied an unrelated `hle_kernel.cpp` `sem_init` change into A's tree and **dropped B's entry** doing it. The measurement A was about to take would have run against a tree carrying a second lane's edit, under a banner saying only its own change had been removed.
